## F2-07b · Eight SOC-Ops screens were built, wired and shipped unreachable

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-07b (route/nav CI test) |
| **Category** | Information architecture / shipped-but-invisible feature |
| **Severity** | **P2 Medium** — no data risk; eight paid capabilities a customer could not open |
| **Provenance** | Building the CI test F2-07b asks for ("every route has a nav path or explicit `detailOnly: true`") and reading the output rather than just counting it |
| **Current State** | `App.tsx` declares **263 static routes**. Eight had no link from anywhere in the application: `/socops/appsec`, `/dlp`, `/forensics`, `/iam-jml`, `/zt-maturity`, `/brand-monitoring`, `/cyber-insurance`, `/ma-due-diligence`. Each is a full page — its own API calls, KPI cards, tables and detail-route links — not a stub. **Their sibling `/socops/awareness` *was* in the sidebar**, which is what identifies this as an oversight rather than a decision: someone added nine routes and nine pages and wired one of them into the nav |
| **Problem** | A route with no link is a feature that was paid for twice — once to build, once to not use. Application security findings, DLP events, forensics chain-of-custody, JML access review, Zero-Trust maturity, brand/dark-web monitoring, cyber insurance and M&A due diligence are all NCA-ECC-relevant capabilities that no user could reach |
| **Root Cause** | Routes and nav are maintained in two files with nothing tying them together, so a route can be added without a nav entry and nothing notices |
| **Business Impact** | Eight capabilities invisible to every customer. Two of them (forensics chain-of-custody, JML "who-had-what") are the ones an ECC assessor asks for by name |
| **Technical Impact** | All eight added to the existing SOC-Ops nav group — **completing a group, not designing IA**, so it does not pre-empt F2-07c's workspace restructure. Labels are each page's **own bilingual title**, so the nav matches the screen and no Arabic was invented (house rule 7): 8 keys × EN + AR. `routeReachability.test.ts` then holds the invariant, with an `INTENTIONALLY_OFF_NAV` map where every entry carries its **reason** |
| **UX Impact** | Eight new SOC-Ops sidebar entries. Browser-verified: all eight render in the nav with correct Arabic labels, and the pages load with their own titles and KPI cards, clean console |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **The sidebar does not filter by permission** — these eight are guarded by `hasAnyRole('CISO','FORENSICS_LEAD',…)`, so a `GRC_ADMINISTRATOR` now sees eight entries that 403. That is pre-existing platform-wide behaviour (184 entries shown regardless of grant), not introduced here, and F2-07c owns it — but it is worth fixing there. 3. The nine legitimately-off-nav routes are documented in the test rather than deleted |
| **Alternatives** | Leave them out until F2-07c restructures the IA — rejected: F2-07c is an L and these are working features hidden today. Add a `detailOnly: true` flag to the route table as the plan suggests — rejected: it would mean touching 263 route declarations to express what a 12-entry allow-list says more clearly |
| **Trade-offs** | Eight more entries in a sidebar F2-07c already criticises as too flat. Accepted: an unreachable feature is worse than a long menu, and F2-07c will regroup them anyway |
| **Verification** | FE suite **67/67** (was 63; 4 new), `tsc` clean. **Mutation-proven:** removing the forensics nav entry fails **both** the orphan test (naming `/socops/forensics`) and the regression pin; reverted, `git diff` clean. **Browser-verified** all eight sidebar entries with their Arabic labels, and two pages rendered end-to-end with no console errors. **Live RBAC check:** all eight endpoints return **200 for `so_officer` (CISO)** and **403 for `compliance`** — so the pages are genuinely usable by the intended persona, and the 403 seen in the browser was correct authorisation rather than a defect |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-29** |
| **References** | `src/__tests__/routeReachability.test.ts`, `components/layout/Sidebar.tsx`, `translations/en.ts` + `ar.ts`, the eight `pages/socops/*Page.tsx`. Related: **F2-07c** (workspace IA + permission-filtered nav) |
| **Lesson** | **A scan that matches too little does not under-report — it invents defects.** My first version checked `to:`, `href:`, `to=` and `navigate(` and reported **45** unreachable routes. Twenty-two of those were the per-workflow screens, reached through a `route:` key in `lib/workflowCatalog.ts` that the scan did not know about; adding the missing key shapes took the figure to **17**, of which 9 are deliberately off-nav and **8 were the real finding**. Four backend regex scans under-counted this month and hid real problems; this one over-counted and would have sent me rewiring navigation that already worked. Both failures have the same fix — **validate the scan against something you already know before believing its output** (which is why this test asserts it finds >200 routes, >300 link targets, and specifically the `route:`-keyed `/workflows/pdpl/ropa`) |

## W2-1b · Demo data substitutes for a customer's own empty register — disclosed, and a decision you own

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | W2-1b (CI rule + backend-down acceptance test) |
| **Category** | Data provenance in the UI / test coverage |
| **Severity** | **P3 Low as implemented — but it hides a product question that is not mine to answer** |
| **Provenance** | Working W2-1b after W2-1a-i (Cmd+K palette off 8 mock datasets) and W2-1a-ii (dashboard off 3 fabrications). I expected a third instance of silent fabrication and **did not find one** |
| **Current State** | `useApiResource` substitutes a page's demo dataset in **two** situations: when the API call throws, and — this is the one worth naming — when it **succeeds and returns zero rows**, documented in the hook as "prefer the rich demo set so the UI never looks broken on a fresh tenant". **It is disclosed:** the hook raises `isDemo: true`, and measurement found **10 of 10 real consumers reference it**, rendering a warning `Demo mode` badge and disabling live refresh (`enabled: !isDemo`). The eleventh apparent consumer, `lib/promotedAdapters.ts`, names the hook only in a comment |
| **Problem** | Two things, and only the second is a defect I can act on. **(a) A product question:** a genuinely empty register — a fresh tenant, or one whose rows were deleted — shows *another organisation's demo incidents, vendors or evidence* rather than its own empty state. Badged, but a screenshot of it lands in an audit pack with no badge in the crop, and an operator cannot distinguish "no incidents" from "demo" by the table alone. W2-1b's own plan text asks for the opposite behaviour ("every register → error state, **zero rows**"), so the plan and the platform disagree. **(b) An engineering gap:** nothing prevented the *next* page from consuming the hook without disclosing `isDemo`, which is exactly how W2-1a-i/ii happened |
| **Root Cause** | "The UI must never look broken" is a demo instinct; for a compliance register the empty state *is* the truthful answer. The behaviour was implemented carefully and consistently — it is a deliberate choice, not an oversight |
| **Business Impact** | Bounded by the badge today. Unbounded if a page forgets the badge, which is what (b) now prevents |
| **Technical Impact** | Two static/runtime guards, no behaviour change. **`demoFallbackContract.test.ts`** — (i) a **ratchet** on mock imports outside tests at the measured baseline of **33 imports across 27 files** (a hard ban was impossible: those 33 exist, and F2-15 is the row that drives them down), and (ii) **every consumer of `useApiList`/`useApiResource` must reference `isDemo`**. **`useApiResource.test.ts`** — 5 runtime tests pinning the real contract via `renderHook`: error → `error` set + `isDemo` + demo rows; empty success → `isDemo` with **no** error; real rows → no substitution; unauthenticated → demo without an error and **without calling the fetcher** |
| **UX Impact** | None — no behaviour changed |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped (the guards). 2. ⚠ **Needs your decision:** should an empty-but-healthy register show the customer's empty state instead of demo rows? The plan says yes; the platform says no. I did **not** change it, because "how the product presents itself to a prospect" is a commercial call and the current behaviour is disclosed and deliberate — overriding it on the strength of a plan line would be the F2-40 mistake of treating a designed assertion as a defect. If you want the plan's version, the change is small and localised to `useApiResource`'s `list.length === 0` branch. 3. F2-15 remains the row that reduces the 33 |
| **Alternatives** | Assert the plan's "zero rows" and delete the substitution — rejected as above, it is not an engineering decision. Ban mock imports outright — impossible today; a ratchet is the honest form |
| **Trade-offs** | The ratchet's baseline is a number that must be lowered by F2-15, not a target. Pinning current behaviour in tests makes changing it slightly more work — which is the point: it should be a decision, not a drift |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Priority** | **Done** (guards) · **Open, needs your decision** (empty-register behaviour) |
| **Verification** | FE suite **63/63** (was 54; 9 new), `npx tsc --noEmit` clean. **Both guards mutation-proven:** stripping `isDemo` from `IncidentsPage` fails the disclosure test naming the file, and adding one new mock-importing file fails the ratchet naming the growth — both mutations reverted and `git diff` confirmed empty. The ratchet also has an anti-vacuity test asserting the scan finds >20 known sites, because a scan that silently matches nothing would be green for the wrong reason — the failure mode that let four backend regex scans under-count this month |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-29** for the guards; the empty-register question is tracked as an open decision |
| **References** | `src/__tests__/demoFallbackContract.test.ts`, `src/lib/useApiResource.test.ts`, `lib/useApiResource.ts`. Related: **W2-1a-i**, **W2-1a-ii**, **F2-15** (drives the 33 down), **F2-42** (the other "two versions of the truth" decision waiting on you) |
| **Lesson** | **I went looking for a third fabrication defect and the measurement said no.** 10 of 10 consumers disclose the substitution, so what looked like W2-1a's pattern was a disclosed feature — and the honest output was a *guard* plus a question for the owner, not a fix. Writing the fix I had expected would have removed working, deliberate behaviour on the authority of a plan line. Second lesson, on ratchets: the plan asked for a CI **ban** and the codebase has 33 violations, so a ban would either not land or land red. **A ratchet at the measured baseline is what a ban becomes when you count first** |

## F2-41 · The export audit trail recorded that data left, not how much

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-41 (carried from F2-27 recommendation #4) |
| **Category** | Audit completeness / DLP + PDPL evidence |
| **Severity** | **P2 Medium** — no exposure created; the control that is supposed to *detect* exposure could not quantify it |
| **Provenance** | Deliberately unbundled from F2-27, which enforced `MODULE:EXPORT` on all 40 file-returning endpoints. Authorisation and volume are separate concerns, and the volume half needs the *generator's* knowledge, not the interceptor's |
| **Current State** | `ExportAuditInterceptor` fires on every response carrying `Content-Disposition: attachment` and records who exported what, when, in which format and language. It captured **no row count and no byte size**, so the trail could not answer either question a DLP or PDPL enquiry asks: *how much was extracted*, and *how sensitive was it* |
| **Problem** | "User X exported the risk register" is a materially different event depending on whether it was 3 rows or 30,000, and the audit row could not distinguish them. An export-monitoring control that cannot express volume cannot raise a threshold alert, which is most of what such a control is for |
| **Root Cause** | The interceptor sees a byte stream. Only the generator that built the file knows it wrote 12 rows or 12,000, and there was no channel between them |
| **Business Impact** | PDPL and SAMA both expect an organisation to be able to say what left, at what scale. This is also the audit trail a customer's own DLP review would ask to see |
| **Technical Impact** | Three parts. **(1)** `ExportAuditContext` — a request-thread channel the generator writes and the interceptor reads. **(2)** The interceptor now records `bytes` (from `Content-Length`, so it costs nothing and covers **every** endpoint), plus `rowCount`, `sheetCount` and `rowCountSource`. **(3)** Instrumentation at the generators rather than the 40 controllers: `TabularExportService` (xlsx + docx), `RegisterExport` (xlsx + pdf), and the row-loads in `ExportService` and `DocxExportService` — 16 insertions total, no controller touched |
| **UX Impact** | None. The audit-log screen gains volume detail in the `EXPORT` row's JSON |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **The classification half is NOT done and should not be guessed.** `module/dataclass` already defines the NDMO 4-level scheme (PUBLIC/INTERNAL/CONFIDENTIAL/TOP_SECRET) but applies it to `DataAsset` rows, not to modules — so labelling an export requires a **module → level policy map, which is the customer's classification policy, not an engineering default**. The plumbing is in place; the map is a decision for you. Inventing a mapping would be exactly the F2-40 mistake of asserting a value the platform cannot derive. 3. A volume threshold alert (e.g. > 10,000 rows) is now expressible and would be the natural next step |
| **Alternatives** | Wrap the response to count bytes as they stream — rejected: `Content-Length` already carries it for the `byte[]` returns these endpoints use. Thread a count through 40 controller signatures — rejected as the plan itself anticipated; the generator seam does the same job in 16 lines |
| **Trade-offs** | A thread-local is the one risky part: a value left behind would be attributed to the *next* request on that pooled thread. The interceptor clears in a `finally` that runs on every path including the early returns, and both a unit test and a live probe leg pin it |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Priority** | **Done** (volume) · **Open, needs your decision** (classification map) |
| **Verification** | Live **11/11**, and the coverage is **measured rather than claimed** — the probe hits 20 real export endpoints enumerated from the controllers (the first version guessed paths and 7 of 10 404'd) and reads back what `platform.audit_log` stored. **Row-count coverage 4/21 → 16/21**; `bytes` **21/21**. The 5 uncounted are asserted to be board packs and ZIP bundles — documents, not row registers, where rows are not the unit. `rowCount=10, sheetCount=2` for a request carrying 7 + 3 rows. The leak guard is proven end-to-end: a counted export followed by an uncounted one leaves the second reporting `null`/`UNINSTRUMENTED`, not an inherited 10. `ExportAuditInterceptorTest` 8/8 (5 new), suite **2,590/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-28** for volume; classification tracked as an open decision |
| **References** | `ExportAuditContext` (new), `ExportAuditInterceptor`, `TabularExportService`, `RegisterExport`, `ExportService`, `DocxExportService`, `ExportAuditInterceptorTest` |
| **Lesson** | **Absent is not zero, and this is the third finding in a week to turn on that distinction** (after F2-40's tally and F2-46's score). An uninstrumented export reports `rowCount: null` with `rowCountSource: "UNINSTRUMENTED"` rather than `0`, because to a DLP enquiry "exported nothing" and "we did not measure" are opposite answers and a plausible zero is worse than an honest gap. Second lesson, on method: I guessed ten endpoint paths for the coverage probe and seven of them 404'd, which would have reported a flattering 1-of-3 coverage. Enumerating the real handlers from the controllers turned the figure from an artefact of my guessing into a measurement — **a coverage number is only as good as the denominator, and a guessed denominator flatters** |

## F2-22b(vii) · A sanctions hit could arrive already cleared, with a SAR reference

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-22b(vii) — seventh tranche, **the last ranked tier (3 HIGH + 4 STATE)**, and it retires the ranking as a progress metric |
| **Category** | Forged decision provenance / regulatory-filing assertion |
| **Severity** | **P1 High** — on the AML sanctions-screening register, plus the PDPL Art. 25 DPIA review record |
| **Provenance** | The last two tiers of F2-22b's ranking, read service-by-service like (iv)–(vi) |
| **Current State** | **`KycService.recordHit` saved the request body wholesale.** So a caller could create an OFAC/PEP screening hit that already carried a decision — `decidedAt`, `decidedByName`, `dispositionNotes` — and `sarFiled` + `sarReference`, which assert that a **Suspicious Activity Report was filed with the regulator**. Separately, `/hits/{id}/disposition` read `decidedBy` **from the request body**: DF-3's defect on the one register where "who cleared this match" is the entire audit question. On the privacy side, `DpiaService.addReview` accepted `reviewerId`/`reviewerName`/`decidedAt` from the body — and since there is **no separate decide endpoint**, `addReview` *is* the review, so the actor performing it is the reviewer by definition. `addMitigation` accepted `verifiedAt` |
| **Problem** | A sanctions register whose entries can arrive pre-cleared inverts its own purpose: the register exists to show which matches were reviewed, by whom, when, and what was reported. `sarFiled` is the sharpest of them — a claim about a filing made to a regulator. The DPIA review is the record a PDPL regulator reads to see who signed off on high-risk processing |
| **Root Cause** | Same as tranches (ii)–(vi): a `save(body)` on a register that also has a decision path, and attribution treated as request data |
| **Business Impact** | SAMA/AML and PDPL Art. 25 — the two registers in this tranche are both externally examined |
| **Technical Impact** | `recordHit` scrubs the whole decision block and forces `sarFiled = false`; `disposition` records `CurrentActor.username()` and refuses when unauthenticated; `addReview` stamps reviewer id, name and `decidedAt`; `addMitigation` stamps `verifiedAt` only when the status is VERIFIED, and clears it otherwise. **`disposition` (the classification itself) stays caller-set on purpose** — the screening feed is what classifies a match, and `recordHit` *reacts* to `TRUE_MATCH` by flagging the subject sanctioned/PROHIBITED. Refusing it would break the mechanism: the F2-40 trap |
| **UX Impact** | None — `kycApi.disposition` declares `decidedBy` and **no page calls it**; no FE reaches the DPIA sub-record endpoints either |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **`NdiGap.riskScore` is stale by construction** and worth a separate look: nothing in the platform ever computes *or* updates it — `updateGap`'s whitelist omits it — so a gap's risk score is frozen at whatever the create supplied even as `severity`/`likelihood` change. Not a bypass, a dead number in a register. 3. `CptyService`'s asserted approvals (from tranche vi) still want an F2-40 provenance label |
| **Alternatives** | Refuse a `recordHit` that carries decision fields, rather than scrubbing — rejected: the screening feed is machine-generated and may echo a fuller schema; scrubbing what it cannot know is kinder than a 400, and unlike F2-44's case nothing is silently *lost* (an undecided hit is the truthful state) |
| **Trade-offs** | `disposition()` keeps its `decidedBy` parameter so the controller's shape is unchanged; it is documented as ignored rather than deleted, because removing it would churn a signature for no gain |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | Live **17/17**, subjects and DPIAs created by `admin`, decisions taken by `compliance`, every body carrying `"the_mlro_who_never_saw_this"`: the hit lands with `decided_at`, `decided_by_name`, `disposition_notes`, `sar_reference` all NULL and `sar_filed = false`, **while `TRUE_MATCH` on OFAC_SDN still flags the subject sanctioned + PROHIBITED**; `/disposition` records `compliance`; the DPIA review records `compliance` with the caller's `reviewerId: 9999` and 2001 timestamp both discarded; a PLANNED mitigation has no verification date and a VERIFIED one is stamped now. The by-design verdict is **asserted too** — a `BiaRecord` created with `status=APPROVED` still succeeds. Suite **2,586/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-28.** F2-22b's ranked work is complete: every CRIT/HIGH/STATE endpoint now carries a recorded verdict |
| **References** | `KycService#recordHit`, `#disposition`, `DpiaService#addReview`, `#addMitigation`, `MassAssignmentRatchetTest#ADJUDICATED`. Deliberately unchanged: `BcmController#createBia`/`#updateBia`, `ChangeManagementController#addTask`/`#updateTask` |
| **Lesson** | **The ratchet's tier count was never a progress metric, and I had been quoting it as one.** It measures what *Jackson will bind*, not what the *service will write* — so tranches (ii)–(iv), which used `@JsonProperty(READ_ONLY)`, moved CRIT 54 → 18, while (v)–(vii), which fixed theirs with a service-side whitelist, scrub or reject, moved it **not at all**. `EthicsCaseController` still reads CRIT with the bypass closed. Fixed by making the census durable instead of re-deriving it: `ADJUDICATED` records the verdict for all 18 CRIT endpoints (**4 CLOSED, 5 INERT, 9 BY DESIGN**), the emitted file carries it as a column with a header explaining why the count lies, and a **new unadjudicated CRIT row now fails the build** — so the next entity-bound write gets read rather than annotated blind |

## F2-22b(vi) · The DTO paths a DTO was supposed to fix — eight attribution fields the caller named

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-22b(vi) — sixth tranche of F2-22b, and the one that closes the recorded follow-up list |
| **Category** | Forged approval/verification provenance — **on the DTO paths entity annotations cannot reach** |
| **Severity** | **P1 High** — both forgeable *and* empty in practice, which is the part that makes it worse than it reads |
| **Provenance** | The follow-up list recorded by tranches (ii) and (iii): five services that take a caller-supplied approver or verifier through a DTO and are therefore **invisible to the mass-assignment ranking**. This is the standing correction to F2-22b's own premise — *converting an endpoint to a DTO does not fix mass assignment if the DTO carries the same field* |
| **Current State** | Eight fields across four services, all on `/transition` endpoints: `CapaTaskService` `verifiedBy` (CLOSED); `VulnerabilityService` `verifiedBy` (CLOSED), `riskAcceptedBy` (RISK_ACCEPTED) **and `triagedBy`** — the last two found by this sweep, not on the recorded list; `BoardPackService` `approvedBy` **and `reviewedBy`** (APPROVED); `TreatmentPlanService` `approvedBy` (APPROVED) |
| **Problem** | Two-sided, and the second side is the one that shows up in an audit. **(a) Forgeable:** any caller could name anyone as the verifier of a remediation, the approver of a board pack, or — worst — the *risk acceptor* of an open vulnerability, which is an authority claim a NCA ECC reviewer reads to see who decided to live with the exposure. **(b) Empty:** no UI sends any of these fields (`cyberLifecycle.ts` mirrors `{ status }` only; `riskLifecycle.transitionPlan` passes `by` as `undefined` from every caller), so real approvals recorded **nobody**. Measured before the fix: **2 of 9** APPROVED/PUBLISHED board packs named an approver, and the single APPROVED treatment plan named none. `TreatmentPlanService` was worse than forgeable — it assigned **unconditionally**, so the omitted field wrote `NULL` *over* the approver |
| **Root Cause** | Attribution treated as request data on the very endpoints where the platform's own rule (DF-3, DF-4, F2-40, tranches ii–v) says it must be server-owned. The entity-level `READ_ONLY` sweep could not see these because the request type is a dedicated `TransitionRequest` DTO |
| **Business Impact** | CAPA verification is the evidence a remediation happened; a board pack's approver is the governance record the board reads; a risk acceptance is who signed for the residual exposure. All three were unattributed in practice and forgeable in principle |
| **Technical Impact** | All eight now come from `CurrentActor.username()` — the existing shared helper, so no new lookup was invented. Each service **refuses** rather than writing an anonymous decision. The fields were also **removed from the request DTOs**, so the contract stops advertising what the service ignores; the read DTOs keep them, because all eight are displayed. The audit messages in `CapaTaskService` and `TreatmentPlanService` now read the verifier/approver off the **saved entity** — they used to echo the request, so the forensic record repeated the caller's claim and said nothing when the field was absent |
| **UX Impact** | None — no UI sent any of them. The visible change is positive: approvals now show a name where they showed nothing |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **`CptyService` is the fifth service on the list and was deliberately NOT changed.** It has `approvedByUserId` on credit ratings and large-exposure limits, and **there is no approve endpoint** — `assignRating`/`upsertLimit` are the only writes. So the field records an *offline* credit-committee approval as data, and forcing the actor would falsify it: the clerk entering a SAMA large-exposure limit would appear to have approved it. That is the F2-40 trap, and the correct treatment is a **provenance label** (`ASSERTED` vs `COMPUTED`, as F2-40 used) rather than an actor stamp. Recorded, not fixed. ⚠ Note the tell that made it decidable: `CptyService` stamps `assignedByUserId` from the actor on the line *immediately above* the one that takes `approvedByUserId` from the body |
| **Alternatives** | Keep the DTO fields and ignore them — rejected: an API that accepts a field it discards is F2-44's silent-drop defect one level up. Reject a request that carries them — rejected: the caller's *intent* ("record a verifier") is honoured, just with a trustworthy value, so replacing beats refusing here |
| **Trade-offs** | Two unit tests asserted the old contract (`verifiedBy == "auditor"`, `approvedBy == "CRO"` — the values the request had supplied). Both are rewritten to assert the principal, and both now install a `GrcUserDetails` in the `SecurityContextHolder`. That is not test scaffolding for its own sake: production always has a principal because these transitions sit behind `@PreAuthorize`, and **the tests only passed without one because the value came from the body** |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | Live **15/15** with the records created by `admin` and transitioned by a *different* persona (`compliance`, or `so_officer` for the cyber legs — the only other `CYBER:WRITE` holder), every request carrying `"the_ceo_who_never_saw_this"`: all eight fields record the caller, none records the forged name, and the legitimate caller data alongside them (`riskAcceptanceExpiry`, `reviewCommentsEn`) still binds. The CAPA audit row reads `verified by compliance`. The treatment-plan leg sends **no** `approvedBy` at all — the SPA's exact shape — and the approver is recorded anyway. Suite **2,586/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-28** |
| **References** | `CapaTaskService`, `VulnerabilityService`, `BoardPackService`, `TreatmentPlanService`, their four `TransitionRequest` DTOs, `CurrentActor`, `CapaTaskServiceTest`, `TreatmentPlanServiceTest`. Deliberately excluded: `CptyService`. Same class as **DF-3**, **DF-4**, **F2-40**, **F2-22b(ii)/(iv)/(v)** |
| **Lesson** | **"Forgeable" was the less useful half of this finding.** The fields were forgeable *and* almost always empty, and it was the emptiness — 2 of 9 board packs, 0 of 1 treatment plans — that proved no legitimate caller depended on them, which is what made replacing the value safe rather than a guess. Checking the data first turned a judgement call into a measurement. ➕ **And the sweep found three fields the recorded list had missed** (`triagedBy`, `riskAcceptedBy`, `reviewedBy`) — the same companion-field miss as `decidedByName` in tranche (iii) and `approverName` in (iv), now three tranches running: *a hand-written list of field names is always short* |

## F2-48 · Nine registers could accept one new record per tenant per 17½ minutes

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-48 |
| **Category** | Correctness / identifier generation — with a transaction-propagation amplifier |
| **Severity** | **P1 High → widened 2026-07-28 to P0-adjacent in blast radius.** First found on CAPA; a sweep during F2-22b(vi) found the **identical generator copy-pasted at nine sites**, seven of them with the unique index in place and therefore capped the same way: **approval requests** (the approvals kernel), **audit engagements**, **audit findings**, **BCM crisis invocations** (raised *during* a crisis), **cyber vulnerabilities**, **PDPL DSR cases** (which start a statutory clock), **regulatory changes**, knowledge articles, and CAPA. Every one of them: one new record per organisation per ~17.5 minutes, then HTTP 500 |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-28 while verifying F2-22b(iv). Scoring a processor assessment FAIL returned **503** where scoring it PASS returned 200. Six consecutive attempts returned **200, 503, 503, 503, 503, 503** — the first-in-window success is what made the shape legible |
| **Current State** | `CapaTaskService#generateExternalId` was `Long.toHexString(System.currentTimeMillis()).toUpperCase().substring(0, 6)`. The epoch in hex is **11** digits, so taking the first six keeps the *high-order* digits and discards the low five: the value changes only when bit 20 rolls over — once per **2²⁰ ms ≈ 17.5 minutes**. Every CAPA created inside one window got an identical `external_id` and violated `uk_capa_external (organisation_id, external_id)` |
| **Problem** | Two distinct failures, and the second is why it stayed invisible. **(a)** Manual `POST /api/capa/tasks` returned 500 "an unexpected database error" for every payload after the first in a window — reproduced with `audit_finding`, `processor_assessment` and `incident`, while a raw INSERT of the same values succeeded, which is what pointed away from the schema and at the id. **(b)** Every auto-CAPA hook wraps `capaService.create` in `try/catch { log.warn }` while annotated plain `@Transactional` — i.e. `REQUIRED`, joining the caller's transaction. Spring had already marked that shared transaction rollback-only, so swallowing the exception did not save it: the **caller's** commit failed with `UnexpectedRollbackException` → 503. A hook documented as "best-effort … rather than rolling back the original transition" was silently fatal to the transition it decorated |
| **Root Cause** | `substring(0, 6)` on a hex timestamp truncates the wrong end. Hidden because all 16 seeded `capa.tasks` rows carry hand-written `CAPA-GOV-2026-NNN` ids, so the generator was never exercised twice in a window except by a real user, and the automation's `catch` turned each failure into a log line |
| **Business Impact** | CAPA is the remediation record every framework asks for. **Zero** rows had ever been created with a processor-assessment origin by the automation, so ESG/TPRM/privacy auto-CAPA appeared wired and produced nothing. On the PDPL Art. 28 path the amplifier was worse than the CAPA loss: a processor assessment **could not be recorded as failing at all**, which is the only outcome that matters for compliance |
| **Technical Impact** | Three changes now. (1) A single shared generator, `common/util/ExternalIds#generate(prefix)`, keeping the full millisecond plus 4 random hex digits; **all nine call sites** delegate to it, so a tenth copy cannot reintroduce the truncation. (2) All three `TprmAutomationService` hooks become `REQUIRES_NEW`, so a CAPA failure rolls back only the CAPA — which is what their javadoc always claimed. (3) The shared class documents the arithmetic, because `substring(0, 6)` on an 11-digit hex timestamp reads plausible and is the entire bug |
| **UX Impact** | "Create CAPA" stops failing with an opaque 500, and a failing processor assessment can be filed |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **Sweep every other `@Transactional` best-effort hook** — the pattern "catch, log, continue" is only true under `REQUIRES_NEW`, and this class is unlikely to be the only place it appears. 3. Consider a DB sequence or the platform's own code-generator instead of timestamp-derived ids |
| **Alternatives** | Retry-on-collision — rejected: it treats a generator that is wrong by construction as a contention problem. Widening the window (more hex digits, still truncated) — rejected for the same reason |
| **Effort** | **XS** (two edits) |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | Live, before: 200 then five 503s across six scores; `POST /api/capa/tasks` 500 for three different origin types. After: **6/6 200** with `overallOutcome=FAIL`, `riskScore=8.0`; four consecutive CAPA creates return distinct ids (`…7FFD885`, `…80A1C91`, `…8137524`, `…81CA605`); **10 CAPA rows actually created** where the automation had never created one |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-28** |
| **References** | `common/util/ExternalIds` (new), and the nine former copies: `CapaTaskService`, `ApprovalRequestService`, `AuditEngagementService`, `FindingService`, `InvocationService`, `VulnerabilityService`, `KbArticleService`, `DsrCaseService`, `RegulatoryChangeService`. Plus `TprmAutomationService` (all three hooks) and `ProcessorAssessmentService#scoreAndComplete` |
| **Lesson** | **A swallowed exception is not swallowed if the transaction is shared** — the `catch` block reads as isolation and provides none. The diagnostic order that worked: the endpoint 500s while a raw INSERT of the same values succeeds ⇒ the fault is in the service, not the schema; the *first* call succeeds and the rest fail ⇒ uniqueness, not validation. ➕ **And the real lesson arrived two days later: I fixed one site and called it closed.** The sweep that found the other eight only happened because an unrelated probe hit the same 500 on `cyber_lc.vulnerabilities` — *after* this finding was marked CLOSED. **When a defect is a copy-paste, the fix is not done until the copies are counted**; one `grep` for the offending expression would have found all nine on day one |

---

## SCR-004 · An attachment was reachable by anyone with `EVIDENCE:READ`, whatever record it hung off

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | SCR-004 (external secure code review; slice 3 of `SECURE_CODE_REVIEW_REMEDIATION_PLAN.md`) |
| **Category** | Broken object-level authorization — polymorphic attachment surface |
| **Severity** | **P1 High, and the report under-rated it.** It described intra-tenant disclosure. The same unguarded surface also accepted **writes**: a persona 403'd on every route to a record uploaded a file into it (**201**) |
| **Provenance** | Reported as "generic attachment access is tenant-scoped but not object- or classification-authorized"; my validation first marked it CODE-CONFIRMED, then **reproduced it live**, then reproduced it again against a deliberately bypassed build as a mutation proof |
| **Current State** | `AttachmentController`'s seven endpoints each carried one coarse guard (`EVIDENCE:READ` / `WRITE` / `DELETE`) and `AttachmentService.mustGet` filtered the row by `organisation_id`. Nothing checked whether the caller could read the `entityType/entityId` the file belongs to, and `entityType` is a free-form client-supplied string. 56 distinct entity types reach that surface from the SPA, spanning 20-odd modules |
| **Problem** | Measured with `ranalyst`, which holds `EVIDENCE_READ`/`WRITE` and no BCM authority of any kind (`ROLE_RISK_ANALYST` appears on none of the BCM controllers' role lists). Every BCM record route answered **403** — `/api/v1/bcm/plans`, `/bia-assessments`, `/crisis-events`, `/scenario-library`. The same session then read a BCM attachment's **bytes (200)**, its **list (200)**, its **version history including filenames and SHA-256s (200)**, and **uploaded a file into that invisible record (201)**. An `entityType` that does not exist answered **200 with an empty list** — an oracle for enumerating types and ids |
| **Root Cause** | A shared polymorphic surface inherited none of its parents' authorization. Object *identity* was checked (tenant + row) but object *ownership* was not, and the type discriminator was trusted because it arrived in the path |
| **Business Impact** | Governance evidence — contracts, minutes, BCP plans, whistleblower material — readable by any employee with the evidence permission, across modules they have no access to. Cross-tenant isolation held throughout; this is horizontal disclosure *inside* a customer, which is precisely what a GRC buyer's document-security review tests. The write path is worse than the read path: planted "evidence" appears inside an auditor's evidence package |
| **Technical Impact** | `AttachmentAccessPolicy` — 57 entity types, each mapped to **its parent record's own read guard**, read off the parent controller rather than inferred from the entity name. The registry speaks all three styles the platform actually uses: `hasPermission(MODULE,…)`, `hasAnyRole(…)` (the Saudi/financial registers — cpty, fraud, openbanking, opres, rrp, regreporting, outsourcing, ccm, aml, ethics, dataclass, ai-lifecycle — plus several BCM and privacy sub-controllers), and `isAuthenticated()`. Keys are normalised across naming conventions, because the codebase spells the same parent `PolicyDocument` and `POLICY_DOCUMENT`. Unknown type ⇒ 403 |
| **UX Impact** | None for legitimate navigation: the panel only renders on a record the user reached, and a cross-route audit found exactly one screen where the two could diverge (below). Refusals are a bare 403 with the reason in the server log, so a probe learns nothing |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **D-SCR-1 is a user decision** — classification (`PUBLIC`…`RESTRICTED`) stays advisory because **no clearance model exists anywhere in the schema**; enforcing it means inventing an access dimension and mapping 55 personas onto it. A gate that looks like enforcement but was guessed is worse than an honest label. 3. **D-SCR-2** — whether `EVIDENCE:WRITE` should permit attaching to a record the caller can only read |
| **Alternatives** | The report proposed resolving the parent row and invoking a per-type policy (8–15 days). Rejected for this slice: it needs a repository per parent type and would still have to decide *what* the policy is — which is the registry. Module-owned attachment endpoints were also rejected: 56 types would mean 56 copies of the same plumbing, and the copy-count lesson from F2-48 argues the other way |
| **Trade-offs** | Enforcement sits in the controller for the two path-keyed endpoints and in the service for the id-keyed ones, and **deliberately not** in `AttachmentService.list` — `ObligationController#evidencePackage` calls that method internally behind `hasAnyRole(…,'CISO',…)`, so a check there would have 403'd a working endpoint for CISO and BCM_MANAGER, who hold no `COMPLIANCE_READ`. Verified live (leg G, 200). The unknown-type refusal applies **to PLATFORM_ADMIN too**, so a registry gap fails for whoever tests it first rather than only for the customer |
| **Effort** | **M** |
| **Priority** | **Done** — ⚠ committed on `fix/scr004-attachment-object-authz`, **not merged** (the `/loop` requesting it omitted "then merge") |
| **Verification** | Live **29/29**, every uploaded fixture removed in a `finally` and tagged for a `CHARINDEX` sweep. **Mutation-proven both ways:** with `require()` bypassed, all five disclosure legs answer 200/201; with the fix, 403. No over-blocking, also measured: the same tokens still download, list and upload on the parents they can see, `PLATFORM_ADMIN` is unaffected, and both internal evidence-package callers still assemble. `AttachmentAccessPolicyTest` 13/13 + `AttachmentEntityTypeCoverageTest` 2/2, suite **2,611/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-29** (unmerged) |
| **References** | `AttachmentAccessPolicy`, `AttachmentController`, `AttachmentService#download/#history/#update/#delete/#replaceVersion`, `AttachmentAccessPolicyTest`, `AttachmentEntityTypeCoverageTest`. **Settles F2-27's allow-list**, which exempted `AttachmentController#download` as "a single object" — object *count* is not a proxy for sensitivity |
| **Lesson** | **Two, and both come from the same mistake: choosing a demonstration pair is itself a measurement.** (1) My first reproduction used `so_officer` + `PolicyDocument`, because that persona is 403 on `/api/policy/documents/{id}`. But the cyber workspace is wired to the shared policy service, so the *same rows* arrive under `CYBER:READ`, which that persona holds — verified, 200. It was never a disclosure for them, and had I not checked, the registry would have 403'd a CISO on their own screen. **"Who can see this record" is a union over every route that serves it**, and a single-route mapping under-counts it. (2) The scanner I used to read those guards was itself wrong: `@PreAuthorize\(([^)]*?)\)` cannot match any guard containing its own parentheses, so it reported "NO @PreAuthorize" for `RiskController`, which has thirteen. I was one step from designing the whole slice around the false claim that most parents are unguarded. **A scan that matches too little invents defects** — the same failure as the F2-07b nav scan, two slices earlier, which is why the balanced-paren rewrite is kept in the scratchpad with its predecessor's epitaph in the docstring |

---

## SCR-002 · A signed `org` claim was taken at face value, so a viewer could read another tenant

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | SCR-002 (external secure code review; slice 2 of `SECURE_CODE_REVIEW_REMEDIATION_PLAN.md`) |
| **Category** | Authentication — session liveness + tenant binding |
| **Severity** | **P1 High.** The tenant half is now **reproduced live**, not merely upheld: a non-admin read another organisation's risk register |
| **Provenance** | The report claimed bearer auth "ignores disabled/locked state and does not bind the JWT tenant to the live user". My validation upheld it while **correcting the mechanism**, and that correction decided the fix |
| **Current State** | Two defects, one file. **(a) Liveness:** authorities were always re-loaded from the database each request, so a revoked *role* already took effect immediately — that half of the report was wrong, and a fix premised on "stale roles" would have solved nothing. `CustomUserDetailsService` also computed the lock state correctly, honouring `lockedUntil`. **The defect was that nothing consumed it:** the filter built a `UsernamePasswordAuthenticationToken` by hand, skipping the `enabled`/`accountNonLocked` checks a `DaoAuthenticationProvider` applies at login. **(b) Tenant:** `TenantContext.setCurrentTenant(orgId)` took the token's `org` claim at face value — the single most load-bearing value in a request, since every repository query filters on it |
| **Problem** | A user deactivated or locked out mid-session kept working until their access token expired — up to an hour — and lockout is the control that answers a brute-force. Worse, the tenant came from the client: a **correctly signed** token naming another org bound that org, and the server could not tell it from one it had issued |
| **Root Cause** | Hand-rolling the authentication object skips the provider's account-status checks. For the tenant, a signed claim was treated as authoritative about *who the user is* rather than merely about what the token says |
| **Business Impact** | Cross-tenant read on a multi-tenant GRC platform — the risk register of another customer. Plus a revocation control (deactivate / lock) that did not take effect for up to an hour |
| **Technical Impact** | `enabled`/`accountNonLocked` now gate the bearer path, and the tenant comes from the loaded user. One `reject()` helper clears the `SecurityContext` **and** `TenantContext` and returns 401 without touching the chain — both clears matter, because the early `return` skips the `finally` that normally clears the tenant and servlet threads are pooled |
| **UX Impact** | None for healthy sessions. A deactivated or locked user now gets 401 immediately instead of continuing until token expiry |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. Slice 5 (an explicit `typ` claim + split validation entry points) remains the durable SCR-001 fix and would also let the tenant rule be expressed on the token rather than inferred. 3. The 401 is bare, matching slice 1 — deliberately uninformative, so a probe with a stolen token cannot tell disabled from locked from wrong-tenant |
| **Alternatives** | Wire a real `DaoAuthenticationProvider` (a `PreAuthenticatedAuthenticationProvider` with a checker) — the "correct" Spring shape, rejected here as a much larger change to the security config for the same effect; worth revisiting at slice 5. Blanket "token org must equal user org" — **rejected because it breaks the org-switcher**, see the trade-off |
| **Trade-offs** | **The org-switcher is a real, authorised exception and finding it was the substance of this slice.** `POST /api/v1/platform/organisations/switch/{id}` is `hasRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN')`, re-issues a token with a different `org`, and deliberately never updates the user row — that is how a platform admin operates a customer tenant. So the naive rule would have 401'd the first request after every switch. Shipped rule: *tenant from the loaded user, except a PLATFORM_ADMIN, for whom the claim is an authorised override, logged at INFO because a privileged cross-tenant session belongs in the record even when legitimate.* Any other mismatch 401s |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Priority** | **Done** — ⚠ committed on `fix/scr002-filter-liveness`, **not merged** (the `/loop` requesting it omitted "then merge") |
| **Verification** | Live **8/8** with every DB mutation captured and restored in a `finally`. Deactivate the account → the **same** token flips 200 → 401, and reactivating restores access with no new login. Lock it → 401; let the window elapse → 200, so SEC-AUTH-001's auto-unlock still holds. **The headline:** a `viewer` token re-signed with `org=2` (HS384, dev secret) returns **401** — and **mutation-proven**, restoring `tenantId = orgId` makes that same forged token return **200** against another tenant's risk register. The org-switcher still works end-to-end and its token really carries `org=2`. `JwtFilterLivenessTest` 6/6, suite **2,596/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-29** (unmerged) |
| **References** | `JwtAuthenticationFilter#resolveTenant`, `#reject`, `CustomUserDetailsService#buildUserDetails`, `OrganisationController#switchOrg`, `JwtFilterLivenessTest`. Complementary to **F2-22b(i)** (which guards cross-tenant *writes* at the Hibernate layer — neither substitutes for the other) |
| **Lesson** | **A probe's fabricated timestamp is a fixture, and fixtures have clocks.** My first elapsed-lock leg failed, and I nearly recorded it as a regression in the auto-unlock. It was my own fixture: **the JVM stores and reads `LocalDateTime` in UTC while the DB's `GETDATE()` is Riyadh local (+3h)**, so a Python-local `locked_until` landed three hours in the JVM's *future* and the lock read as still open. What settled it was checking whether **login** — which shares `buildUserDetails` — failed the same way. It did, which proved the behaviour was not mine. *When a probe leg fails, first ask whether an untouched path fails identically; if it does, the fault is in the fixture or the shared code, not in the change.* Anchor fabricated times on `GETUTCDATE()` |

---

## SCR-001 · A refresh token authenticated any API request, and survived logout

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | SCR-001 (external secure code review; interim fix = slice 1 of `SECURE_CODE_REVIEW_REMEDIATION_PLAN.md`) |
| **Category** | Authentication / session management — token purpose confusion |
| **Severity** | **P0 Critical** — account takeover with a seven-day window that logout could not end |
| **Provenance** | Reported by the external review that appeared in the tree on 2026-07-26; **independently reproduced** by me on the running application before any code was written, then reproduced again as a regression check after the fix |
| **Current State** | `JwtTokenProvider.buildToken` and `buildTokenFromIds` mint access and refresh tokens through the same builder, differing only in expiry. Neither carried a purpose claim. `validateToken` returns true for any correctly signed, unexpired JWT, and `JwtAuthenticationFilter` authenticated anything it approved. Refresh-token revocation is checked only by `/api/auth/refresh`. Measured claim sets: access = `enabled_modules, exp, iat, lod_band, org, roles, sub, usr`; refresh = the same **plus `jti`** |
| **Problem** | A refresh token taken from the cookie, a host, a backup, telemetry or another defect could be sent as `Authorization: Bearer` and receive the user's full authority set — reproduced: **200**. After `POST /api/auth/logout` returned 204, the same revoked token still returned **200**. The documented 15-minute access-token lifetime and every revocation control were bypassed; a GRC audit trail would attribute the attacker's actions to the victim |
| **Root Cause** | Signature validity treated as authorisation to use a token in *any* authentication context. Token purpose was never expressed, so there was nothing for the bearer path to check |
| **Business Impact** | Approvals, risk acceptances, regulatory submissions, evidence and personal data are all reachable under a victim identity, and logout gives users and administrators a false assurance that access ended. Production-blocking for government, banking and listed-company buyers |
| **Technical Impact** | **The review's prescribed rollout was more expensive than necessary, and that is this entry's most useful conclusion.** It called for an immutable `typ` claim plus invalidating all existing sessions. But the discriminator already existed — refresh tokens carry a server-issued `jti`, access tokens do not — so `JwtTokenProvider.isRefreshToken` plus a guard in the filter closes the reproduced attack with **no token-format change and no forced re-login**. Shipped alongside: `AuthController`'s store-absent rotation branch now mints a UUID `jti` (it previously minted a **jti-less** refresh token, which would have punched a hole straight through the fix), and `generateRefreshTokenFromClaims` was **deleted rather than deprecated** — leaving it would leave a one-line way to reintroduce the vulnerability |
| **UX Impact** | None. Every existing session keeps working; the SPA's access token is unaffected and its auto-refresh path still rotates |
| **Recommendation** | 1. Slice 1 as shipped. 2. **Slice 5 remains necessary** — the explicit `typ` claim with separate validation entry points is the durable fix; the `jti` heuristic is a correct-but-incidental property that a future mint path could break, which is why the test asserts it in both directions. Slice 5 forces re-login, so it belongs to a release. 3. Slice 2 (enforce `enabled`/`accountNonLocked`, tenant from the loaded user) is the same file and should follow immediately |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Opaque server-side access sessions — immediate revocation, at the cost of a lookup on every request. (b) An OAuth 2.1/OIDC authorization server — right long-term, far beyond this fix. (c) Reject refresh tokens by *expiry length* — rejected: brittle, and a config change would silently break it |
| **Trade-offs** | The interim fix depends on an invariant that is true but not self-evident ("carries a jti" ⇒ "is a refresh token"). That is a real fragility, accepted deliberately because it buys a same-day close of a reproduced account-takeover path, and mitigated by pinning it in tests until slice 5 replaces it |
| **Effort** | **XS** for the interim; the review's 2–4 days applies to slice 5 |
| **Priority** | **Interim done.** Slice 5 with the next release |
| **Verification** | Before: access-as-bearer 200 (control), refresh-as-bearer **200**, revoked-refresh-as-bearer **200**. After: control still 200, both attack legs **401**. Live **16/16** including the regression that mattered — `/api/auth/refresh` still returns a new access token, the cookie rotates, a **second** hop works (a chain that broke on the second rotation would pass a single-shot probe), a superseded token is refused, and logout refuses the token on both surfaces. `JwtTokenPurposeTest` 7/7 |
| **Status** | ✅ **INTERIM CLOSED 2026-07-28.** SCR-001 stays open in the plan until slice 5 lands the `typ` claim |
| **References** | `JwtTokenProvider#isRefreshToken`, `JwtAuthenticationFilter#isRefreshTokenPresentedAsBearer`, `AuthController` rotation fallback, `JwtTokenPurposeTest`, `SECURE_CODE_REVIEW_REMEDIATION_PLAN.md` §2 and slice 1 |
| **Lesson** | **A `Secure` cookie is never sent over `http://`, and that silently invalidated one of my own verification legs.** My pre-fix probe used `http.cookiejar` and reported "the refresh endpoint rejects the revoked token, so revocation works" — that 401 was an *absent cookie*, not a rejected token. The same artifact then made the post-fix probe look like a refresh-flow regression, which cost a round to diagnose. Two rules: **when a probe's negative result is the evidence for a claim, check that the request carried what you think it carried**; and prefer parsing `Set-Cookie` by hand over a cookie jar when probing `Secure` cookies on localhost. The three legs that carried the actual finding were unaffected — they used the `Authorization` header — so the finding stands, but one sentence of my validation did not |

---

## F2-22b(iii) · Decision, verification and signature provenance was settable — with no live bypass

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-22b(iii) — third tranche of F2-22b |
| **Category** | Mass assignment / provenance hardening — **defence in depth, not a closed hole** |
| **Severity** | **P3 Low as it stands.** Every service on these paths already defends; the exposure is that the guarantee lives in five services rather than in the type |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-28 continuing F2-22b's CRIT tier in order, after (ii) took the approval fields |
| **Current State** | `decidedBy`, `decidedByName`, `decidedAt`, `verifiedBy`, `verifiedAt` and `signedAt` were deserialisable on five entity-bound types: `AmlAlert`, `CrisisDecision`, `Disclosure`, `CorrectiveAction`, `DataSharingAgreement`. **But measurement found no live bypass** — every `setDecidedBy`/`setDecidedAt`/`setSignedAt` call in the platform takes its value from an authenticated actor or from `now()`, and `CorrectiveActionService` is explicit: create scrubs `verifiedBy`/`verifiedAt` (SEC-ESG-003 did this deliberately), `update()` whitelists around them, and only `transition()` sets them, from the verifier |
| **Problem** | Nothing is currently exploitable, and stating that plainly matters — the register would otherwise imply five new bypasses. The real problem is structural: five services each remember to defend, and the sixth endpoint added to any of these entities will not. That is precisely the argument F2-22a made for `BaseEntity`'s nine fields, and the reason DF-3 and DF-4 both happened in the first place |
| **Root Cause** | The rule "a field recording who did something, or when, is not the caller's to set" was applied per-incident (DF-3 for a committee vote, DF-4 for a CAB approval, SEC-ESG-003 for CAPA verification) and never generalised to the type system |
| **Business Impact** | Latent. The fields concerned are the audit answers to "who cleared this AML alert", "who took this crisis decision", "who verified this remediation", "when was this data-sharing agreement signed" |
| **Technical Impact** | `@JsonProperty(READ_ONLY)` on all six field names across the five entities, still serialised (asserted — every one is displayed or exported). **CRIT 28 → 19** in the risk ranking; cumulative 54 → 28 → 19 |
| **UX Impact** | None |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **The follow-up list is now five DTO paths, and it is the more interesting problem**: `CapaTaskService.transition` sets `verifiedBy` from `CapaTaskDto.TransitionRequest` on its CLOSED branch, `VulnerabilityService` does the same, and `BoardPackService`, `CptyService`, `TreatmentPlanService` each accept a caller-supplied approver. Entity annotations do not reach any of them |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Move these fields to a mapped superclass like `BaseEntity` — cleaner in principle, but they appear on a minority of entities with inconsistent types (`decidedBy` is a `Long` on `CrisisDecision`, a name elsewhere), so the superclass would be a poor fit. (b) Do nothing, since nothing is exploitable — rejected on the F2-22a argument |
| **Trade-offs** | Six field names are now un-postable platform-wide on these types. If a future flow genuinely needs to record a historical decision made outside the platform, it needs the F2-40 treatment — an explicit asserted-vs-computed provenance marker — not a re-opened field |
| **Effort** | **XS** |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | Sweep verified purely additive (`git diff` shows **0** deleted lines). `DecisionProvenanceBindingTest` 5/5: one hostile body naming all six fields binds none of them on any of the four entities checked, and all of it still serialises. Suite green |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-28** |
| **References** | `AmlAlert`, `CrisisDecision`, `Disclosure`, `CorrectiveAction`, `DataSharingAgreement`; `DecisionProvenanceBindingTest`; `MassAssignmentRatchetTest` risk profile. Same rule as **DF-3**, **DF-4**, **F2-22a**, **F2-22b(ii)** |
| **Lesson** | Two, both about the tools rather than the code. **(a) The first sweep missed `decidedByName`** because my target list held `decidedBy` and not its display-name companion — and the *name* is the field DF-3 was actually about, since a name in a decision log forges more convincingly than a user id. A field-name list is a guess about naming conventions; check the entity. **(b) A bare `ObjectMapper` is not the application's `ObjectMapper`.** Three defaults differ from Spring Boot's and all three produced false failures: no JSR-310 (the trap F2-22a already documented), *throwing* on unknown properties where Boot ignores them, and writing dates as numeric arrays rather than ISO strings. A test asserting Jackson behaviour must configure Jackson the way the application does, or it is testing a different mapper than the one that runs |
