## F2-13b · A read-only viewer could approve any workflow request — in the engine 14 modules approve through

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-13b *(first slice — the B1 `WORKFLOW:WRITE` row)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation / over-broad grant — the **B2 "granted but unrestrictive"** class, and the mirror image of F2-43 |
| **Severity** | **P1 High** — an unprivileged authenticated persona could issue an approval decision of record. Not a data leak; a governance forgery, and the approval is the artefact an auditor relies on |
| **Provenance** | Surfaced 2026-07-26 as the top row of section B1 in `Appendix/GRANT_TIGHTNESS.md` (F2-13a's report); **verified live, and the report's framing sharpened**, 2026-07-29. The report can only say "held by 55 of 57"; it cannot say whether passing the guard reaches a decision. It does |
| **Current State** | `POST /api/v1/workflows/requests/{id}/approve` and `/reject` were guarded by `hasPermission('WORKFLOW','WRITE')`, and `WORKFLOW:WRITE` was held by **55 of 57 roles** — including `VIEWER`, `REGULATOR_VIEWER`, `EXTERNAL_AUDITOR`, `SUPPLIER_USER` and all eight `*_INGEST` machine accounts. `WORKFLOW:APPROVE` **already existed**, was held by 3 roles, and already guarded the analogous act in three sibling controllers: `ApprovalRequestController#transition`, both `ApprovalChainController` decision endpoints, and `WorkflowSubmissionController`. Only the request lifecycle used WRITE |
| **Problem** | The workflow engine is adopted by **14 modules**, so this was not one endpoint — it was the most permissive approval path on the platform, and the one most module approvals route through. A second, record-level gate (`requireActor`, "only the designated actor may approve") did limit *which* request a given user could decide, but it is not an authority check: it is satisfied by being named on the request, and any of the 55 roles could be named. The permission gate was the only thing standing between "participates in workflow" and "decides workflow" |
| **Root Cause** | The permission split existed in the catalogue and in three of four controllers; the fourth was written against WRITE and nothing compared them. Compounded by **F2-27**, which silently changed what an existing grant *means*: `MODULE:EXPORT` gated nothing before it and 40 file endpoints after, and no grant was re-reviewed when the meaning changed. Same shape as **CM-C2** (change approval by name) and **SCR-003** (`CHANGE:WRITE` reaching approval states) — *the mechanism was correct and one call site never adopted it* |
| **Business Impact** | An approval record is what a SAMA/NCA examiner reads to establish that a control operated. A register in which a read-only viewer, an external auditor's account or an unattended ingest service can appear as the approver does not evidence a four-eyes control — it evidences the absence of one. `EXTERNAL_AUDITOR` is the sharpest case: the party whose independence the record is meant to demonstrate could author the approval |
| **Technical Impact** | Both guards moved to `hasPermission('WORKFLOW','APPROVE')`. **The guard change alone would have broken the platform**: it would have left approval to 3 roles, and the documented four-eyes second approver is a CISO, which held no `WORKFLOW:APPROVE`. **V324** therefore grants it — derived from the platform's own data rather than chosen: every role that already holds *some* module's APPROVE **and** already holds `WORKFLOW:WRITE`. 3 → **17** holders; decision authority **55 → 17**. Zero read-only, external or machine personas qualify — verified, not assumed, which is why the rule needs no hand-maintained exclusion list |
| **UX Impact** | None for the 17 approver personas or for `PLATFORM_ADMIN`. A `VIEWER` now gets 403 where it previously got a completed approval — the intended change. Participation is untouched: create, send, respond, submit, resubmit all still work for every WRITE holder, live-proven on the same token in the same run |
| **Recommendation** | 1. Both guards on `WORKFLOW:APPROVE` + **V324**, as shipped. 2. Keep `WORKFLOW:WRITE` at 55 — after the split it means *participate*, and a `SUPPLIER_USER` answering a vendor attestation needs it. 3. Answer the `/close`/`/reopen` question deliberately rather than by drift; `WorkflowApproveAuthorityTest` pins all ten lifecycle endpoints so a new one cannot be added without a guard decision. 4. Work the other two B1 rows (`CHANGE:WRITE` 42/57, `AI:WRITE` 41/57) the same way — **verify the endpoint reaches a decision first**, since B1 membership alone is not a defect |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Guard on `WORKFLOW:WRITE` **AND** a `hasAnyRole` leg — rejected: F2-27's plan pre-committed to exactly that and the grant table showed it would have 403'd 8 of 18 legitimate holders. (b) Revoke `WORKFLOW:WRITE` from the read-only and machine personas — rejected, and this is the report's own inconsistency: its section B3 lists the machines' grants as wrong while B4 defends the same grants as correct least privilege for a machine. Both cannot be true; B4 is right. (c) Grant `WORKFLOW:APPROVE` to a hand-picked list — rejected: a curated list is my judgement, and it decays. The derivation is the platform's own statement about who is an approver |
| **Trade-offs** | 17 of 57 is deliberately tighter than the four-eyes flows strictly need, and some tenants will want a `SECURITY_ANALYST` approving. That is a role-editor change, not a code change — the reversible direction, and the same trade-off F2-43 recorded. Nothing was revoked here, so no tenant loses a capability it had except the one this finding is about |
| **Effort** | **S** — two guard strings, one idempotent grant migration, one reflective test |
| **Verification** | Live **12/12** through the running stack, every leg one exact expected code rather than a range — the first draft accepted `in (200, 409)` and mistook three `requireActor` 400s for failures. Fixtures driven `DRAFT → SENT → SUBMITTED` (read from `TRANSITIONS`; `DRAFT → SUBMITTED` is not legal) so each code means what its leg claims. **The decisive pair is on the same request in the same SUBMITTED state:** `viewer` `/approve` **403** and `/reject` **403** with the record still `SUBMITTED` afterwards, then `so_officer` `/approve` **200** with the record `APPROVED` — authority is the only variable. Participation preserved on the same `viewer` token: `/send` 200, `/submit` 200 on a request it initiated, GET 200. `admin` 200 (code bypass intact). **The pre-fix 200 is derived, not remembered:** `viewer` holds `WORKFLOW` READ/WRITE/EXPORT and no APPROVE, and a still-WRITE-guarded sibling on the same controller returns 200 for that token, so the identical old guard admitted it. V324 applied to dev twice — **round-tripped from the measured baseline** (revert to 3, apply, apply → 3 → 17 → 17, exact set asserted), because the first apply ran against a DB that already held the rows and so could not distinguish "the predicate selects 17" from "17 rows were lying there". Verification 3 mutation-proven: re-insert the half-grant by hand and the migration refuses with `THROW 50324`. `WorkflowApproveAuthorityTest` 3/3, suite **2,593/0/0**. Fixtures removed with `CHARINDEX` |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-29** — guards + V324 + reflective test. Both other B1 rows since worked and they closed opposite ways: `CHANGE:WRITE` 42/57 was real (**F2-29**), `AI:WRITE` 41/57 is **not a defect**. F2-13b's row stays open for section C, now **14** — `AI:READ` left it via **F2-50**, whose answer to this row's own question was *the feature was missing its guard* |
| **References** | `V324__workflow_approve_authority.sql`, `module/workflow/requests/controller/WorkflowRequestController.java` (`/approve`, `/reject`), `WorkflowRequestService#approve`/`#reject`/`#requireActor`, `WorkflowApproveAuthorityTest`, `Appendix/GRANT_TIGHTNESS.md` §B1 + "What F2-13b has closed so far", `Appendix/scripts/grant_tightness.py`. Mirror of **F2-43**; same shape as **CM-C2**, **SCR-003**; grant-decision rule from **F2-13a** |
| **Lesson** | Three. **(a) The report's own regeneration caught the fix's first draft.** Using only the already-an-approver predicate handed `CHANGE_MANAGER` — which holds no other `WORKFLOW` grant — authority to decide records it cannot list or open, and it appeared as a *new section-B4 row* the moment the file was regenerated. B4's framing is that write-without-read is correct for a **machine**; for a human persona it is a 403 halfway through a flow, offered as a button. **A grant migration is exactly where the half-grant gets created**, so V324 now throws if any holder lacks the module's READ. Fixing a measurement's finding and then re-running the measurement is not ceremony. **(b) The narrative had to go in the generator, not the report.** `GRANT_TIGHTNESS.md`'s prose is `emit()`-ed by `grant_tightness.py`; a hand-edit would have been erased by the next run — the standing hazard of a generated artefact that reads like a document. **(c) An assertion that accepts a range of status codes cannot tell authorization from state.** `in (200, 409)` hid three record-level 400s and would equally have hidden a regression; expecting one code per leg forces the fixture into a state where the code means something |

---

## F2-29 · A change raiser could approve their own emergency change — in one hop, with no votes and no committee

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-29 *(authority half; the named-command split remains open)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation / missing privilege separation — **authorise-vs-author**, the same class as F2-13b one module over |
| **Severity** | **P1 High** — self-approval of a production change, by the requester, recorded as a CAB decision. The plan had this at **P2**; the measurement moved it back |
| **Provenance** | Carried from F-002; downgraded to P2 on the reasoning that "the exploit is blocked by two legs". Re-measured live 2026-07-29 while working the `CHANGE:WRITE` B1 row that F2-13b's own recommendation #4 pointed at. **The P2 downgrade was wrong** — see Root Cause |
| **Current State** | `POST /api/v1/changes/{id}/transition` carried one guard, `hasPermission('CHANGE','WRITE')`, for **every** target status — including the three that *are* CAB decisions. `CHANGE:APPROVE` already existed (permission id 97), was held by 9 roles, and already guarded the sibling act: `POST /{id}/approvals`, the CAB vote. Only the transition path used WRITE |
| **Problem** | `DRAFT → EMERGENCY_APPROVED` is a legal single hop, and its only check was `changeType == "EMERGENCY"`. So: raise an EMERGENCY change, approve it yourself, walk into `IMPLEMENTING` — with `CHANGE:WRITE` alone. `CAB_REJECTED` was the same, killing a change at CAB with nothing on record. Both stamp `cabDecisionAt`, so the row afterwards **claims a CAB decision that never happened**. **33 of 57 roles** hold `CHANGE:WRITE` without `CHANGE:APPROVE` — `DPO`, `HR_LEAD`, `DLP_REVIEWER`, `EVIDENCE_OWNER`, `BRAND_MONITORING` among them |
| **Root Cause** | The plan's "blocked by two legs" was **true of one of the three targets it named in the same sentence**. `CAB_APPROVED` genuinely has a second leg (CM-C2: recorded votes or a carried committee resolution, both cast through APPROVE-guarded endpoints) and it held when probed — 409, *"requires at least 2 recorded APPROVE vote(s)"*. `EMERGENCY_APPROVED` and `CAB_REJECTED` had **no second leg at all**. A risk assessment was written for the set and verified against one member. Underneath that: the state machine gates *sequence* (`NORMAL_TRANSITIONS`) and the switch gates *side-effects*, but nothing gated **authority per target** — so a new state's default privilege was silently WRITE |
| **Business Impact** | The emergency-change path is the one ITIL, NCA ECC and SAMA all single out, because it is the documented way to bypass CAB. A register where the requester is also the approver does not evidence a change-control gate; and unlike a data leak this one *manufactures* the assurance artefact — `cabDecisionAt` populated, notes authored by the raiser. ECAB ratification exists in the design (`referToCommittee` sets mode `RATIFICATION` for EMERGENCY) but nothing compels it — that gap is S7's `ECAB ratification SLA` |
| **Technical Impact** | `DECISION_TARGETS = {CAB_APPROVED, CAB_REJECTED, EMERGENCY_APPROVED}` + `requireDecisionAuthority(target)` in `ChangeManagementService`, called **before** the state machine so an unauthorised caller cannot use the error type to learn which transitions would have been legal. **No migration**: all 9 `CHANGE:APPROVE` roles already hold `WRITE` and `READ` — verified, including the mirror-image check for APPROVE-without-WRITE half-grants that the endpoint's baseline guard would have blocked first. V301's grant matrix was already right; only the endpoint disagreed with it. An empty `SecurityContext` is **refused, not exempted**: `transition()` has exactly one caller, grep-verified across the backend, so there is no off-request path to grandfather |
| **UX Impact** | Would have been a 403 toast for 33 of 57 roles, so the FE was fixed in the same slice: `canApprove(module)` added beside `canExport`, and the two `nextStatus`-driven controls in `ChangesPage` **disabled with a bilingual reason** rather than left to fail on click. `PLATFORM_ADMIN` is honoured, so `admin` sees every control enabled — hiding one the server would allow is the same defect reversed, which is `canExport`'s own stated rule |
| **Recommendation** | 1. Ship the authority gate, as done. 2. Do **not** widen `CHANGE:APPROVE` to solve the clerical case — a `BOARD_SECRETARY` who only *records* a decision the committee already took now needs it; the right answer is a recording endpoint. 3. The named-command split is worth doing for legibility, not for authorisation — sequence it when `ChangesPage` is rebuilt (CE-13), not before. 4. The third B1 row (`AI:WRITE` 41/57) is still unexamined; run it the same way — **verify the endpoint reaches a decision first** |
| **Alternatives** | (a) A target-aware `@PreAuthorize` SpEL indexing the body map — rejected: unreadable, unverifiable by a reviewer, and it would break the moment the body shape changes. (b) Split into named commands *now* and guard each with its own annotation — the plan's prescription, and the correct end state, but it is an API change with FE churn that delivers **no authorisation the service check doesn't already give**; it would have delayed the fix behind a refactor. (c) Gate only the two provably-open targets and leave `CAB_APPROVED` on WRITE — rejected: narrowing the plan's stated scope on my own judgement, when the actor who flips the status also authors `cabDecisionNotes` |
| **Trade-offs** | The guard lives in the service, so **an annotation scan will under-report this endpoint** — the authorization ratchet and my own `@PreAuthorize` scanner both see only `CHANGE:WRITE`. Accepted deliberately, following the `AttachmentAccessPolicy`/SCR-004 precedent, and paid for with a test that pins the whole rule plus a Javadoc on the controller method saying the annotation is the baseline and not the rule |
| **Effort** | **S** for the authority half — one set, one method, one test class, two FE call sites. **M** remains for the named-command split |
| **Verification** | Live **12/12 pre-fix** and **11/11 post-fix**, same script, same actors, only the expectations flipped. Actors chosen from `iam.role_permissions`, not from usernames: `ranalyst` (WRITE, no APPROVE), `so_officer` (APPROVE via CISO — it appears in *both* grant lists, which is why it is the control and not the exploit actor). ⚠ **`admin` was excluded on purpose**: it sits in the 33-role exploit population yet `PLATFORM_ADMIN` bypasses in code, so probing as admin returns 200 before *and* after and proves nothing. **The decisive pair is one actor, two routes to the same decision:** `ranalyst` `POST /{id}/approvals` **403** while `transition → EMERGENCY_APPROVED` **200** with `cabDecisionAt` stamped and `→ IMPLEMENTING` **200**; post-fix both **403**. Operational moves preserved on the same token: `SUBMITTED`, `IMPACT_ASSESSED`, `CAB_PENDING`, `CANCELLED` all 200 in both runs. Both mutations proven — deleting the call fails exactly the 3 authorisation tests; adding a status to `NORMAL_TRANSITIONS` fires the ratchet (proven with `PENDING_RATIFICATION`, one of the three **X-2** will add, so it will fire for real) — both reverted, `git diff` clean. **Browser-verified through the Vite proxy**, not just the API: as `ranalyst` `→ EMERGENCY APPROVED` 1/1 and `→ CAB APPROVED` 8/8 disabled carrying the Arabic tooltip, 16 operational buttons enabled; as `admin` all 31 enabled. `ChangeDecisionAuthorityTest` 7/7, `ChangeManagementServiceTest` 35/35, suite **2,628/0/0**, FE `tsc` clean + 76/76. 16 `[REVIEW-TEST]` changes removed with `CHARINDEX` |
| **Status** | ✅ **AUTHORITY CLOSED 2026-07-29** · ⬜ named-command split open. Two adjacent gaps found and *not* fixed here: the UI offers **no reject control at all** (only a `CAB_REJECTED` label and column), and `ChangesPage` has **no other permission gating** — delete included |
| **References** | `module/change_mgmt/service/ChangeManagementService.java` (`DECISION_TARGETS`, `requireDecisionAuthority`, `NORMAL_TRANSITIONS`), `ChangeManagementController#transition`, `ChangeDecisionAuthorityTest`, `tss-grc-ui-v2/src/lib/auth.ts` (`canApprove`), `pages/changes/ChangesPage.tsx` (`mayTransitionTo`). Precedent for the placement: `AttachmentAccessPolicy` (**SCR-004**). Same class: **F2-13b**, **CM-C2**, **SCR-003**. Downstream: **S7** (ECAB ratification SLA), **X-2** (status set 14 → 17), **CE-13** |
| **Lesson** | Three. **(a) A risk assessment written for a set must be verified against every member.** "Blocked by two legs" held for `CAB_APPROVED` and was checked no further; the two targets in the same sentence had no second leg, and the finding sat at P2 for it. The plan's own words named all three — the verification did not. **(b) The persona that everything gets tested with is the one that proves nothing.** `admin` holds `CHANGE:WRITE` without `CHANGE:APPROVE` and would have returned 200 before and after, because `PLATFORM_ADMIN` bypasses in code. Reading grants out of `iam.role_permissions` and picking an actor from the data — rather than reaching for the default login — is what made the probe decisive. **(c) Where a state machine has no authority dimension, the default for a new state is the weakest guard, silently.** `EMERGENCY_APPROVED` was not a decision anyone made; it was a status added to a map. The ratchet is the fix for that: it makes the next status a question someone has to answer, and it already fires on one of X-2's three |

---

## F2-50 · A read-only viewer could read the AI-drafted board summary — writes were guarded, reads asked nothing

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-50 *(found while measuring F2-13b's third B1 row, which turned out not to be a defect)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation / missing read guard on a derived artefact — the **SCR-004 class**, and the inverse of the two findings before it: here the *writes* were correct and the *reads* were open |
| **Severity** | **P2 Medium** — within-tenant, read-only, no mutation. Aggregate posture data and AI drafts, not raw records; but the audience it reached is the problem, not the volume |
| **Provenance** | F2-13b's recommendation #4 said to work the remaining B1 rows the same way and to **verify the endpoint reaches a decision first, since B1 membership alone is not a defect**. Applied to `AI:WRITE` (41/57) on 2026-07-29. **The row is not a defect** — see below — and this finding is what the measurement turned up instead |
| **Current State** | `AiSuggestionController` had 9 mapped endpoints: 7 writes on `hasPermission('AI','WRITE')` and **2 reads on `isAuthenticated()`**. Meanwhile `AI:READ` exists in the catalogue (permission id 103) and is granted to **41 of 57 roles**, so the platform already had a stated answer for who may read AI output. The two reads were the only endpoints in the module not asking it |
| **Problem** | Measured live as `viewer`, a persona holding **no AI permission of any kind**: `POST /api/v1/ai/board-summary` → **403** (writes guarded), then `GET /api/v1/ai/suggestions?feature=BOARD_SUMMARY` → **200 with 10 rows and full `outputJson`** — *"Executive GRC posture this period: STEADY… 7 open HIGH / VERY_HIGH risks… 3 corrective actions overdue…"* plus NCA and PDPL compliance percentages and the BCM readiness score. `GET /suggestions/entity/CrisisEvent/1` → **200**, 1 row. Both `inputJson` and `outputJson` are `NVARCHAR(MAX)` and come back whole |
| **Root Cause** | The controller predates the permission catalogue's AI entries — it is the Saudi-prompt §27 AI feature set, and its reads were written when `isAuthenticated()` was the norm. `AI:READ` was catalogued later (and `AI:EXPORT` later still, by **F2-27**) and **nothing swept the existing routes** when the permission arrived. Same mechanism as F2-13b's compounding cause: a permission's meaning was established without re-reviewing the endpoints that should have adopted it |
| **The decisive corroboration** | **`AI:READ` was one of section C's 15 "catalogued and granted, but guarding no endpoint" permissions** — the report's own words for those: *"the role editor offers a checkbox that is pure theatre."* It guarded nothing **because the two endpoints that should have used it asked `isAuthenticated()` instead.** F2-13b left section C open with exactly this question — *is the permission dead, or is the **feature** missing its guard?* — and this is its first answered instance: **the feature was missing its guard.** So 41 roles held a meaningless checkbox while a `viewer` holding no checkbox at all read the content. Regenerating the report proves the fix landed: section C **15 → 14**, distinct guarded `MODULE:ACTION` pairs **91 → 92**, guard sites **1432 → 1434**, and the `AI` module row `41/0 → 41/2`. The remaining 14 section-C rows should each be re-asked this way rather than revoked |
| **Business Impact** | The 16 roles without `AI:READ` are precisely the audience this content should not reach: `VIEWER`, `EXTERNAL_AUDITOR`, `REGULATOR_VIEWER`, `SUPPLIER_USER`, and the eight `*_INGEST` machine accounts (plus `AUDITOR`, `INTERNAL_AUDITOR`, `CHANGE_MANAGER`, `EMERGENCY_RESPONDER`). An executive posture summary naming open HIGH-risk counts and compliance shortfalls, readable by a supplier's user account or an unattended SIEM ingest credential, is a disclosure with no upside. `INCIDENT_ROOT_CAUSE` drafts carry crisis-event sitrep highlights |
| **Technical Impact** | Both reads moved to `hasPermission('AI','READ')`. **No migration** — the permission exists and all 41 intended readers already hold it. **Tenant scoping was never the problem**: both repository queries already filter `organisation_id`, verified in the service, so this was strictly a within-tenant gap. `AiSuggestionGuardTest` pins all 9 endpoints |
| **UX Impact** | The 16 roles lose access they had. `AiSuggestionsPage` already carries an `error` state wired into its render, so a 403 degrades to an error panel rather than a crash — checked, not assumed. ⚠ **The sidebar still shows them the link**, because `Sidebar.tsx` has **no permission gating anywhere** — that is the flat 184-entry navigation **F2-07c** exists to replace with registry-and-entitlement-driven nav, and building a gating mechanism for one item was out of scope here |
| **Recommendation** | 1. `AI:READ` on both reads, as shipped. 2. Decide whether `AUDITOR`/`INTERNAL_AUDITOR` should hold `AI:READ` — they currently do not, and if an auditor is expected to review AI-assisted drafts that is a **role-editor** change, not a code change (the reversible direction, same call as F2-13b). 3. Sweep the other modules for reads left on `isAuthenticated()` after their permission was catalogued — this was found by accident, and the class is systematic. 4. The parent-guard question below |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Inherit the **parent record's** read guard, the full SCR-004 rule — the stricter and ultimately correct answer, deliberately **not** done here: `relatedEntity` takes values like `"Dashboard"` that are not records at all and appear in no parent registry, so reusing `AttachmentAccessPolicy` naively would 403 everyone on the board summary. Recorded as a follow-up instead of invented. (b) Leave the reads open on the argument that suggestions are advisory drafts — rejected: the content is derived from real registers and the platform already defines who may see it. (c) Guard with `AI:WRITE` so only generators can read — rejected: it would deny the 0 roles that hold READ without WRITE today (the sets are identical at 41), so it buys nothing and misstates the intent |
| **Trade-offs** | The sets for `AI:READ` and `AI:WRITE` are identical (41 each), so today this changes nothing for anyone who could already generate a suggestion — it only excludes the 16 who could read without any AI entitlement. That also means the fix is *currently* indistinguishable from guarding on WRITE; READ is chosen because it states the intent and will diverge correctly if a read-only AI reviewer role is ever added |
| **Effort** | **XS** — two annotation strings, one reflective test, no migration |
| **Verification** | Live **6/6 pre-fix** and **6/6 post-fix**, same script, expectations flipped. Pre-fix `viewer` read 10 rows with an 873-character `outputJson`; post-fix **403** on both reads, while `ranalyst` (holds `AI:READ`) still gets **200** — so the guard discriminates on the permission and not on the route. The write leg (`viewer` → 403) is asserted in **both** runs, which is what establishes that the reads were the anomaly rather than the module being open. Mutation-proven: reverting `listByFeature` to `isAuthenticated()` fails 2 of 3 tests, one naming the regression explicitly; reverted, `git diff` clean. `AiSuggestionGuardTest` 3/3, suite **2,631/0/0**. The authorization ratchet is unaffected — it counts unguarded *write* endpoints, and these are reads |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-29** — both reads on `AI:READ` + reflective test. Two follow-ups recorded: the parent-guard rule, and the `isAuthenticated()`-after-cataloguing sweep |
| **References** | `module/ai/controller/AiSuggestionController.java` (`listByFeature`, `listByEntity`), `AiSuggestionService#listByFeature`/`#listByEntity` (both already `org()`-scoped), `AiSuggestionGuardTest`, `iam.permissions` id 103. Same class as **SCR-004**; permission-meaning-changed-without-a-sweep is **F2-27**'s mechanism; the sidebar gap belongs to **F2-07c** |
| **Lesson** | Two. **(a) The row I was sent to fix was not a defect, and saying so is the deliverable.** `AI:WRITE` at 41/57 guards six draft generators and a `/review` that — verified live — sets the suggestion's own status and writes an audit line, with `relatedEntityId` null and nothing propagated to any record. So the width is appropriate and there was nothing to fix. Two of the three B1 rows were real; this one was not, which is exactly why the rule is *verify the endpoint reaches a decision* rather than *tighten the widest grant*. **(b) A grant report cannot see a missing guard.** `GRANT_TIGHTNESS.md` ranks permissions by how many roles hold them; an endpoint that asks for **no** permission appears in no row at all. The B1 row pointed at the module and the defect turned out to be the endpoint the report was structurally blind to — so the report is a place to start looking, never the inventory of what is wrong |

---

## F2-51 · A read-only viewer could delete a workflow distribution list — the permission for it existed and enforced nothing

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-51 *(the one non-EXPORT row in section C; found by sweeping all 14)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation / catalogued permission enforcing nothing — **the F2-50 class**, and the third consecutive instance of *the mechanism existed and one call site never adopted it* |
| **Severity** | **P3 Low-Moderate** — soft delete (`is_deleted = 1`), tenant-scoped, audited, recoverable in the DB. Configuration, not a record of consequence. The population that could reach it is the problem, not the blast radius |
| **Provenance** | F2-50 closed section C's first row and recommended re-asking the other 14 the same way. Swept 2026-07-29: for each dead permission, does a feature exist that it should guard? **One defect, two deliberate decisions, eleven genuinely dead** |
| **Current State** | `WORKFLOW:DELETE` was catalogued, held by exactly **one** role (`ORG_ADMIN`), and named by no `@PreAuthorize` anywhere. Three delete endpoints exist in the workflow packages: `ApprovalChainController#deleteTemplate` (`hasAnyRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN','COMPLIANCE_OFFICER')`, 2 roles), `ApprovalRequestController#delete` (`hasRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN')`, 1 role), and `WorkflowRecipientListController#delete` — **`hasPermission('WORKFLOW','WRITE')`, 55 of 57 roles** |
| **Problem** | `WORKFLOW:WRITE` is held by 55 of 57 roles, and **F2-13b deliberately left it that wide**: after the approve/write split it means *participate*, and a `SUPPLIER_USER` answering a vendor attestation needs it. That population includes `VIEWER`, `REGULATOR_VIEWER`, `EXTERNAL_AUDITOR`, `SUPPLIER_USER` and all eight `*_INGEST` machine accounts. Participating in a workflow is not the same act as deleting the shared distribution list that **14 modules** route requests through. So the permission designed for this act was held by 1 role and enforced nothing, while the act itself was reachable by 55 |
| **Root Cause** | Identical to F2-50: the permission was catalogued and the endpoint was never swept to adopt it. `WORKFLOW:DELETE` sat in the role editor as a checkbox that changed nothing — F2-27's "manufactures false assurance in the customer's own access review" — while the guard defaulted to the module's WRITE because that is what the sibling CRUD methods used. **A DELETE verb inheriting its siblings' guard is the same silent default that produced F2-29's `EMERGENCY_APPROVED`** |
| **Business Impact** | Modest but real: an external auditor's account or an unattended SIEM ingest credential could remove a distribution list and disrupt workflow routing across every adopting module. The delete is soft and audited, so it is recoverable and attributable — which is why this is P3 and not P1. The governance point is the access review: a tenant revoking `WORKFLOW:DELETE` to restrict deletion achieved nothing |
| **Technical Impact** | Guard moved to `hasPermission('WORKFLOW','DELETE')`, and **V325** makes the permission satisfiable first — **1 → 12 holders**, derived rather than chosen (already holds *some* module's `DELETE` **and** already holds `WORKFLOW:WRITE`), the same two-predicate rule as V324 one action over. Predicate (2) is what prevents the half-grant V324's first draft created. Zero read-only, external or machine personas qualify — verified in the migration, not assumed. ⚠ **The two sibling deletes are deliberately NOT repointed here:** at 1–2 roles they are *tighter* than 12, so moving them to this permission would **widen** them, and a fix that loosens a guard is not a fix. Their `hasAnyRole` form is **F2-05**'s conversion scope |
| **UX Impact** | 43 roles lose the ability to delete a recipient list; all 55 keep read, create and update. ⚠ **A consequence worth naming: the creator can no longer delete their own list unless they hold `WORKFLOW:DELETE`** — live-demonstrated (`viewer` creates a list with 201, then gets 403 deleting it). "Creator deletes own" is an *ownership* rule, not a permission one, and inventing it here would have been a feature. If that turns out to matter, the fix is an owner check, not a wider grant |
| **Recommendation** | 1. Guard + V325 as shipped. 2. Convert the two sibling deletes from `hasAnyRole` to `hasPermission` under **F2-05**, keeping their current tightness — not under this finding. 3. On the eleven genuinely-dead `*:EXPORT` rows: each is a **product** question (build the export, or revoke the permission), and **revoking is a data change needing the owner's go-ahead**. `PRIVACY:EXPORT` looks like a missing feature rather than a spurious permission, given PDPL RoPA reporting. 4. Leave `EVIDENCE:EXPORT` and `ADMIN:EXPORT` alone — both are documented allowlist decisions |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Leave it on WRITE because the delete is soft — rejected: the permission exists for this and enforcing nothing is worse than not existing, per F2-27's own framing. (b) Guard on `WORKFLOW:DELETE` **without** V325 — rejected: that leaves deletion to `ORG_ADMIN` alone and breaks every other administrator, the exact mistake F2-13b caught (a guard change is also a re-grant decision). (c) Add an ownership check so creators can delete their own — a feature, not a fix, and it would need a `created_by` semantic this table does not currently gate on |
| **Trade-offs** | 12 of 57 is tighter than strictly necessary for a soft-deletable config object, and some tenants will want a `SERVICE_OWNER` or `CHANGE_MANAGER` to curate distribution lists. That is a role-editor change, not a code change — the reversible direction, the same trade-off F2-13b and F2-43 both recorded. Nothing was revoked, so no tenant loses a capability except the one this finding is about |
| **Effort** | **XS** — one guard string, one derived grant migration, one reflective test |
| **Verification** | Live **5/5**. **The decisive pair is one actor on one object:** `viewer` (WORKFLOW READ+WRITE+EXPORT, **no** DELETE) creates a list → **201**, then deletes it → **403**; `compliance` (holds DELETE) deletes the same list → **204**, and it stops being returned by the list view. **The pre-fix 204 is derived, not remembered:** that same `viewer` token returns 201 on `POST` to this same controller, which still carries the identical `hasPermission('WORKFLOW','WRITE')` expression the delete used to have — so the old guard demonstrably admitted it. V325 applied to dev **twice from the measured baseline of 1** (1 → 12 → 12, exact 12-role set asserted, grants 1453 → 1464), because applying to a DB that already held the rows would only have proven idempotence. `WorkflowRecipientListGuardTest` 3/3 incl. a named assertion that the delete must not drift back to `'WRITE'` and an inventory test over all 5 endpoints. Suite **2,634/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-29** — guard + V325 + reflective test. **Section C fully swept: 14 rows → 1 fixed here, 2 confirmed deliberate, 11 awaiting a product decision that is the owner's to make** |
| **References** | `V325__workflow_delete_authority.sql`, `module/workflow/requests/controller/WorkflowRecipientListController.java#delete`, `WorkflowRecipientListService#delete` (soft delete + audit), `WorkflowRecipientListGuardTest`, `Appendix/GRANT_TIGHTNESS.md` §C + "Section C, swept", `ExportAuthorizationRatchetTest.ALLOWLIST` (the two deliberate rows). Derivation pattern from **V324**/**F2-13b**; same class as **F2-50**; sibling-delete conversion belongs to **F2-05** |
| **Lesson** | Two. **(a) A sweep's most valuable output was the eleven rows it closed without changing anything.** One of fourteen was a defect; two were decisions someone had already made and documented; eleven were feature-absence. Writing all three verdicts into the generated report is what stops the next reader re-deriving them — and stops someone "fixing" an allowlist entry that exists for a stated reason. **(b) Tightening is not always the answer, even inside a finding.** Two of the three delete endpoints were *tighter* than the permission I was enforcing, so repointing them would have widened them. The instinct to make a module consistent would have loosened two guards while closing one — which is why each endpoint was read rather than pattern-matched |

---

## F2-52 · Guards authorise by role names, and 40 of the 74 they recite do not exist — so features fail closed on the persona they were built for

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-52 *(measured while opening **F2-05**; the platform-wide finding behind that conversion task)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation / **identity-based guards naming roles the role table does not contain** — the fail-closed twin of F2-23. Where F2-23 was a guard demanding a permission nobody held, this is a guard demanding an *identity* nobody can be assigned |
| **Severity** | **P2 High** in aggregate: not a breach but a **capability outage**, and one that is invisible in exactly the way this platform's authorisation defects always are. Per module it ranges from cosmetic to blocking; for `audit_lc` it was blocking |
| **Provenance** | F2-27 recorded two ghost role names in passing (`MODEL_OWNER`, `BOARD_MEMBER`) and called them "F2-05's case in one line". Measuring the whole surface by reflection instead of by grep: **40 of 74**. The one-line case was a 20× under-count — the **fourth** time in this program that a hand or regex count under-reported an authorisation surface |
| **Current State** | Measured 2026-07-29 by `RoleGuardConversionRatchetTest` (reflection over compiled `@PreAuthorize`) joined to live `iam.roles` by `Appendix/scripts/role_guard_census.py`: **647 pure role guards** (a role leg, no `hasPermission` leg) across **38 module packages**, reciting **74 distinct role names**, of which **40 are absent from the 57 rows in `iam.roles`**. **361 of the 647** lose at least one leg to a name that is not there. **40** are left admitting `PLATFORM_ADMIN` alone *by attrition* — distinct from the **31** that name `PLATFORM_ADMIN` and nothing else, which is a decision. Top ghosts by blast radius: `CRO` (72 sites), `TPRM_LEAD` (45), `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_MEMBER` (43), `BOARD_MEMBER` (41), `CIO` (41), `SOC_LEAD` (31) |
| **Problem** | `hasAnyRole('X')` tests for the granted authority `ROLE_X`. A name that matches no role is a permanently false OR leg — no compile error, no startup warning, no log line. Worse, **a role guard never reaches `ModulePermissionEvaluator`, so it never reaches the hardcoded PLATFORM_ADMIN bypass either**: the population that passes is exactly the surviving real names, and nothing else. Neither existing measurement can see any of this — the authorization ratchet counts a role guard as *guarded*, and `GRANT_TIGHTNESS.md` joins **permissions**, which a role-only guard names none of. **First module measured, `audit_lc/ia_plan`:** `IaController`'s 26 endpoints recited 12 roles on reads and up to 5 on writes; `CHIEF_AUDIT_EXECUTIVE`, `AUDIT_DIRECTOR`, `INTERNAL_AUDITOR_LEAD`, `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_MEMBER` and `BOARD_MEMBER` are all absent, leaving **11 of 26 endpoints PLATFORM_ADMIN-only** — create the annual plan, create/update/delete the audit universe, bulk import, recompute residual, transition a plan or an engagement |
| **Root Cause** | A role list is a set of string literals in Java with **no referential integrity to the role table**, written when a design document named personas that the seeded 57-role matrix never adopted (V272 catalogued permissions for 24 modules; it did not create a `CHIEF_AUDIT_EXECUTIVE`). The class javadoc on `IaController` *documented* the intended tiering — "Write roles are tighter: PLATFORM_ADMIN, CHIEF_AUDIT_EXECUTIVE, AUDIT_DIRECTOR on the plan" — so the design was recorded, reviewed and unimplementable, and the code read as though it worked |
| **Business Impact** | A GRC platform whose **internal audit module cannot be operated by an internal auditor**. A customer's head of internal audit cannot build the audit universe or approve the annual plan; the only account that can is the platform administrator, which is precisely the segregation an audit function exists to avoid — an external auditor reviewing IA independence would flag it. Multiply by the other 29 attrition sites: `BoardPackController#create` needs `CRO`, `BiaQuantitativeController#boardApprove` needs `BOARD_MEMBER`, `AuditCommitteeDashboardController#recordResolution` needs `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_CHAIR` or `COMPANY_SECRETARY` |
| **Technical Impact** | Slice 1 of F2-05 shipped: `IaController`'s 26 guards → `hasPermission('AUDIT','READ'/'WRITE'/'DELETE')`, which is **not a new model but the module's own** — `AuditLifecycleController` (same package, `/api/audit-lc`) and `audit_eng`'s `AuditEngagementController` have always used it, including `AUDIT:WRITE` on their annual-plan transition, which is why plan transitions here are WRITE and not APPROVE. **V326** grants `AUDIT:READ` to `CISO`, `RISK_MANAGER`, `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER` — the three real roles the old read guard named that the permission did not cover — so **nobody loses access**. Two permanent instruments added: the reflective **census** (`role-guard-sites.txt` → `ROLE_GUARD_CENSUS.md`, with the attrition split computed rather than eyeballed) and the **ratchet** at 621, which may only decrease |
| **UX Impact** | ⚠ **The frontend has no role gate on these screens at all** — `IaPlanDetailPage.tsx` contains no permission check — so an internal auditor saw every button, clicked "create plan", and got a Forbidden toast. That is the worst of both: no client-side honesty about what the user may do, and a server refusal derived from a role that cannot exist. After the fix the same buttons work, with no FE change needed; the widening is stated rather than incidental — reads gain `BOARD_SECRETARY`, `EXECUTIVE_REVIEWER`, `GRC_ADMINISTRATOR`, `ORG_ADMIN`, `VIEWER`, all of whom already held `AUDIT:READ` **and could already read the sibling register at `/api/audit-lc/annual-plans`** |
| **Recommendation** | 1. Continue F2-05 module by module, ordered by `ROLE_GUARD_CENSUS.md` §B2 (attrition first — those are correctness, not modelling): `socops` 110 · `bcm` 65 · `tprm` 39 · `cyber` 31 · `risk` 28. 2. **Per module, measure the population before and after and grant the difference** — the conversion target is usually already catalogued and already granted, so the migration is small. 3. **Do not create the 40 ghost roles to make the old guards work.** Creating a role is a persona decision (which permissions, which tenant, which SoD implications); the permission catalogue already carries the intent. 4. Fix the 9 `hasAuthority('MODULE_ACTION')` sites separately — they read like permission guards and skip the evaluator, so they fail the *other* way (too tight, no admin bypass) |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Seed the 40 missing roles.** Rejected: it legitimises identity-based authorisation the platform has already replaced, adds 40 rows to every tenant's role editor, and each needs a permission set — a bigger decision than the conversion, taken for the sake of not doing the conversion. (b) **Convert but keep each guard's exact surviving population** (grant the new permission to only the real roles named). Rejected *for this module*: on 11 endpoints that population is `PLATFORM_ADMIN` alone, so "behaviour-identical" would freeze the defect and erase the only written record of the intent. (c) **Leave the guards and fix the docs.** Rejected: the feature stays unusable |
| **Trade-offs** | The plan's F2-05 row prescribes **"behaviour-identical on day one"**, and this slice is deliberately not that — it widens writes to `GRC_ADMINISTRATOR`/`ORG_ADMIN` and extends `INTERNAL_AUDITOR` from 6 of 13 writes to all 13. The rule assumes the recited roles are real; where they are ghosts, identical means *broken*. Stated in the migration header, the controller javadoc and here, rather than smuggled in. `AUDIT:APPROVE` was deliberately **not** invented — it is not catalogued, and guarding on an uncatalogued permission is F2-23 |
| **Effort** | Census + ratchet + first module: **M**. Remaining 37 packages: **L**, S-to-M per module, parallelisable, each independently verifiable |
| **Verification** | **22 live legs, twice, with the controller stashed for the first run so the baseline is measured rather than remembered: 22/22 before → 22/22 after, five legs flipping 403 → 200/201.** The decisive pair is one persona on one act: `auditor` (AUDITOR + INTERNAL_AUDITOR) `POST /api/v1/ia/plan` → **403 before, 201 after**; same for the audit universe and both transitions. The preservation claim is measured too — `so_officer` (CISO) and `rmanager` (RISK_MANAGER) read the plan **200 before and 200 after**, and each holds `AUDIT:READ` *only* via the V326 row, so the migration is load-bearing rather than precautionary. Refusals hold: `viewer`/`ranalyst` writes 403, `so_officer` DELETE 403 (READ ≠ DELETE). One `400` in the first after-run was **not** authorisation — `PLATFORM_ADMIN` got the identical `methodologySummary (both EN+AR) is required before submitting to BOARD_REVIEW`, a pre-existing bilingual completeness rule that only becomes reachable once the guard passes. V326 applied to dev **twice** (8 → 11 → 11), three in-migration `THROW 50326` checks silent. `IaControllerGuardTest` 4/4 (mutation-checked: reverting one guard fails 3 tests across 2 classes), `RoleGuardConversionRatchetTest` 2/2, suite **2,640/0/0** |
| **Slice 2 — `tprm`, 2026-07-30** | **39 guards, and every single one named `TPRM_LEAD`.** Six other ghosts beside it: `PROCUREMENT` (the real role is `PROCUREMENT_OWNER`), `OPS_MANAGER`, `INCIDENT_COMMANDER`, `VENDOR_CONTACT`, `BOARD_MEMBER`, `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_MEMBER`. Where `TPRM_LEAD` was the only non-admin leg that left **8 endpoints PLATFORM_ADMIN-only**, including the *entire* vendor-BCP attestation campaign lifecycle — open, send-reminders, close, cancel — which is the SAMA outsourcing control this module exists to evidence. Live before: `rmanager` (RISK_MANAGER) created a campaign **201** and then could not open it, **403**. Converted to `TPRM:READ/WRITE/DELETE`, already used by this module's other two controllers. **V327** grants READ+WRITE to the four register owners the guards named and the permission lacked — **CISO, RISK_MANAGER, COMPLIANCE_OFFICER, BCM_MANAGER** (READ 13→17, WRITE 4→8) — the four personas who actually run a SAMA outsourcing register. Live **20/20 before → 21/21 after**. ⚠ **Two things this slice got wrong first, both caught by measuring:** (a) the conversion script attributed the HTTP verb from the *first* mapping annotation in its backward search window rather than the last, so `#create`, `#createExitPlan`, `#submit` and the campaign `#create` were classified READ — **writes guarded by a permission 17 roles hold, VIEWER and SUPPLIER_USER included.** Caught by reading the script's own output before running it, reverted, re-derived, and now asserted by a dedicated test. (b) `VENDOR_CONTACT` being a ghost looked like suppliers had been locked out of their own attestation — **refuted by reading the controller instead of the guard list**: `POST /bcp-attestations/by-token/{token}/submit` is `permitAll()` with an opaque per-attestation UUID and is untouched, so `#submit` on `{id}` is only the internal manual submit-on-behalf. Had that not been checked, the "fix" would have granted `SUPPLIER_USER` `TPRM:WRITE` — a third party writing the whole third-party register — to solve a problem that did not exist |
| **The one new permission** | `TPRM:APPROVE`, catalogued by V327 and granted to exactly `CISO` + `RISK_MANAGER`. `ThirdPartyCyberAssessmentController#approve/#retire` named CISO, RISK_MANAGER and PLATFORM_ADMIN — **all real, so those two guards were not defective.** Folding them into `TPRM:WRITE` would have widened an approval gate to all 8 writers including `SUPPLIER_MANAGER`; guarding on `TPRM:APPROVE` *without* cataloguing it would have been F2-23, a permanently closed endpoint. Cataloguing plus an exact-set grant is the only option that is behaviour-identical, and it is what F2-05's row authorises ("**catalogue** permissions for the role-guarded modules"). The migration asserts the holder set is *exactly* those two, and the test asserts APPROVE ≠ WRITE — because every other state-changing endpoint in the module is WRITE, which makes collapsing the tier the most likely tidy-up regression |
| **Slice 3 — `fraud`, 2026-07-30** | **Ten guards, two constants, and the first module with no permission to inherit.** `FraudController` holds its guards in `READ_GUARD` and `WRITE_GUARD` rather than ten annotations — the shape a source regex misses and reflection catches (F2-27 had already recorded 8 constant-valued guards missed that way; these are two more). Both named `FRAUD_OFFICER` and `FRAUD_INVESTIGATOR`, **neither in `iam.roles`**, and on the write constant those two were the *only* non-admin legs — so opening a case, triaging it, recording an update, setting the outcome and closing it were all **PLATFORM_ADMIN-only**. In a Saudi bank, fraud case handling available only to the platform administrator is both an operational dead end and an SoD problem. Live before: `compliance` (COMPLIANCE_OFFICER + GRC_ADMINISTRATOR, 86 of 96 permissions) **403** on `POST /fraud/cases`; `admin` **201**. ⚠ Unlike slices 1–2 there was **nothing to convert to** — `FRAUD` was absent from `iam.permissions` entirely — so **V328** catalogues `FRAUD:READ`+`FRAUD:WRITE` before any guard can name them. Reads go to exactly the **three real roles** the old guard named, so the read population **does not widen at all** (and `VIEWER` is deliberately excluded — the earlier slices widened reads only because VIEWER already held those permissions; here it held none). Writes go to `GRC_ADMINISTRATOR`+`ORG_ADMIN`, **derived**: the only two roles holding ≥80% of the platform's module WRITEs (ORG_ADMIN 24/26, GRC_ADMINISTRATOR 21/26; next is COMPLIANCE_OFFICER at 14). Live **20/20 after**, the full lifecycle create → triage → update → outcome → close all flipping 403 → 200/201 for `compliance`, with `rmanager`/`auditor`/`viewer`/`so_officer` still refused. |
| **What slice 3 found that the first two did not** | **The conversion was not finished at the controller.** `AttachmentAccessPolicy` (SCR-004) maps each `entityType` to *its parent record's own read guard*, and its `FRAUD_CASE` entry had faithfully mirrored this controller's role list — ghosts included. Converting the controller alone would have let a `GRC_ADMINISTRATOR` open a fraud case and then **403 on its attachments**: precisely the failure that class's own union rule warns about, reintroduced by the fix rather than by the original code. Both moved together. ⚠ **No seeded persona isolates it** — `compliance` holds `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER` as well, so it satisfied the old role list anyway — so the leg was measured with a purpose-built single-role user (`GRC_ADMINISTRATOR` for `FRAUD:READ` + `VIEWER` for the `EVIDENCE:READ` the attachment endpoint's *own* guard needs, and zero overlap with the six old role names), then deleted. It reads **200** on the case and **200** on the attachments; `viewer` gets **403** on the attachments while holding `EVIDENCE:READ`, proving the parent check is what refuses; and `admin` reads them holding **no** `FRAUD_READ` at all, so PLATFORM_ADMIN passes *only* via that class's explicit bypass — which is also why moving the entry off role names did not lock the administrator out. **And the existing `AttachmentAccessPolicyTest` had been asserting that `ROLE_FRAUD_INVESTIGATOR` *grants* fraud-attachment access** — a green assertion over a path no real token could take, now inverted. |
| **What slice 3 deliberately did not decide** | **Which persona investigates fraud.** The two ghost roles are **not** created and no existing role is anointed — `FORENSICS_LEAD`, `IR_LEAD` and `SECURITY_ANALYST` all exist and all are plausible, which is exactly why picking one here would be a guess dressed as a fix. `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER` is also deliberately left without `FRAUD:WRITE`: it reads the register today and the old guard did not let it write. What changed is that the answer is now a **checkbox in the role editor** rather than a Java edit and a jar — before V328, `FRAUD:WRITE` did not exist to grant. ⬜ **Open for the customer.** |
| **Slice 4 — `integrations`, 2026-07-30** | **The documentation told the customer to hand an external system a platform-administrator token.** Four inbound-integration endpoints named `SOC_AUTOMATION` and `INTEGRATION_SERVICE` — the Splunk alert webhook, the ServiceNow incident-updated webhook, and the CrowdStrike and Tenable manual polls. Neither name exists in `iam.roles`, so all four were `PLATFORM_ADMIN`-only. That much is the usual F2-52 shape. **What makes this slice different is that the defect had been written into the operator runbooks:** `docs/integrations/SPLUNK.md` and `SERVICENOW.md` instructed the operator to “provision a dedicated integration service account and grant it one of those roles” — and with two of the three ungrantable, **the only name left that a customer could actually grant was `PLATFORM_ADMIN`**. A customer following the shipped instructions to wire up their SIEM or ITSM would issue that system administrator credentials. Same class as **F2-33** (onboarding promised an email the platform cannot send), one tier worse in consequence. Both pages corrected in the same commit, with the reason stated inline rather than silently rewritten. |
| **The sibling that was already right** | The platform **seeds eight `*_INGEST` machine roles** (`SIEM_INGEST`, `SCANNER_INGEST`, `TI_INGEST`, `APPSEC_INGEST`, `DLP_INGEST`, `JML_INGEST`, `SBOM_INGEST`, `BRAND_INGEST`), every one holding `CYBER:WRITE`, and **nine `socops` endpoints already guard ingestion that way** — `SocOpsController#ingestSecurityEvent` is `hasAnyRole('SIEM_INGEST','PLATFORM_ADMIN','CISO')`. So `SIEM_INGEST`, the account provisioned for SIEM ingestion, was **refused by the SIEM webhook while a sibling endpoint writing the same `socops.security_event` table accepted it**. An inconsistency, not a missing control — the F2-13b / CM-C2 shape. Converted the four to `hasPermission('CYBER','WRITE')`: 18 holders — the 8 machines, the cyber humans (CISO, CYBER_MANAGER, SECURITY_ANALYST, IR_LEAD, VULN_MANAGER, APPSEC_LEAD, OT_LEAD, EMERGENCY_RESPONDER), ORG_ADMIN, PLATFORM_ADMIN — and **no** VIEWER, REGULATOR_VIEWER, EXTERNAL_AUDITOR or SUPPLIER_USER. The grant was already correct, so **this is the first F2-05 slice needing no migration at all.** |
| **Why only 4 of this package's 24 role guards moved** | Because the other 20 are **not defective**, and converting them would have loosened the module while closing part of it — the F2-51 lesson at module scale. They name `PLATFORM_ADMIN`, `CISO` and `RISK_MANAGER`, all real, and **no catalogued permission has a population matching {`PLATFORM_ADMIN`, `CISO`}**: `CYBER:READ` would open integration configuration and recorded third-party API-call logs to 19 roles including VIEWER, REGULATOR_VIEWER and EXTERNAL_AUDITOR; `PLATFORM:WRITE` is held by ORG_ADMIN **alone** and would drop CISO, locking out the persona that runs the integrations; `ADMIN:WRITE` is BOARD_SECRETARY / GRC_ADMINISTRATOR / ORG_ADMIN, which is governance administration, not a SIEM connection. And two tiers must be **narrower** than `CYBER:WRITE`, not equal to it: host containment (`CrowdStrikeController#contain`) and vulnerability risk acceptance (`TenableController#acceptRisk`) — because `CYBER:WRITE` includes eight machine feeds, and a SIEM must not be able to quarantine a host. **Live-proven, not asserted:** a `SIEM_INGEST`-only account posts to the webhook **200** and is refused containment **403**. ⬜ **Naming the owner of integration administration is a product decision** — catalogue an `INTEGRATIONS` module, or accept `CYBER` and re-tier the reads. Same open question as `socops` (110 guards, no catalogued module). |
| **Verification (slice 4)** | **12/12 before → 12/12 after, five legs flipping 403 → 200**, and the before-run was taken with the four guards `git stash`ed and **the backend rebuilt from that source**, so the baseline is measured rather than remembered. Headline pair on one account: `revtest_siem` (SIEM_INGEST only, zero overlap with the old guard's three names) POSTs the Splunk webhook **403 → 200**. `so_officer` (CISO) gains both manual polls. Refusals hold: `viewer` (CYBER:READ, no WRITE) and `ranalyst` (no CYBER at all) 403 on every one. `admin` returns 200 either side — **which is why it is the control and not the evidence.** `IntegrationsIngestGuardTest` 4/4, ratchet **572 → 568**, suite **2,654/0/0**. No migration. ⚠ **Two probe errors of mine, both already-catalogued classes:** I posted to `/contain` when the route is `/contain/{hostId}` and read the 404 as a refusal — a range-tolerant assertion hiding a routing miss (the F2-13b lesson again); and my first guard test asserted *package-wide* that no guard names `SOC_AUTOMATION`, which **failed**, because three read endpoints I deliberately did not convert still recite it beside the real `CISO`. That test was asserting work I had not done, so it was scoped to the converted four rather than the claim being quietly widened. |
| **⚠ Probe residue, recorded because it was broader than it should have been** | Triggering the two manual polls made the **stub adapters ingest fake data** — 8 CrowdStrike detections, 5 Tenable findings, 5 `cyber.vulnerabilities`, 20 `integration_external_link` rows — **none of it tagged**, because the stub generated it, not the probe. A tag-based cleanup missed all of it; a sweep for *rows created today* across every `created_at` table in the affected schemas found it, and it is now removed (verified 0 rows written today in all five tables; an FK from `crowdstrike_detection_log` meant children had to go first). ⚠ **And my first cleanup deleted 5 rows it did not create** — pre-existing `[REVIEW-TEST]`/`REVIEW-SIEM`/`SIEM-TEST` `socops.security_event` residue from an earlier session, caught by a `CHARINDEX` filter on `source_host`/`rule_name` rather than on what this probe inserted (20 → 15). Tagged test rows, so no real data lost, but it breaks *delete only what you inserted* and is recorded rather than rounded off. `platform.audit_log` entries are **deliberately kept** — the calls did happen. |
| **Slice 5 — `board_pack`, 2026-07-30** | **A ghost role is not always a lockout. Sometimes it is camouflage.** Every previous F2-05 slice found dead role names *narrowing* an endpoint to PLATFORM_ADMIN by attrition. `BoardPackController`'s read guard did the opposite. It named six roles, **five of them ghosts** — `CRO`, `BOARD_MEMBER`, `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_CHAIR`, `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_MEMBER`, `COMPANY_SECRETARY` — and then ended `or hasAuthority('REPORTING_READ')`. `REPORTING:READ` is held by **20 roles including `VIEWER`**. So the inert half of the expression closed nothing; it merely made a wide guard *read* as a narrow board audience, four lines under a comment asserting that reads "must be gated to a board/governance audience, **not any authenticated principal**". Live before the fix: **`viewer` GET `/api/board-pack/packs` → 200 with all 16 packs** — every audience (BOARD, AUDIT_COMMITTEE, RISK_COMMITTEE, CYBER_COMMITTEE, EXEC), every status, including the 7 APPROVED and 2 PUBLISHED, with `risk_summary_json` populated on 8 rows and `pdpl_summary_json` on 6. The transferable rule: **a mixed guard (`hasAnyRole(…) or hasAuthority(…)`) is the shape where a lockout and an over-exposure are indistinguishable in the source**, and the ghosts hide whichever one is real. |
| **⚠ My own instrument was reporting the wrong direction, and that is why I walked in expecting a lockout** | `ROLE_GUARD_CENSUS.md` had bucketed these two sites as *"PLATFORM_ADMIN alone, by attrition"*. Reading the script: it checked whether a site's authority leg was **dead** (`hasRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN') or hasAuthority('KB_WRITE')` with nobody holding `KB_WRITE` → correctly attrition) but **never checked whether one was alive**. A live `REPORTING_READ` leg admitting 20 roles was therefore invisible to it, and the site was filed under exactly the opposite defect. My going-in note for this slice — *"reading a board pack is administrator-only"* — was inherited from that miscount, and measuring the guard refuted it in the first minute. **Fixed in the same commit:** a live authority leg is now tested first, such sites get their own section **B3 (camouflage)**, and the summary table counts them separately from B2 (attrition). Verified by **replaying the pre-fix guard through the corrected script** and confirming B3 lists `BoardPackController#list` with `REPORTING_READ` / 20 holders — because a new bucket that reports `0` on its first real run is indistinguishable from a broken one, and this one now reads zero *legitimately* (board_pack was the only instance). ⬅ **Third time in this program an instrument, not the code, was the thing that was wrong** (after the `/mock/i` CI rule that would have caught none of W2-1a-ii's three fabrications, and the source regex that under-counted guards four times). |
| **The write tier, decided by the data rather than by the guard** | `create` and `transition` were `hasRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN') or hasRole('CRO') or hasAuthority('REPORTING_WRITE')`. `CRO` is a ghost and `REPORTING:WRITE` is held by exactly two roles (GRC_ADMINISTRATOR, ORG_ADMIN), so authoring a board pack was a GRC-administrator function. But **4 of the 16 existing packs record `drafted_by='board.secretary@tss.com.sa'`** — the persona whose actual job this is, and which the old guard could never have admitted. `ADMIN:WRITE` is exactly {`BOARD_SECRETARY`, `GRC_ADMINISTRATOR`, `ORG_ADMIN`}, so the conversion target was chosen by asking *who the stored data says does this work*, not by pattern-matching the module name. `ADMIN` is also the module that already owns governance administration, and both permissions were already granted — so, like slice 4, **no migration**. |
| **⚠ Not a strict narrowing, stated rather than smoothed over** | Read population goes 20 → 21 roles: **out** go `VIEWER`, `EMERGENCY_RESPONDER`, `MA_LEAD`; **in** come `CRISIS_MANAGER`, `LEGAL_COUNSEL`, `RISK_MANAGER`, `SUPPLIER_MANAGER`. `VIEWER` leaving is the point. `RISK_MANAGER` arriving is a **fix** — a risk manager was 403 on a RISK_COMMITTEE board pack, which is absurd on its face. `SUPPLIER_MANAGER` arriving is the honest cost of using a catalogued tier: **no catalogued permission's population *is* "the board"**, and there is no `BOARD`, `COMMITTEE` or `GOVERNANCE` module in `iam.permissions` (measured — 28 modules, none of them these). Inventing one means choosing its grant list, which is a product decision, so it is recorded as open rather than guessed. `delete` deliberately stays `hasRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN')`: soft-deleting a board pack destroys a record the board reads, and `ADMIN:DELETE` is **not catalogued at all** (ADMIN carries only APPROVE/EXPORT/READ/WRITE), so there was nothing to convert it to. Pinned by a test so a later sweep does not "fix" it. |
| **➕ And the same file carried an F2-22b(vi) miss** | That slice stamped `reviewedBy` and `approvedBy` from the authenticated principal — and its explanatory note sits in `BoardPackDto.TransitionRequest`, a dozen lines from the defect. `draftedBy` stayed `@NotBlank` and client-supplied on the **create** path, and `BoardPackService.create` did `p.setDraftedBy(req.getDraftedBy())` verbatim. Proven by creating a pack that claimed `draftedBy="IMPERSONATED-admin"` and reading it back unchanged. It matters more here than the usual mass-assignment finding because **`board_pack.packs` has no `created_by` or `created_at` columns at all** (checked against `INFORMATION_SCHEMA`) — `drafted_by` is the *only* record of who authored the artefact, so a forgeable value means authorship is simply unattributable. Now `@JsonProperty(access = READ_ONLY)` — the `BaseEntity`/F2-22a choice, so the drafter still serialises **out** for every list row while refusing to bind **in** — plus the service routing it through the existing `actorOrFail` helper, whose message no longer says "approver" now that it stamps three fields. ⬜ **A data-consistency note, not a regression:** existing rows hold display labels (`Sarah Al-Qahtani`, `Board & Committees Secretariat`) while new rows hold usernames. Backfilling is a product call. |
| **What I checked and did *not* change** | `drafted_at` looked unstamped in `create()` — no `setDraftedAt` call anywhere — which would have been a second bug of the same family. It is not one: the entity field carries `= LocalDateTime.now()` as an initialiser, and all 16 rows have it populated. Recorded because *nearly* fixing a non-bug is the failure mode that the `EVIDENCE:EXPORT` allowlist entries exist to prevent. Two genuine findings were also left for their own slices rather than half-done here: **`audience` is stored on every pack and filters nothing** — `listForOrg()` returns all packs to anyone who passes the read guard, so an `AUDIT_COMMITTEE` pack is served to the whole read population, and one existing row has `audience='CYBER_COMMITTEE'` which the DTO's `@Pattern` no longer even permits on create; and **`nca_ecc` has the identical `draftedBy` defect** (`NcaEccFilingDto` `@NotBlank`, `NcaEccFilingService:77` trusting it) **with a UI form field that collects it** (`NcaEccFilingsPage.tsx:119`, *"Drafted by *"*), which makes it a different-shaped fix — the FE falsifies the server-owns-it change — and possibly not a defect at all if an external preparer is meant to be named. |
| **Verification (slice 5)** | **11/11 live, on the rebuilt backend.** The three legs that had to move, all measured on real tokens: `viewer` read **200 (16 packs) → 403**; `revtest_rmgr` (RISK_MANAGER only) read **403 → 200**; `revtest_bsec` (BOARD_SECRETARY only) create **403 → 200**. The three that had to hold: `auditor` and `so_officer` keep read and stay refused write; `ranalyst` (no ADMIN permission at all) stays 403 on both. `admin` is 200 either side — **the control, not the evidence, because PLATFORM_ADMIN bypasses in code.** Forgery leg: every create sent `draftedBy="FORGED-<user>"` and all three stored rows came back stamped `admin` / `revtest_bsec` / `compliance`. `BoardPackGuardTest` **7/7**, ratchet **568 → 564**, full suite **2,661/0/0 BUILD SUCCESS**, no migration. ⚠ **One probe bug of mine, caught before it produced a false result:** `generateExternalId()` sanitises the period label and takes the **first six characters**, so my first probe gave all seven callers the same external id `BP-2026-REVIEW` — a collision that would have made six creates fight over one row. Fixed by prefixing each label with the username. Cleanup deleted **exactly** the rows this probe inserted, by external id rather than by tag (18→16, then 19→16), with both probe users removed and `0` packs left carrying `drafted_at = today`. |
| **Status** | 🔶 **PARTIAL — 3 packages fully closed + 1 partially** (`audit_lc` 2026-07-29, 26 guards; `tprm` 2026-07-30, 39; `fraud` 2026-07-30, 10; `integrations` 2026-07-30, **4 of 24 — every defective one**; **647 → 621 → 582 → 572 → 568**). The **measurement and the ratchet are permanent and complete**; the conversion is F2-05's remaining backlog. Next by attrition count: `board_pack` 4 — where `#get`/`#list` recite six roles of which **five are ghosts**, so **reading a board pack is administrator-only**, and there is no catalogued board/committee module to convert to (measured: the `fraud` shape again) · `audit_committee` 3 · `platform` 3 (`HelpController`, ghost `CONTENT_EDITOR`) · `aml` 1 · `bcm` 1. `knowledge`'s 3 are **F2-53** and need the product call first. ⚠ **`socops` is the largest by raw count (110) but has zero attrition sites and no catalogued `SOCOPS` module** — it needs a decision about which permission owns SOC operations before any conversion, so it is not the cheap next slice its size suggests |
| **References** | `security/RoleGuardConversionRatchetTest.java` · `Appendix/scripts/role_guard_census.py` → `Appendix/ROLE_GUARD_CENSUS.md` · `V326__audit_read_preserve_ia_reviewers.sql` · `V327__tprm_permission_conversion_grants.sql` · `V328__fraud_permission_catalogue_and_grants.sql` · `module/audit_lc/ia_plan/controller/IaController.java` · `IaControllerGuardTest` · `TprmPermissionConversionTest` · `module/fraud/controller/FraudController.java` · `FraudControllerGuardTest` · `module/attachment/service/AttachmentAccessPolicy.java` (the second surface, from **SCR-004**) · sibling convention in `module/audit_lc/controller/AuditLifecycleController.java` and `module/audit_eng/controller/AuditEngagementController.java`. Same fail-closed class as **F2-23**; the 2-name precursor is in **F2-27**; the derive-then-verify migration pattern is **V324**/**V325** |
| **Lesson** | Four. **(a) A guard can be dead without being wrong.** Every one of these expressions evaluates exactly as written; what is broken is a *string's referent*, which no test, compiler or startup check was looking at. The measurement had to join code to data — neither side alone shows anything. **(b) "Behaviour-identical" is only safe when the current behaviour is intended.** The plan's own prescription would have frozen 11 endpoints as administrator-only, because that is what they do today; preserving measured behaviour and preserving *designed* behaviour diverge exactly where the defect is. This is the seventh slice in which the plan's prescription needed correcting on contact with measurement. **(c) The absence of a client-side gate hid the severity.** With no FE permission check, the defect looked like a backend 403 rather than a missing capability — and the same absence is why the fix needed no FE change. A UI that never claims anything about permissions also never reveals that they are wrong. **(d) Converting a guard is not done until every route that serves the record agrees** — added by slice 3. A record's authorisation is a *union over its routes*, so a second surface that mirrored the old guard (`AttachmentAccessPolicy`'s `FRAUD_CASE` entry) turns a correct fix into a new inconsistency: the case opens and its evidence 403s. The mirror was not a defect before the conversion — it was accurate — which is what makes this class easy to miss. Note the direction: SCR-004 taught this rule about *finding* a record's guards, and slice 3 is the same rule running in reverse, about *changing* one |

## F2-54 · The audit ledger recorded a permission where it claimed to record a role — on every row, and differently each time

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-54 *(found running the look-back ADR-017 D-10a's council seat recommended; shipped 2026-08-01 paired with **F2-31**, same table, one probe session)* |
| **Category** | Evidence integrity / **RC-E, server truth recorded from the caller** — here the caller is not a client but Spring Security's authority collection, and the service sampled it instead of resolving it |
| **Severity** | **P2 High.** Not a breach and not a data-loss defect: it is an **evidence-quality** defect in the one artefact this platform sells as regulator-grade proof. A wrong actor attribution on every row is worse than a missing one, because a missing field prompts a question and a populated field ends it |
| **Provenance** | Not sought. Seat A's D-10a review asked *"after 24-of-59 can download the board pack, internal audit's first question is: was it exercised?"* — and the look-back was runnable only because `ExportAuditInterceptor` covers `/api/**`. The exercise query came back clean; **the ledger it was run against did not.** The defect was in the instrument, discovered by using it |
| **Current State** | Measured 2026-07-30, re-measured 2026-08-01 in **both** databases before any code changed. Dev `TSS_GRC`: **6,065 rows, 16 distinct `user_role` values, ZERO role names** — 13 permissions (`BCM_APPROVE` 1,665 · `REGULATORY_READ` 330 · `EVIDENCE_DELETE` 144 · `SCM_READ` 131 · `CYBER_READ` 123 · `SCM_DELETE` 115 · `SUST_READ` 110 · `COMPLIANCE_DELETE` 39 · `WORKFLOW_APPROVE` 26 · `IAM_DELETE` 19 · `ADMIN_APPROVE` 14 · `SUST_WRITE` 11 · `WORKFLOW_READ` 2) and 3 `ROLE_`-prefixed authorities. Prod `TSS_GRC_Deployment`: **3,989 rows, 12 distinct values, ZERO role names.** Also **non-deterministic** — `admin` under 6 different values, `so_officer` under 4, `rmanager` and `auditor` under 3 |
| **Problem** | `AuditLogService.recordEvent` filled the column with `auth.getAuthorities().iterator().next().getAuthority()`. The authority collection is a `HashSet` holding **both** `ROLE_*` role authorities and bare `MODULE_ACTION` permission authorities, so `iterator().next()` returns an arbitrary element of a hash set. It compiles, never throws, and populates the column on every write |
| **Root Cause** | Two errors compounding. **(a)** Treating a *set* as if it had a first element — the same class of mistake as `HashSet.iterator().next()` appearing in the plan's own "measuring a component and reporting the system" table. **(b)** Conflating two namespaces that Spring deliberately keeps separate: roles carry the `ROLE_` prefix precisely so they can be told apart from authorities, and the code discarded that distinction |
| **Business Impact** | An examiner reconciling *"who did this"* against the role matrix gets an answer that is not merely wrong but **differently wrong each time**. That is the damaging property: two rows that disagree about the same user are indistinguishable from a genuine role change between them, so the ledger cannot be reconciled at all. SAMA CSF, NCA ECC 2-12 and PDPL art. 20 all assume the actor attribution in an access record is dependable; the one board-pack export production holds is attributed to `BCM_APPROVE` |
| **Technical Impact** | Every row written since the column existed. Two writers, not one — `AuditLogService.recordEvent` and `WhistleblowerCaseService.anonymousAudit`, and the second wrote NULL deliberately to protect reporter anonymity. Before the fix that deliberate protection was **indistinguishable in the ledger from the platform failing to record an actor**, so the one path that withholds identity on purpose looked exactly like a gap |
| **UX Impact** | The unified audit viewer at `/admin/audit-log/unified` renders the column under a "Role" heading, so the UI asserted the mis-attribution rather than exposing it. No user could have detected the defect from the screen |
| **Recommendation** | Resolve the role set: keep only `ROLE_*` authorities, strip the prefix, de-duplicate, **sort**. Record provenance in a sibling column so *"no role"*, *"no principal"* and *"suppressed by design"* are separable. Label history; do not rewrite it |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Record the "primary" role.** Rejected, and this was the plan row's own prescription: there is no primary role here — `so_officer` holds three (`CISO`, `SECURITY_ANALYST`, `SUSTAINABILITY_OFFICER`) and the four-eyes flows turn on which one acted, so *a* role was never an acceptable answer. (b) **Back-fill real roles onto history.** Rejected as fabrication: a permission does not determine which role its holder had, several roles grant `BCM_APPROVE`. (c) **A CHECK constraint on the provenance column.** Rejected — the `ck_ca_source` landmine, and here a rejected INSERT is a **lost audit row**; pinned by test instead |
| **Trade-offs** | The column is `NVARCHAR(60)` and the platform has 58 roles, so a heavily-granted principal overflows it. Truncation is **labelled** (`RESOLVED_TRUNCATED`, value ends in an ellipsis) rather than silent, because a silently-truncated value looks like a complete role set and is not one. Widening the column was rejected as a bigger schema change than the finding warrants |
| **Effort** | **S** — one method, one entity field, one migration, ten tests |
| **Verification** | Suite **2,727/0/0** (+10). V339 applied to dev twice, idempotent, **6,065/6,065 rows labelled**. Live **26/26** on `:8099` (`:8084` held by a sibling worktree): four personas holding 1, 3, 2 and 2 roles each performed a real audited export; every `user_role` equalled the full sorted set from `iam.user_roles`, **byte-identical across two consecutive calls**, every component present in `iam.roles`, none permission-shaped; the anonymous portal row labelled `SUPPRESSED_BY_DESIGN` with actor and IP still absent; and `NO_PRINCIPAL` appeared on scheduler rows without being staged. Anti-vacuity: the probe asserts it created rows, so "all labelled" cannot be the reading of an empty set |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-08-01** — `V339`, commit `1eeb70c6`. ⚠ Prod applies V339 on its next restart; until then production's ledger carries the defect and the fix is packaged-not-applied (RC-B) |
| **References** | `AuditLogService.java` (`resolveRoles`, `SRC_*`, `USER_ROLE_SOURCES`) · `AuditLogEntry.java` · `WhistleblowerCaseService#anonymousAudit` · `V339__audit_log_user_role_source.sql` · `AuditLogServiceTest` (10 new) · `scratchpad/probe_f254.py` |
| **Lesson** | Three. **(a) A populated column is more dangerous than an empty one.** A NULL prompts an examiner to ask; a plausible-looking wrong value ends the enquiry. **(b) Determinism is a separate property from correctness, and it was the worse failure here.** Being consistently wrong is repairable by a rule; being differently wrong each time destroys reconciliation itself. **(c) The fix's most important decision was what it declined to do.** Back-filling roles onto 2,748 historical rows would have produced a ledger that reads clean — and *a ledger that edits its own past to look clean is worth less than one that admits a gap.* Same rule as V322's `tally_source`: label every row, flip none |
