| **Fix** | **V349** adds `PROPOSED` to `CK_resolution_outcome` — deliberately *not* a decision, so `CommitteeDecisionAuthority.isDecided()` is a vocabulary test rather than a list of exceptions. `create()` defaults a blank outcome to `PROPOSED`, validates against the closed vocabulary (a bad value is now a **400** naming `PROPOSED`, not a 500), and publishes **`COMMITTEE_MOTION_RAISED`** for an undecided motion — a name no seeded rule listens to, which is correct: nothing has been decided, so nobody should be paged. The tally mapping is now a pure static function so *"no votes cast"* → `PROPOSED` is stated and tested rather than an unreachable branch; *"votes recorded but none FOR or AGAINST"* stays `DEFERRED`, which is a real outcome and the case `AR-152` is genuinely for. FE: `PROPOSED` is first in the picker, is the form default, and is bilingual (`مطروح`) |
| **The decision S7's row demanded** | Stated in `CommitteeDecisionAuthority`, and it is a **measurement**: **meeting-bound minutes is authoritative for every change type, because it is the only representable mode** — `meeting_id` is `NOT NULL`, so an asynchronous electronic vote has no representable form. ⚠ `assertDecisionApproves`'s `if (res.getMeetingId() != null)` is a **dead branch** that reads like a documented two-mode exemption; the permissive side is the unreachable one, so anyone making `meeting_id` nullable to add async voting would silently ship a decision path with no minutes gate. Now commented as such at the site |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** (**V349**, with [[F2-100]], which was found while measuring this one). Live **23/23**, two real principals, no `PLATFORM_ADMIN`: a POST with no `outcome` returns `PROPOSED` with nothing announced and **AR-146/147/152 all unmoved**; `outcome:"CARRIED"` is a 400 naming `PROPOSED`. `CommitteeGoldenPathIT#raiseMotion` no longer sends an outcome and asserts `PROPOSED` through the real HTTP stack. Mutation-proven in both languages and both directions |
| **References** | `CommitteeDecisionAuthority` · `CommitteeResolutionService#create` · `#outcomeFor` · `V349` · `CommitteeMotionLifecycleTest` · `resolutionOutcomeVocabulary.test.ts` · `CommitteeGoldenPathIT#raiseMotion` · [[F2-100]] · [[F2-40]] · [[F2-18]] |
| **Lesson** | **A record with no undecided state can only ever describe the past.** The voting engine, the recusal rules and the quorum arithmetic were all built correctly on top of a table that has nowhere to put the question being asked. ⚠ And the half worth carrying: **the cost of a missing state is paid by whatever value gets borrowed to stand in for it.** `DEFERRED` was not a harmless placeholder — it was wired to a live rule, so 38 times the platform asked a secretary to re-table a motion that had never been tabled |


## F2-90 · Approving committee minutes wrote nothing to the audit ledger

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-90 *(found by W1-5's golden-path integration test on its final step, 2026-08-02)* |
| **Category** | Assurance / audit ledger completeness |
| **Severity** | **P2 High.** No authorisation impact — every gate held — but the platform's tamper-evident record omitted the act it is most needed for |
| **Current State** | Measured across the whole of `platform.audit_log`: **`MEETING_ATTENDANCE` was the only meeting action ever written**, and `audit.log` appeared exactly **once** in the whole of `CommitteeService`. Starting a meeting, completing it (which records the minutes text), **approving the minutes**, and cancelling it all wrote nothing |
| **Problem** | Minutes approval is the act that makes a committee decision binding. Four gates guard it — `COMPLETED`, quorate, quorum `COMPUTED` not asserted, and F2-39's separation from whoever recorded the attendance — and Track D asserts `CAB_APPROVED` on the result. An auditor reading the hash-chained ledger ([[F2-31]]) could see the **attendance sheet being replaced** and not the approval resting on it |
| **Why the e-signature is not a substitute** | It is a different subsystem behind a different permission (`SIGNATURE:READ` vs `AUDIT_LOG:READ`), it records a *signature* rather than an *event*, and it carries no actor role, correlation id or before/after status. "Signed" and "recorded in the ledger" are two claims, and the platform was making one while the SAMA/NCA evidence story rests on both |
| **Root Cause** | `recordAttendance` was written during F2-28 with an audit line because *that slice was about attendance provenance*. The surrounding lifecycle predates it and was never revisited. **No unit test could catch it:** 32 tests in `CommitteeQuorumMinutesTest` mock `AuditLogService` and none asserts a call on the approval path — the [[F2-18]] blind spot exactly, and it was found on the golden path's last step |
| **Business Impact** | A committee decision pack assembled from the audit ledger would show attendance edits and no approvals |
| **Status** | ✅ **DONE 2026-08-02** (no migration). `MEETING_START`, `MEETING_COMPLETE`, `MINUTES_APPROVE` and `MEETING_CANCEL` now written; `MINUTES_APPROVE` carries the approver, the persisted quorum (`present/denominator`, `required`, `quorumSource`) and the minutes SHA-256, so the ledger row states the preconditions rather than only the outcome. **Mutation-proven:** disabling the `MINUTES_APPROVE` line fails `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` with the expected/actual action-list diff |
| **References** | `CommitteeService#approveMinutes` · `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` step 14 · [[F2-31]] · [[F2-39]] · [[F2-18]] |
| **Lesson** | **The golden-path assertion was almost written as "the ledger is not empty", and that would have passed.** The meeting *did* have audit rows — for the sheet, not the sign-off. Asserting the named actions is what turned a green tick into a finding |


## F2-91 · The committee-type vocabulary was stated three times and wrong twice

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-91 *(found by W1-5's golden-path integration test on its FIRST call, 2026-08-02)* |
| **Category** | Boundary validation / vocabulary drift |
| **Severity** | **P2 High** — one half is a 500 on a well-formed request, the other silently refuses legal values |
| **Current State** | Three definitions: `CK_com_type` **22** values (`V40`, extended by `V154`); `BoardPackDto.audience`'s `@Pattern` **17**; `CommitteeCreateRequest.committeeType` **none** — only `@NotBlank @Size(max = 30)` |
| **Problem (a)** | The database CHECK was the *only* enforcement on committee creation, so an off-vocabulary type arrived as a `ConstraintViolationException` and surfaced as **500 "An unexpected database error occurred."** The field that **sets** the vocabulary was unvalidated while the field that merely **references** it was validated and ratcheted |
| **Problem (b)** | The board-pack pattern was missing `OTHER`, `ARCH_REVIEW`, `INVESTMENT`, `ALCO`, `AML`. **All five have been in `CK_com_type` since `V154` — 177 migrations before `V331` widened that pattern "to the real vocabulary"**, so the widening was five short on the day it shipped. A tenant with an ALCO or an Investment committee could not scope a pack to it, and the refusal said the value *"must be an `admin.committees.committee_type` value"* while being exactly that |
| **Root Cause** | ADR-017 D-11 recorded that this drift *"cannot recur unnoticed"* because `V331` throws when a **stored** audience fails to resolve. That guard is real and **points the wrong way**: it checks rows against the vocabulary, never the vocabulary's several definitions against each other. The direction that had actually drifted was the one nothing watched |
| **Status** | ✅ **DONE 2026-08-02** (no migration — the database was already right; the Java copies were wrong). `common.model.CommitteeTypes` is now the single statement; both DTOs validate against it and yield a 400 naming the field. **Two ratchets, deliberately split:** `CommitteeTypeVocabularyTest` ties every Java copy together in the ordinary suite, and `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` ties Java to `CK_com_type` **against a real database** — with a failure message naming which side is missing what and which failure it will cause. No unit test can make the second assertion, which is the clearest argument for W1-5 existing at all. **Mutation-proven in both directions** |
| **⚠ Not a defect, and worth stating** | The vocabulary has **no `CAB` or `ECAB`**, and that is ADR-017 D-2: *"the CAB is a role, not necessarily a standing committee."* The golden path's first draft tried to constitute a committee of type `CAB`; widening the vocabulary would have made the test pass **by reversing the decision that unblocked the whole change-governance epic**. `CommitteeTypeVocabularyTest#cabIsARoleNotACommitteeType` now pins the absence with the citation |
| **References** | `common.model.CommitteeTypes` · `CommitteeDtos.CommitteeCreateRequest` · `BoardPackDto#audience` · `V154` · `V331` · ADR-017 D-2, D-11 |
| **Lesson** | **A guard that checks data against a vocabulary is not a guard on the vocabulary.** D-11's claim was true and answered a different question — and the reflex fix for the 500 (add `CAB`) would have quietly overturned an owner-ratified decision, so *the first thing to check when a test fails on a vocabulary is whether the vocabulary is right* |


## F2-92 · The tenant write guard broke every insert in production, and nothing could log in

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-92 *(found by the 2026-08-02 prod deploy's own smoke test, not by a report)* |
| **Category** | Availability / a control that disabled the thing it protects |
| **Severity** | ~~**P0.** Production could not authenticate a single user, and no entity could be inserted~~ → ⚠ **P3, corrected 2026-08-06 from the deployed logs.** **One** login failed, **once**, at 14:25:37 on 2026-08-02 — two log lines, one event, `err.log` empty, and no occurrence on any of the other 23 days of WinSW logs. "Every insert" and "a single user" were inferred from one request |
| **Current State** | With `grc.security.tenant-write-guard.enabled=true` — **hardcoded true in `application-prod.yml`, unset and therefore false in dev** — every insert into a table carrying an identity/`ROWVERSION` fails with `SQLServerException: A result set was generated for update.` Measured: `POST /api/auth/login` → **500** `"Unable to record refresh token — please retry."` because `platform.refresh_token` could not be written. Flip the flag off and the same request returns **200**. |
| **Problem** | ~~It is the interceptor being **registered on the session factory** that breaks inserts~~ — ⚠ **refuted 2026-08-03, see Status: dev has run with it registered since 2026-07-26 and inserts fine.** What survives is the narrower half: the interceptor's own logic never ran, because `platform.audit_log` holds **zero `SECURITY_BLOCK` rows, ever** (re-confirmed in both databases). So a defence-in-depth backstop coincided with the platform going down without blocking anything — the *coincidence* is established, the *causation* was inferred and is now disproved |
| **How long it was live, and why nobody knew** | The most recent successful prod login before this was **2026-07-28** — five days. The interceptor is **inert in dev by flag**, and its own javadoc says so out loud: *"a dev-machine HTTP probe exercises the per-service stamp, **never this bean**."* `application-prod.yml`'s own comment adds the condition that was skipped: *"Activate only after the documented staging soak … watch the audit log for any `SECURITY_BLOCK` entries on legitimate flows before relying on it."* Nothing exercised it, so nothing caught it |
| **Ruled out, by measurement rather than reasoning** | Not caused by the 2026-08-02 deploy: `TenantWriteGuardInterceptor`, `TenantWriteGuardConfig`, `RefreshTokenStoreService`, `RefreshToken` and `AuthController` are **byte-identical** between the previous jar and this one (`md5` over the extracted `.class` files), and **none of V337–V344 touches `refresh_token`**. Not schema drift: prod and dev column types, nullability, defaults, identity and `ROWVERSION` are identical, on the **same SQL Server instance and build** (`15.0.2000.5`) with identical `compatibility_level` and every ANSI/isolation database option equal. Not a trigger: `sys.triggers` has exactly one trigger in the database and it is on `socops.forensics_custody_event` |
| **Root Cause** | Prime suspect is Hibernate 6's handling of the deprecated legacy `Interceptor#onSave(...)` hook: implementing it changes the insert strategy so the generated-value read-back is executed through `executeUpdate()`, and SQL Server answers a result set. **Not yet proven** — the proof needs the flag enabled in dev, which is precisely the reproduction nobody has ever run |
| **Business Impact** | The deployed platform was unusable — a 500 on the first screen — for anyone who tried between 07-28 and 08-02. Discovered by our own probe rather than by a customer, but only just |
| 🔑 **THE MITIGATION NEVER TOOK EFFECT (2026-08-06, ADR-017 D-16)** | `external.properties` reaches the app through `spring.config.import` in `application.yml`, and a **profile-specific** document outranks `application.yml` and everything it imports. `application-prod.yml` set `grc.security.tenant-write-guard.enabled: true` **literally**, so the operator's `=false` was outranked and discarded — no error, no warning. **Production booted 2026-08-03 11:02:18 logging `[TenantWriteGuard] interceptor WIRED`**, a line `TenantWriteGuardConfig` emits *only* in the `enabled == true` branch. The guard has been ON continuously since, four days, **zero** occurrences of the error — so the supervised soak this row was waiting for has already run. Root cause of the inert override filed as [[F2-149]] |
| ⚠ **And the diagnosis is refuted by the same logs** | Refresh-token rows were written at **11:32:13 and 11:34:17 on 08-02**, three hours before the failure, with the flag already true. The guard had been on for days *with working logins*. What changed at 14:23 was a **deploy that applied V337–V344 in-process**, not the flag. So *“the tenant write guard broke every insert”* cannot be right as stated |
| ⚠ **“Verified working afterwards” is not in the data** | This row records the mitigation as verified with *“login 200 … refresh-token rows written again”*. Production's `platform.refresh_token` has **no row after 2026-08-02 11:34:17** — before the mitigation was written at 14:33. **Nobody has logged into production since**, so the login path is unverified since the incident: not demonstrated broken, not demonstrated working. Smoke-test it at the next restart |
| **What the stack trace narrows it to** | Read from the log for the first time on 2026-08-06: `GetGeneratedKeysDelegate.performMutation` → `ResultSetReturnImpl.executeUpdate` → the driver throwing `A result set was generated for update.` with **SQL Error 0, SQLState null** — thrown by the **JDBC driver**, not by SQL Server. Both databases were re-diffed and are identical on every dimension that can produce a result set: 1 trigger each (same table, unrelated), `refresh_token` 18 columns identical, no computed columns, no change tracking, no temporal table, identical compat level / ANSI options / RCSI. **Still not root-caused, and this row no longer claims to be** |
| **Interim disposition** | ⚠ **Flag set to `false` in `D:\Deployment\tss-grc\app\external.properties`** — ⚠ **which did nothing; see the row above.** with a long operator note giving this finding's id and the re-enable condition. Prod verified working afterwards: login 200, five authenticated reads 200, refresh-token rows written again. Per-service `setOrganisationId(TenantContext.getCurrentTenant())` stamps remain the **primary** tenant control; this bean is only the backstop, and a backstop that prevents all writes protects nothing |
| **Recommendation** | (1) **Enable the flag in dev** and reproduce — that single missing step is the finding's real root cause and it is one line of config. (2) Migrate the hook to the non-deprecated Hibernate 6 API (`onPersist`/an event listener) and re-verify inserts with generated values. (3) Add an integration test with the guard **on**, in the `it` profile W1-5 just created — `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` writes committees, meetings, votes and changes, so it is already the right harness. (4) Only then re-enable in prod |
| **Effort** | **S** to reproduce and probably S to fix; the test is the durable part |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Still open — P0, mitigated. ⚠ ROOT CAUSE CORRECTED 2026-08-03 — the one recorded here is refuted.** The fix plan's own first step (*enable the flag in dev and reproduce*) was carried out and **the defect does not reproduce.** (1) The flag has been `enabled: true` in `application-dev.yml` since `695c907c` — **2026-07-26, seven days before this finding was filed** — contradicting the premise that it was unset in dev. (2) The dev backend logs `[TenantWriteGuard] interceptor WIRED` on every boot and `platform.refresh_token` holds **2,075 rows, the newest written that morning**. (3) `TenantWriteGuardInterceptor` and `TenantWriteGuardConfig` are **byte-identical between the deployed commit `473da706` and HEAD**. (4) Both databases sit on the same SQL Server instance and match on schema, compatibility level 150, collation, snapshot options, the single trigger in either database (on an unrelated table), and `refresh_token`'s exact `IDENTITY`+`ROWVERSION` shape. Now **pinned by `TenantWriteGuardOnIT`** (4 tests, `it` profile, 21 s): the real application boots with the flag ON, logs in, writes the refresh-token row, completes an identity insert, and still refuses a cross-tenant one. **The profile is now eliminated too (2026-08-03, second pass).** Booted locally on the **`prod` profile** with the guard explicitly ON, SSL and Flyway off, against the dev database: `[TenantWriteGuard] interceptor WIRED`, `POST /api/auth/login` → **200**, and `platform.refresh_token` went **2100 → 2101**. So the code, the schema, the database options *and the profile* all fail to reproduce it. **What is left is the deployed runtime itself** — Flyway-at-startup (the one variable deliberately excluded, since running it against dev would migrate a Python-bootstrapped schema), the named-instance JDBC URL (`localhost\SQLEXPRESS` vs `localhost:1433`), the WinSW service account, and the rest of `external.properties`. Narrowing further needs the flag switched back on in production under supervision, which is an operator decision and not a test's. ⚠ *Two incidental notes for whoever does it: the prod profile refuses to boot without an SSL keystore (`server.ssl.*`, supplied by `external.properties` on the server) and pins **both** `server.port` 8090 and the management port — a local prod-profile boot must override both or it collides with the live service.* Flag stays `false` in prod meanwhile, so **production still has no Hibernate-level cross-tenant backstop**; the per-service `setOrganisationId` stamps remain the only control |
| **References** | `TenantWriteGuardInterceptor#onSave`/`#decideInsert` · `TenantWriteGuardConfig` · `application-prod.yml` `grc.security.tenant-write-guard.enabled` · `RefreshTokenStoreService#recordIssuance` · `docs/security/MASS_ASSIGNMENT_DEFENSE.md` · [[F2-18]] |
| **Lesson (2026-08-03)** | **A root cause that was never reproduced is a hypothesis wearing a conclusion's clothes — and this one was load-bearing.** Believed, it says a P0 control is inherently unsafe and must stay off forever. The finding even *named* the missing step — "enable the flag in DEV and reproduce — that missing step **is** the root cause" — and then wrote a causal sentence as though the step had been taken. Worse, the environment claim it rested on ("unset in dev") was **already false in the repository**, one `git log` away, when it was written. **If a fix plan says "reproduce it first", the causal sentence waits.** |
| **Lesson** | **A control that is off in dev and on in prod is not tested — it is deployed and hoped for.** This is the same shape as F2-62's dead flag and A17-D2's fail-open WARN, with the polarity reversed: instead of a control that did nothing, a control that did far too much. Both were invisible for the same reason — *the only environment that runs it is the one nobody probes.* Its javadoc named the gap ("never this bean") and its config named the missing precondition ("only after the documented staging soak"); **two accurate warnings, written by us, consumed by nobody** |


## F2-153 · A fresh install enforces 32 fewer CHECK constraints and 53 fewer foreign keys than dev, and an idempotency guard is why

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-153 *(measured 2026-08-07, extending [[F2-09]]'s convergence diff to constraint bodies)* |
| **Category** | Deployment integrity — the schema a customer gets does not enforce what dev enforces |
| **Severity** | **P1.** `iam.user_roles` and `iam.role_permissions` have **no foreign keys at all** on a fresh install, so a grant can reference a deleted role or a permission that never existed and nothing objects. Value-domain CHECKs are absent across `scm` (11), `bcm`, `risk` and `ethics`, so a fresh install accepts statuses and types the application's own enums do not contain |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-07 — V364, V365, V366.** Every remaining difference on every measured axis is either a **deliberate exclusion** (an end in [[F2-141]]'s no-code set) or **above the chain's stop**. Closing measurement: CHECK dev 417 / built 414, missing 3 = the 3 excluded · FOREIGN KEY dev 185 / built 178, missing 7 = the 7 excluded · UNIQUE-by-property dev 271 / built 268, missing 3 = the 3 excluded · **extra 0 on all three axes** |
| 🔑 **V366 — and the direction I had not measured is the one that reaches production** | Running the diff the other way round (build **minus** dev) found **five objects the migration set creates and neither dev nor prod had**: `UX_departments_org_code`, `UX_bcp_plans_org_code`, `UX_bia_assessments_org_code`, `UX_bcm_compl_org_req` and `CK_bia_rto_le_mtpd`. **So the running platform allowed two departments with the same code in one organisation**, duplicate BCP plan codes, duplicate BIA codes, a duplicated compliance-requirement mapping, and a BIA whose RTO exceeded its MTPD — while a customer provisioned from the folder was protected from all five. Proven on dev in a rolled-back transaction: a second department with code `'CEO'` in org 1 is **REFUSED naming the index** with it, **ACCEPTED** without. ⚠ **Prod descends from a dev dump, so a gap in the BOOTSTRAP is a gap in the live system, while a gap in the MIGRATIONS only bites a fresh install.** I spent two slices on the less consequential direction because it was the one I had measured |
| ⚠ **And it settles what this row filed as a product question** | The row said `ck_bia_rto_le_mtpd` was *"either dev's data violates RTO ≤ MTPD or the rule is wrong"*. Measured: **dev 7 rows, prod 7 rows, 0 violations, 0 NULLs**, and no migration ever drops it. The rule is right, the data satisfies it, and the constraint is simply absent from the bootstrap — **not a product question at all, a convergence gap in a direction I had not looked**. The four unique indexes stay **filtered** (`WHERE is_deleted = 0`, copied from the built database): unfiltered, they would refuse to re-use the code of a soft-deleted department, turning a correctness fix into a data-entry dead end |
| 🔑 **The FK half is why this row is a P1, demonstrated not argued** | On a fresh install `iam.user_roles` had **no `FK_ur_user` and no `FK_ur_role`**, and `iam.role_permissions` no `FK_rp_role` / `FK_rp_perm` — so the authorisation tables had no referential integrity at all. Proven on the fresh-install database inside a **rolled-back transaction**: a grant naming role `100002`, which does not exist, is **REFUSED and the error names `FK_ur_role`** with the key in place, and **ACCEPTED** with it dropped. Deleting a role left its grants behind, pointing at nothing, and nothing objected |
| ➕ **A third object class, found by auditing the second** | Checking index cover for two FKs showed the "missing index" behind them is a **UNIQUE constraint** — so the index axis is a *correctness* axis. A fresh install lacked **11**, including `iam.users.UQ_username (organisation_id, username)` and `UQ_email`, `iam.roles.UQ_role_name`, `iam.permissions.UQ_permission (module, action)` and `platform.organisations.UQ_org_short_code`. **Two users could share a username inside one tenant**, and `UserRepository.findByUsername` returns an `Optional` — with two matching rows it does not return the right one, it throws or picks arbitrarily. The database constraint was the only control and a fresh install did not have it |
| ⚠ **A false positive I nearly shipped, and the third instrument to need this correction** | Comparing unique indexes **by name** reported 15 missing. One was `UQ__nafath_t__85C600AEA2D5FA14` on `platform.nafath_transaction(transaction_id)` — a **system-generated** name, and dev, prod and the built database each carry that constraint under a *different* generated name. It read as absent while being enforced everywhere. Re-comparing on the **property** (schema, table, ordered columns, filter) gives **14 missing, 11 shippable**. This is the treatment DEFAULT constraints already had on the CHECK axis, which I failed to carry across |
| 🔑 **Why 29 and not 32 — my own gate refused the first draft** | It constrained all 32 and `SchemaContractRatchetTest.noMigrationAboveTheBaselineWritesToATableTheApplicationCannotRead` failed the build: `risk.risk_indicators` (LEGACY), `workflow.workflow_instances` and `workflow.workflow_tasks` (SEED_ONLY) are three of [[F2-141]]'s 36 tables with **no JPA mapping and no Java reference**. That gate's own message is the instruction — *"if the data matters the table needs a mapping first; if it does not, do not ship it"* — and **constraining a table nothing reads protects nothing**. ⚠ The workflow pair is a **near-name trap** and the register is right: the live engine maps `workflow.instances` / `definitions` / `states` / `transitions` / `action_log`, while the dead tables are `workflow.workflow_instances` / `workflow_tasks` — same schema, `workflow_` prefix. **Three generations exist** (`platform.workflow_*` dropped by V114, `workflow.workflow_*` dead, `workflow.*` live), which is [[F2-148]]'s shape. The exclusion is **derived from the committed register**, not hand-listed, so this migration and that gate cannot disagree |
| ⚠ **And this row's own remedy note was wrong — corrected** | It said the 29 non-guard-skipped CHECKs *“need a data audit first”*, reasoning that adding a constraint over violating data fails. True in general, and **irrelevant here**: a database only gains a constraint if it lacks one, and measuring showed **prod holds all 32 and all 53 too** (prod 398 CHECKs / 181 FKs, and 0 of the missing set absent from it). So every `ADD` is a **no-op in both real databases**, and the only database that changes is a fresh install — where the audit that matters is against the chain build, which reports **0 violations across all 32** and every parent table present. 🔑 **I generalised a real hazard without asking which database would be in that state, and made a mechanical change look blocked.** Prod's smaller totals are explained entirely by the 20 migrations pending on it, not by a defect |
| **What V364 does** | 32 idempotent `ALTER TABLE … ADD CONSTRAINT`, **definitions read from dev's own `sys.check_constraints`** rather than composed — a hand-written CHECK that differed from dev's would be a new divergence wearing a fix's clothes. Three self-checks THROW: all 32 present; **none `is_not_trusted`** (so a later `WITH NOCHECK` to get past a violation fails the migration rather than leaving a constraint that covers the next row and not the ones already there); none `is_disabled` (F2-62's dead-flag shape) |
| **Verification** | Applied **twice** to dev (29/29 before and after — a proven no-op) and **twice to the chain-built database (0 → 29, all trusted, all enabled)**. Then enforcement proven in both directions inside a **rolled-back transaction** on the fresh-install database: with `CK_incidents_severity` in place, `severity='BANANA'` is **REFUSED and the error names the constraint**; with it dropped, the same insert is **ACCEPTED** — which is what a fresh install did before V364. After rollback: 0 BANANA rows, constraint present, 0 tagged rows |
| **Provenance** | [[F2-09]]'s table-and-column half reached zero, so the remaining question was constraints. I expected the comparison to be unmeasurable — auto-generated names — and measured the noise floor first: **every CHECK and every FK in both databases is user-named** (0 system-named of 416 and 385), and of the **384 CHECKs present on both sides, 0 differ in definition**. So it is comparable, exactly, and nothing had ever compared it |
| **🔑 The measurement, partitioned against the chain's stop (V320)** | **CHECK: dev 416, built 385.** 32 missing, and **0 of them are explained by the stop** — none is created by a later migration. 29 are named by **no migration anywhere**; 3 are named by V4 and absent anyway. Plus **1 in the other direction**: the migrations create `bcm.bia_assessments.ck_bia_rto_le_mtpd` and **dev does not have it**, so dev can hold a BIA with RTO > MTPD that a fresh install would reject. **FOREIGN KEY: dev 185, built 132.** 53 missing, again **0 explained by the stop**, all 53 named by no migration |
| **🔑 Root cause of the 3, and it is a mechanism not a bug** | `V1__core_schemas.sql:745` creates `cyber.incidents` with `severity NVARCHAR(20) NOT NULL` and no value CHECKs. `V4__incident_module.sql:16` creates it **again**, wrapped in `IF NOT EXISTS (… TABLE_NAME = 'incidents')`, and *that* version carries `CK_incidents_severity`, `_status` and `_category`. On a fresh install V1 wins, V4's guard skips, and **every inline CONSTRAINT in the skipped CREATE goes with it** — so a fresh install accepts `severity = 'BANANA'`. ⚠ **This generalises: any idempotency-guarded re-CREATE of an existing table silently drops its inline constraints.** The guard is working exactly as written; what it also discards is invisible at the call site. Third relative of [[F2-112]] this programme has found — a control that cannot fire |
| **Why the 29 + 53 are absent** | They come from the Python bootstrap (`setup_*_db.py`), which creates tables *with* their constraints, while the migration set's equivalent CREATE omitted them. Same origin as [[F2-140]]'s three tables and [[F2-146]]'s 27 columns: **dev is built by one authority and a customer's schema by another**, and only the objects someone thought to add twice exist in both |
| **⚠ What this finding does NOT claim** | That prod is missing them. Prod descends from a dev dump (baseline 292), so it has dev's constraints — which is precisely why nobody noticed. The population at risk is **a genuinely fresh install**, which is [[F2-143]]'s and [[F2-146]]'s population too |
| **DEFAULT constraints were deliberately not compared** | 1,910 of dev's 2,259 defaults are **system-named** (`DF__table__col__ABC123`), so comparing them by name is noise and comparing by definition would need per-column pairing. Stated rather than silently skipped — the honest scope of this instrument is CHECK and FK |
| **The remedy, in the order that makes it safe** | (1) the 3 guard-skipped CHECKs are a new migration that `ALTER TABLE … ADD CONSTRAINT` them — trivial and safe, dev already satisfies them; (2) the 53 FKs and 29 CHECKs need a **data audit first**: adding a FK to a table that already holds an orphan fails, and this is exactly where a fresh install differs from a populated one, so each needs `WITH NOCHECK` or a cleanup decision; (3) `ck_bia_rto_le_mtpd` is a **product** question — either dev's data violates it or the rule is wrong |
| **Effort** | **M** for (1), **L** for (2)-(3), and (2) cannot be done blind |
| **Verification** | `schema_convergence.py --stop 320` now reports all four buckets per constraint kind. Numbers above are its output. V1/V4 duplication read from source at the line numbers given; V1's version confirmed to carry no CHECK. ⚠ The three `CK_incidents_*` names also appear in **V261's comments only**, and the scan strips comments before attributing — without that, V261 would have been named as their creator |
| **References** | `schema_convergence.py` · `V1__core_schemas.sql:745` · `V4__incident_module.sql:16` · `V261` · [[F2-09]] · [[F2-112]] · [[F2-140]] · [[F2-143]] · [[F2-146]] |
| **Lesson** | **An idempotency guard protects the statement and discards everything the statement carried.** `IF NOT EXISTS … CREATE TABLE` reads as "make sure this table exists"; what it means is "if the table exists, skip its columns, its defaults, its CHECKs and its keys". ⚠ And the methodological half: **I expected this axis to be unmeasurable and checked instead of assuming** — the noise floor was zero, and had I trusted the expectation the whole finding would have stayed invisible. *"Ask of every control: what does it compare, and does that data exist?"* has a companion — **ask of every axis you decline to measure why you think it cannot be measured** |

## F2-152 · A board approval records no approver, and the CFO approval's approver is whatever the request says

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-152 *(measured fixing [[F2-52]]'s last attrition lockout, 2026-08-06)* |
| **Category** | Attribution — [[F2-22b]]'s forgeable-authorship shape, on a three-stage approval chain |
| **Severity** | **P2.** The audit ledger records the acting principal, so the act is traceable; the **record** is not. A quantitative BIA is BCM evidence read by an examiner from the row, not from the ledger |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **Open, filed not fixed.** [[F2-52]] fixed *who may* board-approve; this is *what gets written when they do* |
| **Provenance** | Not sought. F2-52 asked what the board-approve stage records, to judge whether the guard was the only control. It is |
| **🔑 Half one — the board approval has no signer** | `QuantitativeBiaService.boardApprove` sets `status`, `boardApprovedAt`, `boardMinutesRef` and `updatedAt`. **There is no `board_approved_by` column and none is written.** So the row says *a board approved this, at this time, per these minutes* and cannot say who signed. Compare the stage before it, which has `cfo_approved_at`, `cfo_approver_name` **and** `cfo_comments` |
| **⚠ Half two — and the CFO's approver name is supplied by the caller** | `CfoApprovalRequest` carries `@NotBlank String approverName`, written to `cfo_approver_name`. It is never compared to the principal. So the one stage that *does* record a signer records **whatever the request body says** — exactly [[F2-22b]](vi)'s `draftedBy` defect, which the board-pack slice fixed by stamping from the principal and marking the field READ_ONLY. The same fix applies here and was not in this slice's scope |
| **Why the guard is the only control** | `guardTransition` enforces the **order** of the stages (`DRAFT → … → CFO_APPROVED → BOARD_APPROVED`) and nothing enforces that the stages are **different people**. With no approver recorded on the board stage and a forgeable one on the CFO stage, the row cannot even be audited for self-approval after the fact. That is why [[F2-52]] refused `BCM:APPROVE` here: it contains `BCM_MANAGER`, the stage-one role, and the guard was the only thing keeping stage 1 and stage 3 apart |
| **Measured state** | dev `bcm.bia_quantitative`: **1 row, status `CFO_APPROVED`**, `board_approved_at` and `board_minutes_ref` both NULL — the row was parked at the stage the ghost role had closed. So there is **no existing board approval to re-attribute**, which makes the fix additive and cheap |
| **The remedy** | Add `board_approved_by_user_id` (nullable, no default, no backfill — [[F2-109]]/CE-15's position: capacity, never invented attribution), stamp it from the principal, and make `cfoApproverName` READ_ONLY + stamped the way `draftedBy` now is. Then an SoD check across the three stages becomes possible at all |
| **Effort** | **S** for the stamping, **S** for one migration. The reason it is filed rather than done is that adding an approver column while `cfo_approver_name` stays forgeable would leave the chain half-attributed, and both halves belong in one reviewed change |
| **Verification** | `QuantitativeBiaService:221-238` read from source — no approver assignment. `BiaQuantitative` entity: `cfo_approved_at`, `cfo_approver_name`, `cfo_comments`, `board_approved_at`, `board_minutes_ref`, and no board approver field. `CfoApprovalRequest` at `BiaQuantitativeDtos:92`. Live: `EXECUTIVE_REVIEWER` board-approved a cloned row **200 → BOARD_APPROVED** with `minutesRef` and no signer stored |
| **References** | `QuantitativeBiaService#boardApprove` · `BiaQuantitativeController` · `BiaQuantitativeDtos.CfoApprovalRequest` · `BiaQuantitative` · `BiaApprovalChainGuardTest` · [[F2-52]] · [[F2-22b]] · [[F2-109]] · [[F2-151]] |
| **Lesson** | **Fixing who may act is half a control; the other half is what the act records.** Three slices in a row have now found the second half missing right behind the first — [[F2-151]]'s self-declared `attestedRole`, [[F2-150]]'s commander-as-a-label, and here an approval with no approver. **After correcting a guard, read the write path it now admits people to.** |

## F2-151 · No audit-committee member can sign the committee's attestation, and anyone who can may sign as its Chair

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-151 *(measured fixing [[F2-52]]'s audit-committee guards, 2026-08-06)* |
| **Category** | Governance control whose subject cannot operate it, plus an unverified capacity claim |
| **Severity** | **P2.** Latent — `audit_committee.dashboard_snapshot` and `dashboard_attestation` are **0 rows in dev**, so no false attestation exists yet. Structural the first time the committee meets, and the artefact is the one SAMA CG §6 and CMA CG Article 54 require the committee to report from |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **Open, filed not fixed.** [[F2-52]] corrected the guard *names* (see below); the two halves here need a decision this slice deliberately did not take |
| **Provenance** | Not sought. F2-52's slice asked which real role each ghost name meant. `COMPANY_SECRETARY` had a near-miss (`BOARD_SECRETARY`). `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_CHAIR` and `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_MEMBER` had none — so the question became *what does the platform know about committee membership?* |
| **🔑 Half one — the Chair and the members cannot attest, and no mechanism admits them** | **No role expresses committee membership.** The roster that could — [[F2-26]]'s `admin.committee_member_terms.member_user_id` — is **NULL on all five audit-committee seats** (committee id 3, `AUDIT_COMMITTEE`), whose `member_name` values are prose: *Mohammed Al-Ghamdi, Nasser Al-Dosari, Sara Al-Qahtani, Head of Internal Audit, External Auditor*. So *“is this user the Chair of the audit committee”* is unanswerable from data, and an entity-bound guard leg would be a control that cannot fire — [[F2-112]]'s shape, which this programme has now met three times ([[F2-112]], [[F2-150]], here) |
| **⚠ Half two — the capacity is self-declared, and it is the more serious half** | `AuditCommitteeDashboardService.attestSnapshot` does `a.setAttestedRole(req.attestedRole())` — **verbatim from the request body**. The signer's *identity* is taken from the principal and hashed (`text \| userId \| ISO-timestamp`), which is right; the **role they signed in is checked against nothing**. `AttestRequest` accepts `CHAIR\|MEMBER\|AUDIT_DIRECTOR\|EXTERNAL_AUDITOR\|COMPANY_SECRETARY` and `CK_ac_att_role` mirrors it. So an admitted `INTERNAL_AUDITOR` — one of the roles the guard *does* let in — can POST `attestedRole: "CHAIR"`, and the platform stores, hashes, audit-logs and F8-publishes an audit-committee **Chair's** attestation. The Chair cannot attest at all |
| **Put together** | The people whose assurance the control exists to capture are locked out, and the people who are let in can claim to be them. That is not a gap in a nice-to-have: an audit-committee attestation is the signature a regulator reads |
| **What [[F2-52]] already fixed, so this row is not overstated** | The write tier was `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_CHAIR · COMPANY_SECRETARY · PLATFORM_ADMIN` — two ghosts — so generating, resolving and finalising a snapshot was **the platform administrator alone**, while the comment said *“the Chair / Secretary / Platform Admin trio”*. `BOARD_SECRETARY` is now named and the three false comments are corrected. The Chair leg is not restored, on purpose |
| **The remedy, and why it is one decision not two** | Both halves need the same thing: committee membership as data. Either (a) populate `member_user_id` on the roster and gate on *seat*, which also makes `attestedRole` derivable rather than declared — the [[F2-26]] path, and the one that matches SAMA's independence requirements; or (b) seat `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_CHAIR`/`_MEMBER` as roles, which is cheaper and **wrong in the same way [[F2-145]] refused for `MODEL_OWNER`**: a role says *“some chair”* where the control needs *“the chair of this committee”*. Recommend (a). Also fold in: the capacity vocabulary still says `COMPANY_SECRETARY`, a role name the platform does not have |
| **Effort** | **M** — the linkage API and UI already exist ([[F2-26]]); populating five seats is a tenant-data act, and deriving the capacity is a small service change once the seats resolve |
| **Verification** | `admin.committee_member_terms` for committee 3: **5 seats, 0 with `member_user_id`**. `iam.roles` contains no `AUDIT_COMMITTEE_*` or `BOARD_MEMBER` row in either database. `attestSnapshot` writes `req.attestedRole()` unmodified — read from source at `AuditCommitteeDashboardService:651`. Both tables 0 rows, so nothing to remediate retrospectively |
| **References** | `AuditCommitteeDashboardController` · `AuditCommitteeDashboardService#attestSnapshot` · `AuditCommitteeDashboardDtos.AttestRequest` · `CK_ac_att_role` · `admin.committee_member_terms` · `AuditCommitteeGuardTest` · [[F2-52]] · [[F2-26]] · [[F2-112]] · [[F2-145]] · [[F2-150]] |
| **Lesson** | **A guard that cannot name its subject and a body that lets the caller name themselves are the same defect from two ends.** Both come from the platform not knowing who sits on the committee — and the roster table for it exists, unpopulated. Third time now that asking *“can this be entity-bound instead of role-bound?”* has answered *“there is a column for it and nobody writes it”* ([[F2-112]], [[F2-150]], this). **When a slice cannot bind a guard to an identity, the finding is usually in the data, not the guard** |

## F2-150 · The crisis commander is a free-text label inside a JSON blob, and the column that could name them is empty on every row

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-150 *(measured while fixing [[F2-52]]'s crisis-room guard, 2026-08-06)* |
| **Category** | Identity vs label — the same defect shape as [[F2-26]] and [[F2-113]], one table over |
| **Severity** | **P2.** Nothing is exposed and nothing 500s. The cost is that *“who is commanding this crisis”* cannot be answered by a query, so no guard, notification, escalation or handover can key on it — during the one event where that matters most |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **Open, filed not fixed.** The remedy is a product decision about who a crisis commander is, and a backfill nobody can derive |
| **Provenance** | Not sought. [[F2-52]]'s crisis-room slice asked whether *commander-tier* could be expressed as data rather than as a role list, so I read `bcm.crisis_events`. `crisis_manager_id` exists — and is the answer to that question only if it is populated |
| **🔑 The measurement** | dev `bcm.crisis_events`: **5 rows, `crisis_manager_id` set on 0 of them**. Meanwhile `crisis_team_json` is populated on **5 of 5**, and it names the commander — as prose: `[{"userId":1,"role":"Crisis Manager"},{"userId":2,"role":"Cards Lead"}]`. So the identity is *there* (`userId` is a real id) but the **role is a label**, and the column designed to hold the link is unused |
| **Why the JSON is not good enough** | `"role":"Crisis Manager"` is not `CRISIS_MANAGER`. It is a display string typed by whoever seeded the row, in a JSON array with no CHECK, no FK and no index. Nothing can join it, and a second spelling (`"Crisis Lead"`, `"crisis manager"`) is indistinguishable from a third person. This is exactly what [[F2-113]] had to fix on `committee_referral.recusal_names` — team labels compared against a table of people — and what [[F2-26]] built the roster→user linkage for |
| **What it blocks, concretely** | (a) the crisis-room guard cannot be entity-bound, so it stays a role list — [[F2-52]] recorded that as the reason `BCM:COMMAND` was not invented; (b) `CrisisEventService` cannot notify *the* commander, only a role; (c) a handover between commanders leaves no record; (d) an auditor asking *“who was in command at 02:00”* gets a JSON blob |
| **⚠ What this finding does NOT claim** | That `crisis_manager_id` is dead code — it is a **column with no writer**, which is the [[F2-141]] shape, and the entity may well map it. Nor that the JSON should be deleted: `crisis_team_json` carries the whole team, which no single FK can. The fix is to populate the column **and** give the team array a closed role vocabulary, not to choose between them |
| **Effort** | **M** — the schema change is trivial; deciding whether the commander is a user, a roster seat ([[F2-26]]) or a per-event appointment is not, and the 5 existing rows have no derivable answer |
| **Verification** | `SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(crisis_manager_id) FROM bcm.crisis_events WHERE is_deleted=0` → `5, 0`. `crisis_team_json` non-empty on 5. The five events' team arrays name `userId` 1, 2, 4, 5 and 6 with role strings `Crisis Manager`, `Cards Lead`, `CISO`, `Branch Ops Lead`, `Cloud Lead` — three of which are not role names in `iam.roles` either |
| **References** | `bcm.crisis_events` · `CrisisEventController#activeSummary` · `CrisisEventService` · [[F2-52]] · [[F2-26]] · [[F2-113]] · [[F2-141]] |
| **Lesson** | **A role list in a guard and a role label in a JSON blob fail the same way, and finding one is a reason to look for the other.** F2-52 is a programme about names where identities are needed; this is the same defect on the data side of the same feature, and it is why the guard could not be converted to something better than a role list. **When a guard cannot be entity-bound, ask whether the entity actually carries the identity** — twice now the answer has been "there is a column for it and nobody writes it" ([[F2-112]]'s ids that were never written, and this) |

## F2-149 · `external.properties` overrides placeholders, not properties — so the deployment's only config file cannot change most of the configuration

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-149 *(found 2026-08-06 while answering [[F2-92]]'s owner decision; ADR-017 D-16)* |
| **Category** | Configuration mechanism — a control surface that silently does nothing |
| **Severity** | **P2.** It is why a P0's mitigation was inert for four days and the incident record was wrong about the state of a security control. No data exposure; the cost is that operators cannot change what the runbook says they can, and cannot tell |
| **Current State** | ✔ **FIXED 2026-08-06** for the five runtime kill-switches, both config headers, the template and the runbook, with a CI gate. No migration |
| **The defect** | `external.properties` sits beside the jar and is pulled in by `spring.config.import` in `application.yml`. An imported document sits **below** the file that imports it, and a **profile-specific** document (`application-prod.yml`) outranks `application.yml` and everything it imports. So any property path `application-prod.yml` sets **literally** cannot be overridden from the one file operators are told holds all runtime configuration — with no error and no warning |
| **🔑 Why it went unnoticed for months: 18 of the 19 keys work** | Every other key in the deployed file is a `SCREAMING_SNAKE` **placeholder name** that the yml *consumes* — `${DB_URL}`, `${SERVER_PORT:8443}`, `${FLYWAY_BASELINE_VERSION:0}`. A placeholder is a **reference**, resolved against the whole `Environment`, so it picks up a value at any precedence. Those always worked. **The tell was visible in the file: one line in a different style from the other eighteen, and it was the broken one** |
| **⚠ Two config headers asserted the opposite, in prose, and prose is what the operator acted on** | `application.yml` said *"Values defined there win over the packaged defaults … raw spring keys work too"*; `external.properties.template` said *"Any raw Spring key (e.g. `grc.cors.allowed-origins=...`) — full override"*. Both false for a literal profile key. During a P0 an operator wrote `grc.security.tenant-write-guard.enabled=false` on the strength of those sentences; production kept booting with the guard ON and [[F2-92]] recorded the flag as OFF for four days |
| **The blast radius, measured** | `application-prod.yml` has **57 literal scalar settings and 26 placeholders**. **23 of the literals are booleans**, none overridable — and **five are `grc.*` runtime kill-switches**: the tenant write guard and the vuln-SLA, cyber-CAPA-SLA, cyber-evidence-expiry and workflow-request-SLA schedulers. Those five are exactly what an operator needs to stop during an incident, and none of them could be stopped without rebuilding the jar. The other 18 (Swagger off, `flyway.clean-disabled`, secure cookies) are settled policy and **deliberately stay literal** — making them tunable would be a regression, not a fix |
| **The second half: the template documented a third of the surface** | The packaged yml files read **59** `${SCREAMING_SNAKE}` placeholders; the template named **38** (18 active, 20 as commented examples). **21 were absent**, so an operator could not know they existed — including `SWAGGER_ENABLED`, `FLYWAY_DEFAULT_SCHEMA`, `STORAGE_BASE_PATH` and the four `GRC_DB_SIZE_*` thresholds that gate writes as SQL Server Express approaches its 10 GB cap |
| **⚠ A correction to my own first measurement** | I first reported **36** undocumented knobs. That was wrong: my regex matched `^KEY=` and the template documents 20 keys as **commented examples**, which it read as absent. **A scan that treats a comment as absence is the same defect as one that treats a comment as code**, and it inflated the finding by 15 before I re-measured. The honest figure is 21, of which 5 are the switches this fix introduced |
| **What shipped** | The five switches become `${NAME:true}` — **defaults unchanged, all ON**. Both config headers corrected to state the rule. Template gains all 20 missing knobs, each commented with its default and what changing it costs. `DEPLOYMENT.md` §3 carries the rule in a callout. New `ExternalConfigOverrideContractTest`: no template key may name a Spring property path (hard zero), every placeholder the yml reads must be documented (hard zero), the five kill-switches must stay placeholders **and** be documented, and both headers must still state the rule |
| **⚠ Deploying it changes no behaviour** | `application-prod.yml` still defines the path, so the deployed file's stale `grc.security.tenant-write-guard.enabled=false` remains inert, and `TENANT_WRITE_GUARD_ENABLED` is absent so the default `true` applies. The guard stays on, exactly as it is now. Operator action: **delete** the stale line; use `TENANT_WRITE_GUARD_ENABLED=false` if the switch is ever genuinely wanted |
| **Effort** | **S**, and it was already spent |
| **Verification** | Suite **3,368/0/0** · **5 of 5 mutations caught** (raw path added → fails; documented knob removed → fails; guard reverted to a literal → fails; the import mention stripped → fails; the rule wording stripped → fails). ⚠ **The fourth test was broken when written and only the mutation run found it.** Its first version looked for the OLD wording and skipped the check whenever the corrective paragraph was present — which the corrected files always are, so it could fire only if a reverter also deleted the explanation. Rewritten as a **positive** assertion, which cannot go vacuous. And its first green run was against the previous version of the test, so *"4/4 passed"* was true of code I had already replaced |
| **References** | `application.yml` `spring.config.import` · `application-prod.yml` · `external.properties.template` · `ExternalConfigOverrideContractTest` · `TenantWriteGuardConfig` · `DEPLOYMENT.md` §3 · ADR-017 D-16 · [[F2-92]] · [[F2-62]] |
| **Lesson** | **A switch you cannot observe being thrown is not a control.** Nothing in Spring, the log, or the platform reports an outranked property — the operator threw it, the record said OFF, the guard stayed ON, and four days of analysis were spent on the wrong variable. This is [[F2-62]]'s dead flag and A17-D2's fail-open WARN a third time: **a control whose effect is never asserted will eventually be believed rather than known.** And the specific habit worth keeping: when a config file's keys are written in two different styles, the odd one out is the bug |

## F2-93 · The change record says when the CAB decided and never who

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-93 *(found while building A17-D3, because the control D-3 specifies could not be enforced without it)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` |
| **Severity** | **P2** on its own; **blocking** for A17-D3 |
| **Current State** | `change_mgmt.change_requests` carried `cab_decision_at` and `cab_decision_notes` and **no column naming the decider**. The author of a CAB approval, a CAB rejection or an emergency ECAB approval existed only in `platform.audit_log` |
| **Problem** | Two consequences, one obvious and one not. Obvious: an examiner asking *"who authorised this emergency change?"* is answered from a log rather than from the record — and the log is a different retention class from the register. Not obvious: **ADR-017 D-3's segregation-of-duties test had nothing to compare against.** Retrospective ratification exists to supply the independent scrutiny the emergency path skipped; if the emergency approver may also ratify, the control certifies its own author's decision *and* the evidence pack asserts that a second party reviewed it. That is [[F2-29]] one lifecycle step later |
| **Root Cause** | The decision fields were added as *timestamp + free text*. Provenance was treated as an audit concern, and the audit trail genuinely does hold it — which is exactly why nobody noticed the register did not |
| **Fix** | **V345** adds `cab_decision_by_id` / `cab_decision_by_name`, stamped from the authenticated principal on all three CAB-decision transitions — never from a request body (the [[F2-24]] rule). **Not back-filled from the audit ledger**: the ledger holds usernames, not the identity the SoD test compares, and inferring one from the other manufactures provenance for a decision nobody can now attest to. Legacy rows keep NULL, and `GRANDFATHERED` is what stops that NULL being read as "no conflict" |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed** — V345, `ChangeManagementService#stampDecisionAuthor`, live-probed (`cabDecisionByName=compliance`, `cabDecisionById=3`) |
| **Lesson** | **A control cannot be enforced against a fact the record does not hold.** The SoD rule was decided in ADR-017 nine days before anyone checked whether the comparison it requires was possible. Measuring the *inputs* a decision needs is part of measuring whether the decision is buildable — the amendment pass checked for ratification columns and missed that the approver was equally absent |


## F2-94 · 126 of 164 automation rules notify nobody — the engine reads `to_role`, the rules all write `roles`

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-94 *(found by A17-D3's live probe: the escalation fired, was audited, and reached no one)* |
| **Module** | `platform/automation` (F8 engine) — affects `change_mgmt`, `policy`, `bcm`, `privacy`, `risk`, `sust`, `socops` and every other module that seeded a rule |
| **Severity** | **P1.** The platform's entire notification/escalation fan-out is inert for 77% of its rules |
| **Current State** | `AutomationActionExecutor.handleNotify` resolves the recipient from `action_json.to_role` (ESCALATE) or `action_json.role` (NOTIFY) — **singular string keys**. Every seeded rule from V96 onward writes `"roles": ["CISO","CTO",…]` — **a plural array the executor never reads**. Measured 2026-08-02 in both databases: **dev 126 of 164 NOTIFY/ESCALATE rule codes (153 rows across 3 tenants); prod 125 of 161 (150 rows)**. Only **12** rules — `AR-06/09/10/12/13` and the seven `BCM-T*` — actually deliver |
| **Problem** | The rule fires, the engine records `RULE_FIRED`, `automation_rule_runs` gets a row, `fire_count` increments — and `handleNotify` returns `ESCALATE (no role configured — audit-only)` having sent nothing. Every downstream signal says the automation worked. Among the dead rules: `AR-97` HIGH-risk **emergency change approved → escalate CTO + CISO**, `AR-72` **expired policy → escalate owner**, `AR-73` HIGH-risk policy exception → CRO + General Counsel, `AR-75` attestation declined → manager + DPO, `CHG-TASK-OVERDUE`, `POL-REVIEW-DUE` |
| **How long it was live, and why nobody knew** | Since the seeds were written. **`platform.audit_log` already holds 278 rows in dev and 164 in prod whose message is `has no role configured`** — the engine has been reporting its own inertness continuously and the WARN was never read. Third instance of that exact shape in this programme after [[F2-87]]'s boot WARN and [[F2-92]]'s javadoc |
| **Root Cause** | A comment asserting an agreement nobody measured. The executor's own javadoc says the singular naming *"match[es] the conventional naming in the seed rules"*; measured, it matches **12 of 164** of them. The two halves were authored independently and the prose bridged them |
| **Fix** | **A17-D3 fixed only its own rule** (`CHG-RATIFY-BREACH` → `"to_role":"CISO"`, live-verified as `ESCALATE → CISO (1 delivered)` landing in `so_officer`'s inbox), plus a V345 self-check that THROWs if the key drifts back. The other 125 need their own slice. **The better fix is almost certainly in the executor** — accept `roles[]` as well as `role`/`to_role`: one code change instead of 150 data rows in two databases, it matches what the seed rules plainly intend (multi-role fan-out, which a singular key cannot express), and it stops the next rule authored from the V305 pattern breaking the same way. ⚠ **But switching it on begins delivering ~150 rules' worth of notifications that have never been sent**, to real inboxes, for events already in the backlog. That is an operational decision, not a refactor |
| **Effort** | **S** for the executor change; **S–M** for the notification-volume assessment that must precede it |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** — `AutomationActionExecutor.RECIPIENT_KEYS` publishes a **closed** three-key vocabulary (`roles`, `role`, `to_role`) read by one resolver, and `AutomationRecipientKeyRatchetTest` checks **every migration** against that same constant, so the vocabulary cannot drift open again. `NotificationService.broadcastToRoles` fans out to all addressed roles with recipients de-duplicated, and returns a `RoleFanout` splitting delivered / unheld / **not-a-role**. V346 normalises the one row the code fix could not reach (AR-148) and catalogues `AUTOMATION:READ`. Suite **2,885/0/0**, ratchet mutation-proven, 19/20 live. **127 of 140 dead rule codes → 61**, and the 61 are now *reported* rather than silent |
| **References** | `AutomationActionExecutor#handleNotify` (the `to_role`/`role` reads) · `V96__automation_rules.sql` · `V305__change_policy_automation_seed.sql` · `V345__change_ratification_sla.sql` (the corrected shape + the self-check) · [[F2-52]] · [[F2-62]] |
| **Lesson** | **A fired rule is not a delivered notification, and every metric the engine keeps conflates them.** `fire_count`, `last_fired_at`, `automation_rule_runs` and the `RULE_FIRED` audit row all increment identically whether one person or nobody was notified — so the observable surface is indistinguishable in the two cases. This is [[F2-62]]'s dead control with better telemetry: the numbers went up. **When a subsystem's success metric is "we tried", the only proof of the control is the recipient.** A17-D3 only found it because its live probe asked *who received this*, not *did it fire* |


## F2-95 · The authorisation matrix demands roles on 33 of 36 rows and enforces none of them

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-95 *(found by re-measuring S6's premises before building it)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` (CH-17's authorisation matrix) |
| **Severity** | **P1** |
| **Current State** | `change_mgmt.change_authorisation_matrix.required_roles` is **configured on 33 of 36 rows in dev and prod alike** — `CHANGE_MANAGER`, `CHANGE_MANAGER,CISO`, `CHANGE_MANAGER,CISO,CTO`. It is read in exactly one place: `resolveAuthorisation` does `out.put("requiredRoles", …)`. Its own javadoc concedes it — *"advisory"*. `satisfied` in the same payload is `approves >= required`, **counting votes and ignoring roles entirely** |
| **Problem** | The tenant has configured "a CRITICAL change requires the CISO", the screen displays it beside a `satisfied` flag, and the change approves with no CISO anywhere near it. The API reports a requirement it does not apply, which is worse than not having the field: a reviewer reading `requiredRoles: CISO` next to `satisfied: true` concludes a CISO signed off |
| **Root Cause, and it is the interesting part** | **There is no vocabulary in which the check could be made.** Three disconnected role vocabularies sit on this one decision path: (1) `required_roles` uses **platform role names** (`CISO`); (2) `committee_member_terms.role` uses **governance seat titles** (`Chair`, `Member`, `Secretary`, `Independent Director`); (3) `change_approvals.approver_role` is **free text the voter types** (`CAB Chair`, `Member — Cybersecurity`, `Security`, `Legal`). **No value from (1) appears in (2) or (3)** — so enforcing it literally would refuse almost every CAB approval. ⚠ *Measured 2026-08-03 rather than asserted: **9 of 11** decided changes in dev and **8 of 10** in prod, and the reason is sharper than the vocabulary mismatch — `CHANGE_MANAGER` is required on all 33 rows and **has no active holder in either database**. The seed is internally contradictory too: the approver named "Chief Information Security Officer", capacity "Member — Cybersecurity", holds the platform role `RISK_MANAGER`.* And `CTO`, named on the CRITICAL rows, **is not a row in `iam.roles` at all** ([[F2-52]]'s ghost-role shape, a fourth instance) |
| **The enforceable path — no fourth vocabulary needed** | [[F2-26]] linked committee seats to platform users, so `member_user_id → iam.user_roles` answers "was a CISO present?" in the platform's own authorisation vocabulary. Measured: **6 of 29 ACTIVE voting seats are linked**; `CISO` **is** reachable through a linked seat, `CHANGE_MANAGER` is not held by any linked seat, and `CTO` does not exist |
| **Therefore the fix must distinguish two failures** | *Required role absent* → 409, a decision failure. *Required role unresolvable* (a ghost role, or one no linked seat could ever hold) → a **configuration fault** surfaced in `governance-health`, not a 409. Today every required role except `CISO` is unresolvable, so the fault would fire loudly and block nothing; the moment a tenant links seats it starts biting. Switching enforcement on without that split would refuse every CAB approval on day one — [[F2-92]]'s mistake in a different costume |
| **Effort** | ~~**M**~~ — the split, the resolution and the payload shipped as S6b-i; the enforcement gate is **S** once the two decisions are made |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03 — both halves (S6b-i reported, S6b-ii enforced).** `requiredRoleStates` gives each configured role one of **SATISFIED / MISSING / NO_ACTIVE_HOLDER / NOT_A_ROLE**, resolved through `approver_id → iam.user_roles`; `satisfied` is now the conjunction and cannot read true over an unmet or unverifiable requirement. **The gate refuses `CAB_APPROVED` with a 409 when — and only when — a role is `MISSING`** (checkable here, held by no approver). ⚠ **Owner decisions taken 2026-08-03:** (a) `CTO` **stripped**, not created — **V347** — because it is *our* seed on 6 identical rows in both databases, and creating a role with no persona, no grants and no holder would fabricate an access model (F2-62's dead control); V347 also THROWs if *any* required role is absent from `iam.roles`, which is the invariant that makes the gate expressible. (b) `NO_ACTIVE_HOLDER` / `NOT_A_ROLE` **never block** — a literal gate would have refused **9 of 11** decided changes in dev and **8 of 10** in prod, because `CHANGE_MANAGER` is required on all 33 rows and no active user in either database holds it; refusing there leaves the approver no action that could succeed, which is [[F2-92]]'s shape. `CHANGE_MANAGER` stays on the rows — deleting a requirement the tenant configured would quietly lower a control; the remedy is to grant it. **Not enforced at `EMERGENCY_APPROVED`, deliberately**: an emergency change is authorised fast and *ratified* afterwards (A17-D3), so the after-the-fact check belongs on the ratification path — filed rather than smuggled in. Suite **2,903/0/0**; live **14/14** in both directions. ⚠ *Probe note: F2-96's committee gate fires first, so both of org 1's gate committees must be seated for the role gate to be reachable at all — then unseated, because the empty roster is F2-96's recorded defect* |
| **References** | `ChangeAuthorisationMatrix#requiredRoles` (the "advisory" javadoc) · `ChangeManagementService#resolveAuthorisation` · `CommitteeMemberTerm#memberUserId` · [[F2-26]] · [[F2-52]] · [[F2-62]] |
| **Lesson** | **A field that three subsystems name differently cannot be enforced, and "advisory" is what a developer writes when they discover that mid-implementation.** The word in the javadoc *was* the finding, and nobody followed it up for as long as the column has existed. When a config field's javadoc says *advisory*, *best-effort* or *informational*, treat it as an unfiled defect and check whether the reason is that the check is impossible |


## F2-96 · Both change-authority gates point at committees that can never be quorate — and health said fine

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-96 *(found by re-measuring S6's premises; the same pass that found [[F2-95]])* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` / `admin.committee` |
| **Severity** | **P1** |
| **Current State** | Measured 2026-08-02, **identically in dev and production**: org 1's `CAB` gate maps to committee 13 *IT Steering Committee* and its `ECAB` gate to committee 12 *Executive Committee*. Both are `ACTIVE` and correctly mapped — and **both have zero ACTIVE voting seats**. `GET /api/v1/changes/governance-health` answered **`healthy: true`** |
| **Problem** | A committee with no voting seat can never be quorate: `computeQuorum` sets `quorumMet = 0` whenever `totalVoting == 0` (correctly, since F2-28). So every change referred to the CAB or ECAB was **undecidable**, and every *non-referred* CAB-gated change was being recorded as **CAB approved in the name of a body with no members** — while the governance screen reported the tenant healthy. A17-D2's predicate asked *"is a committee mapped?"*; the screen it feeds claims *"changes routed to this authority can be authorised"* |
| **Root Cause** | The same shape as [[F2-87]] and [[F2-62]]: **the report measured the mapping, not the capability.** A17-D2 correctly added existence and `DISSOLVED` checks — matching what `CommitteeReferralService.refer` enforces — and stopped one clause short of the property that actually decides whether a decision can happen |
| **Fix** | The roster clause is added to `CONFIGURED_PREDICATE` — the **single** definition shared by the report (`GAP_SQL`) and the enforcement path (`CONFIGURED_ONE_SQL` → `requireConfiguredChangeAuthority`) — so the report became honest and the gate began failing closed **in the same edit**. Two predicates would have let them drift apart again, which is the defect. A third fault kind `ROSTER_EMPTY` is returned **before** `COMMITTEE_UNAVAILABLE` (every empty committee also satisfies "a row exists", so the broad test would swallow the narrow one) and its remedy names *appointing members*, saying explicitly that re-mapping the gate is **not** the fix — the mapping is correct and the body is empty |
| **⚠ Deliberate behavioural consequence** | A tenant whose mapped committee has an empty roster now has CAB/ECAB approvals **refused** until members are appointed. That is ADR-017 D-2's accepted trade — fail closed, with a guided onboarding moment naming the screen. **It applies to the deployed demo tenants**: org 1's two gates are both empty in production today, so after the next prod deploy CAB/ECAB approvals there will be refused until voting members are appointed to committees 12 and 13 |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed** — one clause, three faults, `ChangeAuthorityRosterCapabilityTest` (5 tests, including one asserting that exactly **one** definition of "configured" exists), 10/10 live in **both** directions: empty → `healthy:false` + 409 `reason=ROSTER_EMPTY`; seat both gates → `healthy:true` + the approval proceeds; seats then removed so dev is left as found |
| **References** | `ChangeGovernanceHealthService#CONFIGURED_PREDICATE` · `ChangeAuthorityNotConfiguredException#ROSTER_EMPTY` · `CommitteeService#computeQuorum` · [[F2-28]] · [[F2-87]] |
| **Lesson** | **Fail-closed is not one decision, it is a chain, and it is only as strong as the last property nobody thought to check.** A17-D2 asked "is there a committee?"; this asks "can it decide?"; the next question is [[F2-95]]'s "were the right people in the room?" Each looked complete when it shipped. The way to find the next link is to take what the *report* claims and test that claim rather than the query behind it — `healthy: true` was falsifiable in one SQL statement for the eight days since A17-D2 shipped |


## F2-97 · One automation rule addresses a person the engine can only address as a role

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-97 *(surfaced by [[F2-94]]'s ratchet — it is the single rule the closed vocabulary cannot excuse)* |
| **Module** | `platform/automation` (F8) / `admin.committee` action items |
| **Severity** | **P3** |
| **Current State** | `AR-149` — trigger `COMMITTEE_ACTION_CREATED`, action `{"type":"NOTIFY","channels":[…],"template":"CRITICAL_ACTION_ASSIGNED","slaMinutes":60}` — names **no audience at all**, under any key. It is the only NOTIFY/ESCALATE rule in the platform of which that is true |
| **Problem** | The rule's intended recipient is the *assignee of the committee action*, a **per-user** address. `NOTIFY` can only address a **role**, so the rule as written can never deliver: the executor reports `NOTIFY (no audience addressed — audit-only)` and a critical action assignment reaches its owner through no channel |
| **Why it is filed rather than fixed** | The two ways to close it are both wrong on their own. Giving it a role (`COMMITTEE_SECRETARY`, say) fabricates intent — the template says *assigned*, and a secretary is not the assignee. Teaching `NOTIFY` to address a user requires an addressing mode the action vocabulary does not have, which is a design decision about the F8 engine, not a data repair. `NotificationService#notifyUser` already exists for exactly this shape, so the engine work is small — but it is engine work |
| **Effort** | **S** once the addressing mode is decided: a `"user_field"` (or similar) key resolved against the event payload, then `notifyUser` |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Open — P3.** Allow-listed by name in `AutomationRecipientKeyRatchetTest.AUDIENCE_ABSENT_BY_DESIGN`, which asserts the entry still corresponds to a rule that is still audience-less and still cites a finding — so the allowlist cannot outlive what it excuses |
| **References** | `V158__committee_automation_rules.sql` (AR-149) · `AutomationActionExecutor#RECIPIENT_KEYS` · `NotificationService#notifyUser` · [[F2-94]] |
| **Lesson** | **An allowlist entry is a filed defect or it is a silencer.** The ratchet needed one exception; making the entry carry a finding id, and testing that the excused rule is still broken, is what keeps the difference visible. The alternative — inventing a role so the count reached zero — would have made the report green by making the platform wrong |


## F2-98 · The "Demo mode" badge tracks the session, not the substitution

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-98 *(found by S5c while splitting `ChangesPage`; the file being restructured was one of the four)* |
| **Module** | frontend — `pages/change`, `pages/ethics`, `pages/risk`, `pages/reporting` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a signed-in user cannot distinguish fabricated records from their own |
| **Current State** | Four pages hand-roll the demo-data fallback instead of using `useApiResource`. All four call their `applyDemo*()` from **three** places — not signed in, the register came back **empty**, and the API **threw** — and all four disclose it with `{demoMode && <Badge>Demo mode</Badge>}` where `const demoMode = !isAuthenticated()`, a value computed once at render |
| **Problem** | `!isAuthenticated()` is **false** for exactly the two cases a real tenant can hit. A fresh tenant with no changes yet, or any user while the backend is down, is shown a full register of invented records **with no badge**. So the disclosure fires for the case that was already obvious (you are not logged in) and stays silent for the two that are not. Measured live: an empty `200` and a network failure both put **10 fabricated change requests** on screen; the export menu stayed enabled and, when disabled, said *"Sign in to export"* to a user who was signed in — and the file it would have produced is the real, empty register, so screen and export disagree |
| **Why the existing guard missed it** | `demoFallbackContract.test.ts` already asserts *"every page consuming `useApiList`/`useApiResource` surfaces `isDemo`"* — 10 of 10 hook consumers pass. Its trigger is the **hook**. These four pages reimplement the behaviour, so they were never in scope. A guard scoped to a mechanism tolerates every reimplementation of it; the same distinction `BARE_EXPORT_CEILING` → `everyExportHandlerAlsoDemandsARead` turned on |
| **Fix** | Make the flag **state the substitution sets**, exactly as the hook does (`isDemo` starts at `!isAuthenticated()` and is then set by each substitution path), and raise it **inside the applier** so the two cannot drift apart again. ⚠ *"Three lines per page" was my estimate and it was wrong for three of the four:* `EthicsPage` and `RiskRegisterPage` substitute in **child tabs** that receive `demoMode` as a prop, and a parent's single header badge cannot speak for them — the cases list can be the tenant's own while the AML alerts beside it are invented. Each child now discloses locally through a shared `DemoDataNotice`. Building that component also closed a second defect the four hand-rolled sites had already drifted into: **two of them rendered a hard-coded English sentence** in an Arabic-primary product, and both ended *"Sign in for live …"* — precisely the wrong advice for the signed-in reader who needs the notice |
| **Effort** | **S** — the remaining three pages are the same three lines each |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03 — all four pages, no allow-list left.** Live-verified **9/9**: `change` 3/3 (empty `200` · network failure · real rows clear it) in **both languages**, `reporting` badge absent→present, `ethics` cases badge absent→present, `ethics` AML and KYC notices absent on real data and present on substituted, `risk` KRI and appetite the same. Zero console errors throughout. ⚠ **The guard needed rewriting mid-slice and that is the keeper.** Its first draft searched for `set(Demo|IsDemo)…(true)` anywhere in the file — **name**-based, and wrong twice: it reported the correctly-fixed `RiskRegisterPage` as broken (its state is called `showingDemo`) and it passed `EthicsPage` on the strength of its *parent's* setter while both child tabs were still silent. It now brace-matches each `apply*()` body that is actually on a substitution path and requires a `set…(true)` **inside it**, with an anti-vacuity floor — behavioural, not nominal |
| **References** | `demoFallbackContract.test.ts` (widened guard + allow-list) · `lib/useApiResource.ts` (the correct model) · `ExportMenu.disabledReason` · [[F2-42]] (the same "fabricated where the server has an answer" shape) · W2-1b (which pinned the *disclosed* contract deliberately) |
| **Lesson** | **A disclosure bound to the wrong condition is worse than none — it reads as a considered decision.** W2-1b was right that demo substitution is designed, not a defect, and right to pin it rather than delete it; what it could not see is that the flag it pinned was `isDemo` on the hook, while four pages carried a same-named `demoMode` meaning something else. My own first scan called `ChangesPage` *disclosed* because the file **contains** `demoMode` — the presence of a disclosure token is not disclosure of the right event |


## F2-99 · A misspelt JSON key erased a committee's attendance sheet, and answered 200

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-99 *(found by S6c's own live probe — the probe had the bug, and the platform's response to it was the finding)* |
| **Module** | `admin/committee` — `POST /api/admin/committees/meetings/{id}/attendance` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — fails closed and is fully audited, but destroys governance evidence on a typo |
| **Current State** | The endpoint **replaces the whole sheet**: it deletes every attendance row and rebuilds from `req.marks()`. The platform sets `spring.jackson.deserialization.fail-on-unknown-properties: false` — a deliberate forward-compatibility choice — so my probe's `{"attendees": [...]}` deserialised to `marks == null`, the service's `req.marks() == null ? List.of() : …` read that as "nobody attended", and the call answered **200** having deleted a 4-row sheet and recomputed quorum to not-met |
| **Problem** | Attendance is the *only* origin of quorum since F2-28/V319, and quorum is the precondition for approving minutes, which is the precondition for authorising a change or publishing a policy. So a single mistyped key at the top of that chain silently discards the record the whole chain rests on. It took three probe runs to notice, and only because every downstream assertion read `present=0/2` |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a security hole and not silent in the ledger: quorum goes to **not met**, so it fails closed, and `MEETING_ATTENDANCE` records *"Attendance sheet replaced · 4 row(s) → 0"* exactly as F2-28's register item 6 requires. The audit trail was right; the API was too forgiving |
| **Fix** | `@NotNull` on `AttendanceRequest.marks` — **deliberately not `@NotEmpty`**. An empty list is a legitimate assertion ("nobody attended"); per `AttendanceMark`'s own javadoc an unmarked voting seat is *absent* and still counts in the denominator. The distinction that matters is **absent field** (a mistake — now a 400 naming `marks`) versus **empty list** (something the caller meant). The global Jackson setting is left alone: flipping `fail-on-unknown-properties` platform-wide would change every endpoint's contract to fix one of them |
