| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** (no migration). Live 5/5: a good sheet records 4 present · the misspelt key answers **400** with `{"marks": "Field is required"}` · **the existing sheet survives the rejected request** · `[]` still returns 200 with 0 present |
| **References** | `CommitteeDtos.AttendanceRequest` · `CommitteeService#recordAttendance` · `application.yml:108` · [[F2-28]] (which made attendance the sole origin of quorum, and therefore made this matter) |
| **Lesson** | **A replace-the-collection endpoint has to distinguish "you sent nothing" from "you sent an empty set".** With unknown properties ignored — a reasonable platform-wide choice — every such endpoint reads a typo as a deliberate wipe. Worth sweeping the other `List<…>`-replacing request bodies for the same shape; that is a separate slice, and it is filed rather than claimed. ⚠ And the meta-lesson: **my probe was wrong three times before I read the endpoint's own DTO.** `present=0/2` was the platform reporting the truth about a request I had malformed, and I spent two edits looking for the defect in my new code |


## F2-100 · A committee decision reached by voting is announced to nobody; raising the motion pages the secretary

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-100 *(found while measuring F2-89's blast radius for S7a — the two are the same publish site and the same decision)* |
| **Module** | `admin/committee` — `CommitteeResolutionService`, `CommitteeService#approveMinutes` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a statutory disclosure clock and a board-register filing instruction both fire on the wrong event, in both directions |
| **Current State** | The whole module had **exactly one** `publishEvent` for resolution outcomes, in `create()`. `recomputeTally()` — the method that turns counted votes into an outcome — published nothing, and `approveMinutes()` — the act that makes a decision binding — published nothing. So `COMMITTEE_RESOLUTION_<outcome>` fired **only** when a tally was typed in from paper minutes, never when a motion was decided in-platform |
| **Proven from the ledger, not argued** | Resolutions **69 and 70** were created 2026-08-03 09:56:40 and voted to `APPROVED` (2/0/0 and 1/0/0, `tally_source=COMPUTED`). `AR-147` (`COMMITTEE_RESOLUTION_APPROVED` → "file in the board-resolutions register within 48 h") has `last_fired_at` **2026-07-26 14:19:20** — a week earlier. It provably never fired for either. Meanwhile `AR-152` (`COMMITTEE_RESOLUTION_DEFERRED` → "re-table at the next meeting") shows `fire_count = 38` while **zero** resolutions in the database are `DEFERRED`: every one of those 38 fires was on [[F2-89]]'s transient placeholder |
| **Problem** | The behaviour was exactly inverted: **raising a motion notified the secretary to re-table it, and deciding the motion notified nobody.** The provenance-bearing path — [[F2-40]]'s `COMPUTED`, the one with per-member vote rows and recusals — is the path automation cannot see. `AR-146` exists to start a regulator disclosure clock ("so the disclosure clock starts visibly", CMA/SAMA/SDAIA); it cannot start on a decision it is never told about |
| **What it is NOT** | Not the cause of `AR-146`'s 0 fires. The three `regulator_notify = 1` resolutions are all `ASSERTED_LEGACY`, seeded directly on 2026-05-23 and never passed through `create()`, so no event could have fired for them. That is a seeding artefact and saying otherwise would overstate the finding |
| **Fix** | **One publish site**, `publishIfBinding`, guarded by a new `decision_published_at` column (**V349**), called from all three moments a decision can become binding: create, the deciding vote, and minutes approval. Whichever happens last publishes; the other two find it stamped. All three are needed because both orders occur in real data — **19 of 22** resolutions in dev sit on meetings that were already `MINUTES_APPROVED` when the resolution was recorded, so publishing only at minutes approval would have announced nothing for the dominant pattern, the mirror image of the bug. Never throws: an announcement failure must not roll back a recorded vote |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** (**V349**, with [[F2-89]]). Live **23/23** with two real principals and no `PLATFORM_ADMIN`: a motion voted to APPROVED moved **AR-147 5 → 6** — the APPROVED rule firing for an in-platform decision for the first time — while **AR-152 stayed at 38**. A second vote did not re-announce it. Asserted end-to-end in `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` at a new declared step, because the ordering is invisible to a unit test: the votes were cast while the meeting was `IN_PROGRESS`, so minutes approval is the only moment the decision could become binding |
| **References** | `CommitteeResolutionService#publishIfBinding` · `#publishBindingDecisionsForMeeting` · `CommitteeDecisionAuthority` · `V349` · `V158` (AR-146/147/152) · [[F2-89]] · [[F2-40]] · [[F2-90]] |
| **Lesson** | **An event published at creation attests to nothing that has happened yet.** The defect and its inverse were one line apart, and the measurement that separated them was a timestamp comparison: `last_fired_at` earlier than the row it should have fired for. ⚠ And a live probe of an async engine has to *wait*: the F8 executor publishes `afterCommit` in a `REQUIRES_NEW` transaction, so reading `fire_count` once right after the HTTP response reports a rule that did fire as not-fired. My first probe run "failed" on exactly that and I nearly recorded a working control as broken |



## F2-101 · The authorisation matrix demands a CAB and the code lets direct votes stand in

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-101 *(found by S7b, measuring what "the CAB queue" actually means before building an agenda over it)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` — `ChangeManagementService#requireConfiguredChangeAuthority` + the `CAB_APPROVED` transition |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the column that records which body may authorise a change does not decide which body does |
| **Current State** | `change_authorisation_matrix.gate` names the authorising body per (change type × risk): measured **15 CAB + 18 ECAB + 3 AUTO of 36 rows**, identical in dev and prod. The `CAB_APPROVED` transition reads it, and if the gate is CAB/ECAB it checks only that the gate is *configurable* — `gateConfigurationFault` — then, absent a referral, falls through to the **legacy recorded-votes path** (`change_approvals`: ≥ `cabMinApprovals` APPROVE, zero REJECT). Referral is a separate explicit call (`POST /{id}/refer-to-committee`), never a consequence of the transition |
| **Measured** | **8 changes in dev and 8 in prod** are `CAB_APPROVED` or beyond, their matrix row demands **CAB or ECAB**, and they have **no committee referral at all**. And of the changes awaiting a decision right now, **3 of 4 in dev and 3 of 3 in prod** have no referral either |
| **Problem** | "Approved by a quorate committee" and "approved by some people who voted" are not the same governance fact, and the difference is exactly what a SAMA or NCA examiner asks about. The committee path carries an active-roster denominator, COI and SoD recusal, a chair clause, e-signed minutes over a content hash and — since F2-90 — a ledger entry. The direct path carries named approval rows. The matrix column that is supposed to say which one is required is **advisory**, which is the same defect [[F2-95]] found one column to the left in the same table, one slice earlier |
| **What it is NOT** | Not an ungated bypass. The direct path has its own gate (CM-C2: a REJECT on record blocks approval, and `cabMinApprovals` APPROVEs are required), and `requireConfiguredChangeAuthority` still fails closed when the tenant's gate map is broken (A17-D2/F2-96). The `AUTO` rows demand nothing **by design** and are correct. So this is a wrong-authority finding, not a no-authority one |
| **Recommendation — and it is an OWNER decision** | Making `gate=CAB` require a referral is a one-line change and it would **refuse the path by which 8 changes in each database were authorised**, plus every future change in a tenant that does not use committees. That is S6b-ii's shape exactly, and the same three options apply: (a) hard gate; (b) report the divergence and leave the path open; (c) per-tenant opt-in, default off — the V348 pattern. The measurement is what the decision needs and it is now on the record; picking (a) without it would have been the control-refuses-everything mistake for the third time |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Open — measured, filed, and deliberately not enforced in S7b.** S7b's own scope was the agenda; enforcing the gate changes who may authorise a change, which is not a decision to take inside a slice about agenda lines |
| **References** | `ChangeManagementService#requireConfiguredChangeAuthority` · the `CAB_APPROVED` case · `ChangeAuthorisationMatrix#gate` · [[F2-95]] · [[F2-96]] · [[F2-102]] |
| **Lesson** | **A column that names an authority is not an authority.** Two slices running, the same table has produced the same defect: `required_roles` was advisory (F2-95) and `gate` is advisory (F2-101). Worth checking every remaining column of `change_authorisation_matrix` against its enforcement rather than waiting for the third one to surface |


## F2-102 · A committee referral outlives the record it refers to, and nothing notices

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-102 *(found by S7b while defining the CAB queue — it is why the queue has a presence test)* |
| **Module** | `admin/committee` — `committee_referral` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — dev-only debris today, but it is the input an agenda builder trusts |
| **Current State** | Measured 2026-08-03 in dev: the only two `PENDING` referrals, **#7 and #8**, both created by `compliance`, point at `source_entity_id` **101** and **102** — and **no `change_requests` row with either id exists in any organisation**. Deleting a change does not withdraw, delete or flag its referral, and no sweep or report detects the dangling pair. Prod holds zero referrals, so this is dev debris rather than a production data problem |
| **Problem** | The referral table is what the CAB queue is read from. A naive agenda keyed on `status = 'PENDING'` would have listed **two items that do not exist while omitting all three real ones** — wrong in both directions, which is [[F2-96]]'s shape: a confident report that is wrong twice over. Governance artefacts that name non-existent items are worse than an empty agenda, because an empty agenda is visibly empty |
| **Fix (partial, in S7b)** | The queue read requires V350's subject snapshot (`source_code`), so pre-V350 rows — including both orphans — are excluded **for a stated reason rather than by luck**, and `CommitteeReferralGateTest.theQueueExcludesSubjectlessReferrals` pins it with the measurement in the test body. That makes the agenda correct; it does **not** clean up or detect the orphans |
| **Still open** | Nothing withdraws a referral when its source record is deleted, and nothing reports a dangling one. The clean fix needs a direction the boundary has no port for (the committee module cannot ask whether a `change_requests` row exists — ADR-012, and S5a took those imports to zero), so it belongs on the **referring** side: the change module's delete path should withdraw the referral, and a health check should count danglers |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Open** — the agenda is protected; the orphans are not cleaned. Filed rather than swept, because a `DELETE` against governance-routing rows needs the owner's explicit OK (house rule 6) |
| **References** | `CommitteeReferralService#awaitingDecision` · `V350` · [[F2-96]] · [[F2-101]] |
| **Lesson** | **A queue is only as trustworthy as what removes things from it.** Every control in this module reads the referral row — the gate, the recusal list, the decision write-back — and none of them asks whether the thing referred still exists. ⚠ And a practical one: **the orphans are almost certainly my own probe debris from an earlier slice**, which is the argument for cleanup on the referring side rather than a manual delete: a probe that leaves routing rows behind will do it again.


## F2-103 · The CAB approval recorded when it was decided and never who

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-103 *(found by S7c while adding two decision branches next to the existing ones)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` — `ChangeManagementService`, the `CAB_APPROVED` transition |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the platform's central authorisation act had a timestamp and an anonymous approver |
| **Current State** | `stampDecisionAuthor` had exactly **two** call sites: `EMERGENCY_APPROVED` and `CAB_REJECTED`. **Not** `CAB_APPROVED`. So the ordinary CAB approval set `cab_decision_at` and `cab_decision_notes` and left `cab_decision_by_id` / `cab_decision_by_name` NULL |
| **Measured** | **Zero** rows in dev carry `cab_decision_by_id`, and **2** carry `cab_decision_at` without it. The only test asserting the column lives in `EmergencyRatificationTest`. (In prod the columns do not exist yet — V345 is among the pending set) |
| **Problem** | This is [[F2-93]]'s own finding, unfixed on the branch that matters most. F2-93 was raised because "the record says when the CAB decided and never who" and was fixed **while A17-D3 was in flight**, so the author landed on the two paths that slice touched. The SoD check F2-93 existed to enable — comparing the approver against the requester/owner — had nothing to compare against for an approval, which is the only decision where that comparison matters |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a missing column (V345 added both) and not a missing audit trail: the ledger's `TRANSITION` row names the actor. But an audit row and the record are two different reads, and every report, export and board pack reads the record |
| **Fix** | One line — `stampDecisionAuthor(c)` in the `CAB_APPROVED` branch — plus `CabDecisionOutcomesTest.everyDecisionBranchStampsItsAuthor`, which parses the real switch and fails if **any** `DECISION_TARGETS` branch records a decision without its author or without `cab_decision_at`. Mutation-proven: restoring the omission fails that test |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** (no migration — the columns already existed). Live: an ordinary approval now stamps `cab_decision_by_id=3` / `cab_decision_by_name=compliance` where every previous row had NULL |
| **References** | `ChangeManagementService` `CAB_APPROVED` branch · `#stampDecisionAuthor` · `CabDecisionOutcomesTest` · [[F2-93]] · [[F2-90]] |
| **Lesson** | **A fix applied while working on one path stops at the edge of that path.** F2-93 was correct, tested and incomplete, and the incompleteness was invisible because its test lived in the slice that found it. When a fix adds a field that several branches should set, the guard has to be *"every branch of this kind sets it"* — a per-branch assertion cannot fail for the branch nobody wrote it for. Same shape as [[F2-100]], where one publish site was mistaken for the only one needed |


## F2-104 · The audit ledger's structured status columns are used by 4 of 32 transition types

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-104 *(found by S7c's live probe asserting the wrong column, which is how the gap surfaced)* |
| **Module** | `platform/auditlog` — `platform.audit_log.workflow_status_before` / `_after` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — the history is recorded and readable, but only queryable by `LIKE` over prose |
| **Current State** | Measured 2026-08-03 in dev: **18 of 7,701** audit rows populate `workflow_status_after`, and they come from **4 of the 32** entity types that record a `TRANSITION` action (all four are `Bcm*`). Every other transition — including every `ChangeRequest` one — puts the statuses in the free-text `message`: *"Change CHG-2025-0014 status CAB_PENDING → CAB_APPROVED"* |
| **Problem** | The columns exist to make lifecycle history *queryable*. "Show me every change that reached `CAB_APPROVED` last quarter, and who moved it" is a one-line query against the structured columns and a `LIKE '%status%→ CAB_APPROVED%'` against prose — which breaks the moment a message is reworded, and cannot be indexed. The tamper-evident chain (F2-31) hashes the row either way, so nothing is *lost*; it is a findability defect, not an integrity one |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a change-module defect and not caused by S7c: it is platform-wide and pre-dates every slice in this programme. And not a silent one — the information is in the message, so an auditor reading the ledger sees the transition |
| **Recommendation** | Populate the two columns from the transition call sites. Cheap per site, but there are **28 entity types** to touch, so it is a sweep with a ratchet (the `AuditLogService` overload that takes before/after already exists — the four `Bcm*` types use it), not a one-line fix. Sequence it after S7/S8 rather than inside a slice about CAB outcomes |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Open — filed, not fixed.** S7c's probe was repointed to read the message, with a comment saying why, rather than quietly asserting a weaker thing |
| **References** | `platform.audit_log` · `AuditLogService` · [[F2-31]] (the hash chain, which is unaffected) · [[F2-90]] |
| **Lesson** | **A probe that asserts the wrong column finds a real gap.** My assertion read `workflow_status_after`, failed, and the honest answer was neither "my probe is wrong" nor "the code is broken" but "these columns are populated by 4 of 32 producers". ⚠ Also worth noting the shape: a nullable column that *one* module fills in looks like a feature and behaves like a convention

## F2-105 · The post-implementation review card could not save, and that is why the retrospective looked unused

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-105 *(found by S7d's live probe — three legs 400'd on their first run)* |
| **Module** | `tss-grc-ui-v2` — `pages/change/ChangePirCard.tsx` against `PUT /api/v1/changes/{id}` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a whole governance feature was unusable, and its emptiness read as "nobody uses this" |
| **Current State** | ✅ **FIXED 2026-08-04** (S7d). The card now sends `{...change, …pirFields}`, the shape the register's edit form has always used |
| **Problem** | `PUT /api/v1/changes/{id}` binds the `ChangeRequest` **entity** with `@Valid`, and [[F2-30]] put `@NotBlank` on `titleAr` ("Arabic names are backend-mandatory" — asserted in CLAUDE.md and enforced nowhere until then). The server's `apply()` is a deliberate *partial merge* that skips null fields, but `@Valid` runs **before** it, so a body carrying only the four PIR fields is rejected 400 with `errors: {titleAr: "must not be blank"}` and `apply()` is never reached. `PirCard.save()` sent exactly those four fields. So **the PIR card had been unsaveable since F2-30 landed** — every "Save review" click produced a bare validation toast |
| **How it was found** | S7d set out to consume `pir_follow_up`, having measured **zero** populated PIR fields across dev and prod and concluded the field went nowhere. The live probe's `PUT` legs then 400'd, and the sharper truth appeared: the column was not unused, it was **unreachable**. The measurement that opened the slice had been reading the *consequence* of this defect and attributing it to indifference |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a server defect and not a reason to relax the server. Dropping `@Valid` from the PUT, or moving `@NotBlank` off `titleAr`, would reverse F2-30 — bilingual records are a product requirement (house rule 7) and the constraint was measured safe (0 blank rows) before it landed. A partial-merge API with required fields legitimately demands the caller resend them, and the register's edit form always did |
| **Fix** | `ChangePirCard.save()` spreads the record. `status` is safe to resend because `apply()` deliberately never copies it ([[CM-C3]]). Pinned by `pirCardSavePayload.test.ts`, which reads the **entity's own** bean-validation annotations rather than hard-coding `titleAr`, so a future `@NotBlank` on another column cannot quietly break the card again — mutation-proven by removing the spread |
| **Blast radius, measured** | `changeApi.update` has three call sites. The register's edit form sends `{...c}` (`reqFromDto`) and worked; `updateTask` is a different endpoint; the PIR card was the only partial sender. No other module's card was affected — this is a change-module instance of a general trap, not a platform-wide sweep |
| **References** | `ChangePirCard.tsx` · `ChangeManagementController.update` · `ChangeRequest.titleAr` · [[F2-30]] (the constraint that exposed it) · [[F2-98]] (the same module's other honesty defect) |
| **Lesson** | **"Nothing consumes this column" and "nothing can write this column" produce identical data.** Zero populated rows was the finding I built a slice on, and it had two possible causes; I checked the read side and inferred the write side. ⚠ And the general trap: **adding a constraint to an entity that doubles as a partial-update DTO breaks every caller that was legitimately sending a subset** — `@Valid` on a merge endpoint validates the *patch* as if it were the whole record, and there is nothing at the point of the annotation to warn you |

## F2-106 · The evidence subsystem accepts a link to any module name and any evidence id, checking neither

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-106 *(found by S8a while writing the change module's adapter over the peer)* |
| **Module** | `module/evidence` — `EvidenceItemService.addLink` / `EvidenceLinkRequest` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — two distinct defects on one endpoint: an unvalidated vocabulary that surfaces as a 500, and an unvalidated foreign key that writes a cross-tenant reference |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **OPEN.** S8a defends the change module's own side (`EvidenceAdapter` resolves the item in-tenant before linking, and `EvidenceAdapterTest` regression-tests it), but the peer's endpoint is untouched — every other caller, and any future one, is still exposed |
| **Problem (a) — the vocabulary** | `EvidenceLinkRequest.linkedModule` carries `@NotBlank` and nothing else, while the column is guarded by `CK_ev_links_module` (**9** permitted values before V353, 10 after). A caller sending any other string — a typo, a lower-case `risk`, a module that has not joined the subsystem — gets a **500** out of a constraint violation instead of a 400 naming the permitted set. This is the `ck_ca_source` landmine ([[green-finance-rbac-impl-progress]]) in a second place: a closed database vocabulary with no application-layer counterpart |
| **Problem (b) — the tenant** | `evidenceId` carries `@NotNull` and nothing else. `addLink` never confirms the item **exists**, and never confirms it belongs to the caller's organisation, so a link row pointing at another tenant's evidence item persists successfully. The read side filters the *link's* `organisation_id`, so the practical effect is a silently dead reference rather than a disclosure — but "the reference is dangling" and "the reference is to somebody else's evidence" are indistinguishable in the row, and a future read that joins on `evidence_id` without re-filtering would disclose it. Measured: `attached()` in S8a's adapter re-filters the **item** for exactly this reason |
| **How it was found** | Not by probing the evidence module. S8a needed `attach()` to promise "an existing evidence item", so the adapter resolved the id before delegating — and reading `addLink` to decide whether that resolution was redundant showed it was the only place doing it |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a reason to add validation in the adapter *instead* of the service — the adapter's check protects change and nothing else. Not an argument for a `@Pattern` on `linkedModule`: the vocabulary lives in the CHECK and a duplicated regex is the F2-91 shape (a vocabulary stated in N places was wrong in N−1 of them). The right fix reads the permitted set from one place, or an enum the migration is ratcheted against |
| **Fix, proposed** | (1) A `LinkedModule` enum or constant set in `module/evidence`, ratcheted against `CK_ev_links_module` by a test that parses the migration — the same instrument `EvidenceAdapterTest.theModuleStringMatchesTheMigration` uses for one value. (2) `addLink` resolves the evidence item by id **and** organisation, 404 when it does not. (3) A backfill audit: count existing `evidence_links` whose `evidence_id` resolves in a different organisation, or in none — S8a measured **21** links total across 8 modules, so this is a small, checkable set |
| **References** | `EvidenceItemService.addLink` · `EvidenceLinkRequest` · `V353__evidence_link_change_module.sql` · `module/change_adapters/EvidenceAdapter.java` (the defended side) · [[F2-91]] (the same duplicated-vocabulary shape) · [[F2-94]] (a closed vocabulary plus a migration ratchet, as the pattern to copy) |
| **Lesson** | **A database CHECK is not input validation, and the difference is a 500 instead of a 400.** Every closed vocabulary in the schema needs an application-layer counterpart *and* a test binding the two — which is what F2-94 built for escalation recipient keys and what `ck_ca_source` still lacks. Second: **writing an adapter over a peer is a free audit of that peer**, because a port's contract forces you to ask what the far side actually guarantees |

## F2-107 · The change success rate divided by the wrong denominator and rendered 100%

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-107 *(found by S8b's CE-12 feasibility census, before any code was written)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` — `ChangeManagementService.analytics()` → `successRatePct`, on the change dashboard and the KPI strip |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a headline governance figure reading 100% when the population it measures is almost empty. Not a crash; a false assurance |
| **Current State** | ✅ **FIXED 2026-08-04** (S8b). Denominator is now changes that reached a terminal status, and the response carries `successRateDenominator` + `successRateOutcomesRecorded` beside it |
| **Problem** | `successRatePct = SUCCESS ÷ (rows that have an outcome)`. Measured: dev holds **17** live changes of which **2** carry an outcome, both `SUCCESS` — so the dashboard rendered **100.0%**. Prod: 15 changes, 3 outcomes, 2 SUCCESS → 66.7%. The denominator silently excluded every change that reached an end and never had its outcome recorded, which is exactly the population a success rate is supposed to hold to account. Over changes that actually reached a terminal status the figures are **66.7%** (dev) and **50.0%** (prod) |
| **How it was found** | Not by looking at the metric. S8b set out to build §12's KPI pack and began by measuring whether each metric's source columns were populated — the S7f discipline applied before writing code rather than after. `outcome` at 2 of 17 made the existing rate's denominator obviously wrong on the way past |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a data-quality issue to be fixed by backfilling outcomes — inventing outcomes for 15 changes would be far worse. Not solved by hiding the metric either: the honest rate is *lower*, and a governance platform that only shows flattering numbers when the data is thin has the same defect pointed the other way |
| **Fix** | Denominator = `IMPLEMENTED + ROLLED_BACK + CLOSED` (published as `ChangeKpiService.TERMINAL_STATUSES` and pinned by name in a test). The key is unchanged so the frontend keeps working, and two new keys expose the population. The full §12 pack reports the same rate as a `ChangeKpi` carrying `basis`, `denominator` and `population`, so a thin denominator is visible rather than inferable |
| **Blast radius, measured** | One key, three call sites in the frontend. ⚠ The *pattern* is wider and worth a sweep: any `x ÷ (rows where x is recorded)` has this shape. [[F2-42]] was the same defect in a board pack, and this register's own §12 spec lists twelve more bare percentages that would have inherited it had the KPI pack been built as specified |
| **References** | `ChangeManagementService.analytics()` · `ChangeKpiService` · `ChangeKpi` · [[F2-42]] (fabricated aggregate) · S7f (the export-provenance precedent) |
| **Lesson** | **A percentage without its denominator is not a measurement, it is a mood.** The fix that matters is structural: the KPI type now makes it impossible to emit a rate without saying what it was computed over, and impossible to emit `0%` where the truthful answer is "nothing has happened yet". ⚠ And my own first drafts of two new metrics made the same mistake in the same hour — "0 conditions overdue" over 0 conditions, "0 terms expiring" over 0 terms — both caught by the live probe, not by review |

## F2-109 · Nothing generates `change_code`, so every change a user creates is unciteable

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-109 *(found by S8d while building the decision register over real rows)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` — `ChangeManagementService.create`; consequences in `module/reporting` (change register, CAB pack, decision register) |
| **Severity** | **P3** — no data loss and no wrong answer, but a governance record nobody can cite by reference. ISO 20000 documented information is expected to carry an identifier |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **OPEN.** S8d ships a mitigation, not a fix: `DecisionRow.reference()` falls back to `#<id>`, the platform's real identifier, so the decision register can always cite a row. The other change exports still print the blank |
| **Problem** | `change_code` is the human identifier every change artefact is keyed on — `CHG-2025-0001` in the register, the CAB pack title, the minutes. **No code path assigns it.** The only `setChangeCode` call in all of `module/` is a merge of a client-supplied value inside the update path, so a change created through `POST /api/v1/changes` — including from the UI's own intake flow — carries `NULL` for its whole life, and every export prints an empty Code cell for it |
| **How it was found** | The CE-14b probe created a `[REVIEW-TEST]` change through the API and then looked for it in the served register by its code. `change_code` came back `None`. The first reaction was that the probe had read the wrong column; grepping the module showed there is no writer |
| **Why both databases look clean** | Dev shows **1 of 18** live changes without a code and prod **0 of 15** — but that one was the probe's own row. Every other row was written by `seed_real_data.py` or by the W1-5 integration test, both of which set the code explicitly. **The gap is invisible in the data because the create path has never been exercised in either database.** Same shape as F2-105, where "nothing consumed `pir_follow_up`" turned out to be the shallower half of "nothing could write it either" |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a uniqueness or collision bug — there is no generator to collide. Not a display bug: the column is genuinely empty. And not fixed by the S8d fallback, which only rescues one of the four artefacts |
| **Fix shape** | A per-tenant sequence (`CHG-<year>-<nnnn>`, scoped by `organisation_id`) assigned on create, with a unique index, plus a decision about the rows already holding NULL: backfill, or leave and let `#<id>` stand. Both need the owner's call, and a backfill is a data change, so it is not a slice to take unilaterally |
| **Evidence** | `grep -rn "setChangeCode" module/` → exactly one hit, `ChangeManagementService:1482`, inside the field-merge helper · probe leg `F2-109` in `ce14b_probe.py` asserts the created row has **no** code, so the finding cannot quietly close itself · `DecisionRow.reference()` javadoc records it at the point of use |

## F2-108 · The module boundary ratchet inspects imports, so native SQL crosses it unseen

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-108 *(found by S8b while deciding where the CAB throughput query belonged)* |
| **Module** | `security` — `ChangeModuleBoundaryTest`; instances in `change_mgmt/service/ChangeGovernanceHealthService` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — no live defect. A ratchet narrower than the rule it is believed to enforce, which is how a boundary erodes while CI stays green |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **OPEN.** Named in the javadoc of the query that relies on it, so the next reader is not misled about what the ratchet proves |
| **Problem** | ADR-012's rule is that `module/change**` may not depend on another `module.<peer>`, and `ChangeModuleBoundaryTest` enforces it by scanning **import statements**. `ChangeGovernanceHealthService` reaches `admin.committee_gate_map`, `admin.committee_meetings`, `admin.committee_resolutions`, `admin.committee_meeting_attendee` and `admin.committee_member_terms` through `EntityManager.createNativeQuery` — no import, no failure, full coupling to the peer's **table and column names**, which are a less stable contract than its Java types. A column rename in `admin` breaks `change_mgmt` at runtime with nothing in CI objecting |
| **How it was found** | S8b needed CAB throughput for §12. The clean-looking option was a new `CommitteePort` method; reading the existing code showed A17-D2 and F2-96 had already answered the same class of question with native SQL from inside `change_mgmt`, so adding a port would have given the platform two mechanisms for one concern — the thing `CommitteePort`'s own javadoc warns against. Following the established mechanism was the right call *and* exposed that the ratchet never saw it |
| **What it is NOT** | Not an argument for ripping out the native SQL now — that would reverse A17-D2's shipped design mid-programme for no functional gain, and the interpolated `CONFIGURED_PREDICATE` exists precisely so the report and the enforcement path cannot drift. Also not a claim that native SQL is wrong: the single-statement aggregate is the right tool. The defect is that the **ratchet's name over-promises** |
| **Fix, proposed** | Extend `ChangeModuleBoundaryTest` to scan string literals in `module/change**` for `<peer_schema>.` table prefixes, with an explicit allow-list carrying a stated reason per entry — the shape `EXPORT_WIDER_THAN_READ` uses in `ExportAuthorizationRatchetTest`. That converts an invisible dependency into a reviewed one, which is the whole distinction CLAUDE.md draws between a ceiling and an invariant |
| **References** | `ChangeModuleBoundaryTest` · `ChangeGovernanceHealthService.CAB_THROUGHPUT_SQL` / `CONFIGURED_PREDICATE` · `CommitteePort` (the javadoc that reserves quorum) · ADR-012 |
| **Lesson** | **A ratchet enforces its implementation, not its title.** "The change module has no peer dependencies" was true of imports and false of SQL, and the gap was invisible precisely because the test passed. Worth asking of every ratchet on this platform: *what representation of the rule does it actually read?* |

## F2-110 · The meeting agenda is written in two key shapes, and each reader handled only one

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-110 *(found by CE-13 while pointing the CAB workspace at a sitting's agenda)* |
| **Module** | `admin.committee` — `CommitteeCalendarService.rowOf` vs the seeded agendas + `reporting` — `ExportService.agendaItemText` + `tss-grc-ui-v2` — `CommitteesPage` agenda list |
| **Severity** | **P2** — no data loss, and a blank page in two governance artefacts: the on-screen agenda for 24 of 34 dev meetings, and the circulated agenda pack for every agenda the platform builds |
| **Current State** | ✅ **FIXED in CE-13.** One reader (`CommitteeAgendaJson`) accepting either shape, used by the export and the adapter; `agendaTopic()` on the frontend; `CommitteeAgendaVocabularyTest` pins both halves |
| **Problem** | An agenda item's title is written under `topicEn`/`topicAr` by `rowOf` — the documented V40 convention — and under `titleEn`/`titleAr` by the seeder that created the twelve real committees' meetings. Nothing wrote both. The frontend agenda list read `topicEn` only, so it rendered an **ordered list of empty bullets** for the seeded ones; the CE-14a agenda-pack PDF read `titleEn` only, so it printed `—` for the built ones. Two more keys ran the same way in the same table: `isMandatory` vs `mandatory` (every built item printed "Standing") and `minutesAllocated` vs `minutes` (every built item's time column printed `—`) |
| **The measurement** | All 34 dev meetings: **24** agendas keyed `titleEn` — every one of the twelve real committees, in **both** quarters, including the CAB's own next sitting `MTG-IT-26Q3` — and **8** keyed `topicEn` (the older DEMO meetings), plus 2 with no agenda at all. Zero carried both keys |
| **Why the S7f probe passed** | It exported a **seeded** meeting, which is the shape that export could read. The failing population is the one `populateAgenda` produces — that is, precisely the agendas S7b taught to carry the CAB queue. A control verified against the one input it handles is not verified |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a data-migration job. Rewriting 24 rows of JSON would fix only the rows that exist today and the next seeder or import would reintroduce the shape. `topicEn` stays canonical **for writers**; readers tolerate either. Nor is it cosmetic: an agenda pack with blank item titles reads as a meeting with nothing to discuss |
| **Lesson** | **A reader that knows one shape of its own data is a control that fails silently on the other half.** Two independent readers, each correct against a different population, produced a platform where 100% of agendas were unreadable *somewhere*. When a structure is written by more than one producer, the reader is the place to reconcile them — and the reconciliation belongs in one function, not in each caller |

## F2-111 · A charter standing item counted as a tabled change, because a module tag is not a reference

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-111 *(found in the browser, on CE-13's own first screen)* |
| **Module** | `admin.committee` — `CommitteeCalendarService.rowOf` vs the seeded agendas — the agenda builder's item shape; consumed by `CommitteePort.Sitting` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — a wrong count on one governance screen, in the flattering direction |
| **Current State** | ✅ **FIXED in CE-13.** Referral lines now carry a `decisionRef` field naming the record they table, and `CommitteeAgendaJson.tabledRefs` reads that instead of counting module tags. Pre-CE-13 lines fall back to their title so nothing already tabled reads as untabled |
| **Problem** | "Is this change on the next sitting's agenda?" was answered by counting agenda items whose `sourceModule` was `CHANGE`. But a **charter template's standing item** carries a source module too — dev's CAB agenda holds *Change-management major changes* — so the CAB workspace's header read **"Change items tabled: 1"** on a sitting with no change tabled at all |
| **How it was found** | Reading the finished screen in the browser against the database, not from a failing test. The per-change verdict was already right (it matched on the change's reference), so every unit test passed; only the gate-level header overcounted |
| **The rejected alternative** | Matching the `For decision: ` prefix on the item title. That prefix is display copy in two languages and would stop matching the first time anyone reworded it — the same class of fragility as keying a test on a comment |
| **Lesson** | **A tag says what a row is about; only a reference says which record it is.** The distinction is invisible while the only producer of tagged rows is the one you are counting — and a second producer arriving later turns an exact count into an approximate one with no error anywhere |

## F2-112 · The change module's self-review refusal has never been able to fire

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-112 *(found by CE-15 while measuring whether its two remaining SoD gates had anything to compare)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` — `ChangeManagementService.assertNotSelfWaiver`; root cause in `create` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a documented, tested, shipped separation-of-duties control that cannot refuse anything. No incident is known to have exploited it; the defect is that the platform reports a control it does not have |
| **Current State** | 🟡 **ROOT CAUSE FIXED, POPULATION NOT.** `create` now captures the principal's id, so every change raised from CE-15 onward is checkable, and `GET /api/v1/changes/sod-coverage` publishes the gap. The 17 existing changes still carry NULL ids and team labels — backfilling is a data change and the owner's call |
| **Problem** | `assertNotSelfWaiver` refuses a change's requester or owner from waiving a condition attached to its own approval. It is written correctly — prefer user ids, fall back to case-insensitive names. But **nothing ever wrote the ids**: `create` never captured the authenticated principal, so `requested_by_id` and `change_owner_id` are NULL on **17 of 17** dev changes. The rule therefore always took its name branch and compared a **username** (`admin`) against the change's people labels — which are **teams**: 0 of 13 requester labels and 0 of 15 owner labels resolve to any user in `iam.users` (`'CloudOps'`, `'IAM Team'`, `'CIO Office'`, `'SRE'`). A comparison that cannot match is not a weak control; it is an absent one that reports as present |
| **How it was found** | Not by a failing test — its tests were green throughout, because they prove the *rule* and nothing asked whether the rule could *run*. It surfaced while measuring CE-15's two unbuilt gates: both compare people, so the first question was "what identity do these fields carry?" The answer for the new gates (`implementer_name` has no id column and 0 of 4 values resolve to a user) was also the answer for the shipped one |
| **What it is NOT** | Not an argument for storing only users as a change's people — `requested_by_name` legitimately records a team, and tenants use it that way. The defect is that no **id** was captured alongside. And not a reason to refuse on an unverifiable comparison: 17 of 17 changes would be trapped, the mistake S6c avoided for `quorum_requires_chair` and S8e for `requires_model` |
| **Fix, in two halves** | **(a) Shipped in CE-15**: `create` captures the raiser's id (the name is left exactly as typed), the comparison moved to one place (`ChangeSoD`) with a three-valued answer, and `GET /api/v1/changes/sod-coverage` reports `enabledButBlind` per gate — the instrument that makes this class of defect visible instead of invisible. **(b) The owner's call**: backfilling the 17 rows, for which there is no correct mapping from `'CloudOps'` to a user. F2-109 took the same position on `change_code` |
| **Evidence** | Live, 2026-08-05: `GET /api/v1/changes/sod-coverage` answered `population=17 checkable=0 blind=true` for the waiver gate — enabled, and unable to decide a single record. After the probe created one change through the API the same gate read `checkable=1 blind=false`, and after cleanup it read blind again · `ChangeSoDGateTest` pins the three-valued rule and the coverage shape, with the blind case asserted by name |
| **Lesson** | **A green test proves a rule is right; only a measurement proves it can run.** Every guard on this platform is worth the same question — *what does it compare, and does that data exist?* The three ratchets in `ControllerAuthorizationRatchetTest` / `MassAssignmentRatchetTest` / `RoleGuardConversionRatchetTest` count guards; none of them asks whether a guard's inputs are populated, which is how a correct control sat inert for months with CI green the whole time |

## F2-113 · The committee's force-recusal compares a team label to a person's name

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-113 *(found while fixing the fail-closed CAB gates — the third instance of F2-112's shape, one layer up)* |
| **Module** | `admin/committee` — `CommitteeResolutionService.recordVote`; source in `ChangeManagementService.referToCommittee` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — Track D's separation-of-duties bar on the deciding committee vote cannot engage. Same class as F2-112: a control that reports as present |
| **Current State** | 🟡 **GATE ARMED, POPULATION NOT — fixed `14f62988`, V358.** The recusal now resolves on `committee_member_terms.member_user_id` against a `recusal_user_ids` CSV the referral carries, with the name comparison kept as the fallback for the 29 of 42 active seats that have no user id. **Live-proven 28/28:** `so_officer` (user 8) raised a change labelled `'CloudOps'`, referred it to the CAB the gate map resolves, and their own `FOR` came back **RECUSED** — in a scenario where **0** seat names match **any** carried label, so the pre-V358 basis provably could not have refused it; `rmanager`'s `FOR` on the same motion counted (tally 1, APPROVED). The comparison is `ChangeSoD`'s, not a fourth copy. ⚠ **Still amber for the same reason as F2-112:** the reach is now published rather than assumed — `GET /api/v1/changes/sod-coverage` gained a `COMMITTEE_VOTE_NOT_REQUESTER` row, and on dev it reads **4 of 4 live referrals `absent`** (nothing recorded to bar anybody) because those referrals predate the fix. Every change referred from here on is checkable; backfilling the four is a data change and the owner's call (V357's position, and V358 writes no attribution) |
| **Problem** | A change referred to its CAB carries a CSV of names to force-RECUSE, so the requester/owner cannot cast the deciding vote. `referToCommittee` builds it from `getRequestedByName()` + `getChangeOwnerName()` — and `CommitteeResolutionService.recordVote` matches it with `sameName(member, nm)` against the committee member's `member_name`. But a change's people are **team labels** (`'CloudOps'`, `'IAM Team'`, `'AppSec'`) while committee members are **people** (`'Khalid Al-Rashidi'`, `'Noura Al-Zahrani'`). The comparison cannot match, so the bar never engages for any change in either database |
| **Why it is the same defect as F2-112** | Identical mechanism, different layer. F2-112 was `assertNotSelfWaiver` comparing a username to a team label; this is the committee vote comparing a member name to a team label. Both are correct rules over data that cannot satisfy them. Three instances now — the waiver, the PIR/condition pair CE-15 built, and this — which says the platform's habit is to model *people* as free text and then write controls that compare people |
| **⚠ Why now** | It was unfixable this morning and is not any more. **(a)** CE-15 made `ChangeManagementService.create` capture `requested_by_id`, so a change now knows who raised it. **(b)** The roster fix seated the CAB and ECAB with `committee_member_terms.member_user_id` populated — the first six linked seats in dev, against 37 unlinked ones. So both sides of an id-based recusal exist for the first time. The fix is to recuse on **id** and keep the name match as the legacy fallback, exactly the shape `ChangeSoD` already encodes |
| **Fix** | Pass the change's `requested_by_id`/`change_owner_id` through `CommitteePort.refer` alongside the names; recuse when the member's `committee_member_terms.member_user_id` matches either. Keep the name comparison for seats and changes that carry no id — same three-valued rule as `ChangeSoD`, and the same reason: it must not stop refusing anything it refuses today. Then report the uncheckable population, the way `GET /api/v1/changes/sod-coverage` does for the other three gates |
| **Evidence** | `CommitteeResolutionService.recordVote` lines 246–253, read 2026-08-05 · dev: 15 of 15 change owner labels and 13 of 13 requester labels resolve to no user; the 6 CAB/ECAB seats are linked, the other 37 roster rows are not · the referral path was unreachable in dev before today because both gates failed closed, which is why this sat undetected |
| **Lesson** | Fixing one instance of a defect class is the moment to grep for the others. F2-112 was found by asking "what does this compare, and does that data exist?" — the same question asked of the committee vote answers immediately. ⚠ **And the fix earned a second lesson the hard way:** the first draft added a new comparison method to `ChangeSoD` (`compareUnlinkedActor`, "ids decide when BOTH sides carry one") with a javadoc explaining why it had to differ from `compare`. A mutation test proved it **indistinguishable** from `compare` for every party shape the caller produces — because the caller builds one party per *bar*, so no party ever carries an id and a name at once. It was a second copy of one rule asserting a difference it did not have: the very defect class F2-112 and F2-113 are instances of, introduced by the fix for them. Deleted. **A mutation that changes no test is not proof the test is weak — it can be proof the code is redundant** |

## F2-114 · A paid module has no workspace, so generated navigation would give it no screen

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-114 *(found authoring F2-01's registry — the field §6d insisted on carrying "from day one" is exactly the field that had a hole)* |
| **Module** | `ndi` — sold under the `DATA_AI_GOV` SKU |
| **Severity** | **P2** — latent today, becomes a missing screen for a paid module the moment Wave 4 generates navigation from the registry |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN.** Recorded in `module-registry.yaml` as `workspace: null` with a `workspace_note`, and `ModuleRegistryContractTest` refuses any *other* SKU'd package that tries the same thing silently |
| **Problem** | `TSS-GRC-Platform-Bible/UX/UX_REDESIGN_BLUEPRINT.md` enumerates 13 workspaces and assigns 42 of the 59 backend packages to them. `ndi` is not among them — yet it is the platform's fourth-largest package (7.7 kLoC, 15 tables) and ADR-018 sells it under `DATA_AI_GOV`. Seventeen packages have no workspace and sixteen of those are correct: Base/kernel packages have no screen of their own, and `change_adapters` is glue. `ndi` is the one paid module with nowhere to render |
| **Why it matters later, not now** | Navigation is currently a hand-maintained 184-entry sidebar, and `ndi` appears in it. Wave 4 (F2-07c) retires that sidebar and **generates** navigation from this registry plus entitlements. At that moment a null workspace stops being a documentation gap and becomes a tenant paying for `DATA_AI_GOV` with no route to it. This is the same mechanism §6d caught for Change Enablement — a 12-workspace blueprint would have re-filed it under "Governance & Administration" and silently reversed CE-0 — except that one was caught by a human reading a table, and this one is now caught by a test |
| **⚠ Why it is filed rather than fixed** | Assigning a workspace is an information-architecture decision about where a user goes to find data-and-AI governance, and the honest options differ materially: a **14th workspace** of its own (it is large enough), folded into **12 Governance & Administration** (where `ai` already sits, and `DATA_AI_GOV` sells the two together), or into **3 Compliance & Policy**. Picking one in a data file to make a test pass would be inventing the answer — the F2-14 anti-pattern §6d names in the same bullet that demanded the field |
| **Fix** | An owner or UX decision, then one line in `module-registry.yaml`. `ModuleRegistryContractTest` already fails any SKU'd package whose workspace is null without a stated reason, so the gap cannot recur unnoticed |
| **Evidence** | `TSS-GRC-Platform-Bible/UX/UX_REDESIGN_BLUEPRINT.md` §"12 workspaces" table, read 2026-08-05: workspaces 2–13 name 42 packages, `ndi` absent · `module-registry.yaml` records 17 nulls, of which 16 are Base or adapters · ADR-018 §3 homes `ndi` to `DATA_AI_GOV` alongside `ai` |
| **Lesson** | The field a review insists on "from day one" is worth carrying precisely because writing it down is what finds the hole. Had the registry omitted `workspace:` as undecided, this would have surfaced during Wave 4 as a missing screen instead of during Wave 1 as a row in a register |

## F2-115 · Material outsourcing could go live unnotified — the rule was in the javadoc and nowhere else

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-115 *(found writing F2-03's test floor for `outsourcing` — the floor could not be written honestly until this was fixed)* |
| **Module** | `outsourcing` — SAMA outsourcing governance, sold under the `TPRM` SKU |
| **Severity** | **P1** — a Saudi bank could take material outsourcing live with no SAMA notification, in a module whose purpose is proving it did not |
| **Current State** | 🟡 **The materiality gate is CLOSED 2026-08-05. The 60-day deadline is still absent — see the deferred half** |
| **Problem** | `OutsourcingArrangement`'s class javadoc has stated since the module shipped: *"Material outsourcing must transition through `SAMA_NOTIFIED` within 60 calendar days of `BOARD_APPROVED`."* Nothing enforced any of it. The only gate on the `LIVE` transition keyed on `isCoreActivity`, which **defaults to `false`** — so a MATERIAL arrangement took the `BOARD_APPROVED → LIVE` edge with no notification and no error |
| **Measured** | dev, 2026-08-05: **7 MATERIAL non-core arrangements are LIVE**, one with no `sama_notified_at` at all; 5 MATERIAL core-activity are LIVE and 3 of those carry no non-objection reference either (seeded rows, inserted below the service). 20 arrangements total |
| **Why it is the F2-112 shape** | A documented control with no code, which no test could see because the module had **zero** tests — one of seven regulator-facing modules with none, out of 331 test files. Same family: the rule is written down, a reader believes it, and nothing can enforce it |
| **⚠ Why it had to be fixed before the test floor, not after** | F2-03 asks the floor to cover *every state transition*. Written against the code as it stood, that floor would have asserted `BOARD_APPROVED → LIVE` succeeds for a MATERIAL arrangement — **pinning the defect as correct behaviour inside a ratchet**, which is worse than no test at all. The gate came first; the floor tests the corrected machine |
| **Fix** | `OutsourcingService.transitionArrangement` refuses `LIVE` when materiality is MATERIAL and `sama_notified_at` is null. ⚠ The condition is deliberately **the stamp, not a supplied reference**: a non-objection ref typed into the same request that flips the record LIVE is caller-authored truth (RC-E), the shape F2-93/F2-103 closed elsewhere. `ARRANGEMENT_ALLOWED` keeps its `BOARD_APPROVED → LIVE` edge, because that path is correct for non-material work — a materiality rule belongs in a gate, not in the edge set |
| **Deferred half, stated rather than quietly dropped** | The **60-day** clock is not built. Nothing computes a notification deadline, nothing reports one overdue, and there is no column for it. That is a clock plus a scheduler plus a KPI — the A17-D3 shape — not a conditional, and it needs the same treatment: a due date that is NULL when the start moment is missing, or it reports every arrangement compliant forever |
| **Verified** | Unit: `OutsourcingLifecycleTest` — 11 checks, **8/8 mutations caught**, including one that weakens the gate back to accepting an inline reference. Live: **19/19** over real HTTP — 409 `ProblemDetail` naming the rule, the record unmutated by the refusal, LIVE succeeding after a genuine notification, NON_MATERIAL still direct, and an undeclared transition still refused |
| **Lesson** | A class javadoc is a specification that no build step reads. When one states a rule in the imperative — *must*, *may only*, *within N days* — that sentence is a test case, and its absence from the code is a finding waiting to be written |

## F2-116 · Half of the approval authority for material outsourcing does not exist

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-116 *(measured writing F2-03's authorisation-branch coverage for `outsourcing`)* |
| **Module** | `outsourcing` — `OutsourcingController` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the officers the guard was written for cannot approve anything, silently and permanently. Not an exposure; an unusable control |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN.** Pinned by `OutsourcingLifecycleTest` so a silent edit to any of the three guards fails the build |
| **Problem** | Every state transition in the SAMA outsourcing register is gated by `APPROVE_GUARD` = `hasAnyRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN','RISK_MANAGER','CRO','CIO')`. Measured against `iam.roles` (58 roles, dev, 2026-08-05): **`CRO` and `CIO` do not exist.** So the effective approval authority is `PLATFORM_ADMIN` and `RISK_MANAGER` — the Chief Risk Officer and CIO the guard names cannot approve material outsourcing, and never could |
| **Why it fails silently** | `hasAnyRole` gets **no** PLATFORM_ADMIN bypass and validates nothing against the role table, so a name that does not exist simply never matches. There is no error, no log line and no startup warning — the guard reads as a four-way authority and behaves as a two-way one. RC-C, and F2-52's measured ~50% miss rate across 647 guards, landing in a module that sells as regulatory evidence |
| **Also affected** | `WRITE_GUARD` names `CIO` too, so its four become three. `READ_GUARD` names both, so its seven become five |
| **⚠ Why it is filed and not fixed** | Two remedies exist and they are not equivalent. **(a)** Create `CRO`/`CIO` as roles and grant them — a governance decision about who holds authority in a customer's organisation, which is exactly what ADR-018/F2-56 treat as owner territory. **(b)** Convert the guards to `hasPermission` under F2-05, which is the ratified direction and would make the names irrelevant. Inventing two privileged roles in a data file to make a guard true would be the F2-14 anti-pattern, and inventing them here would pre-empt F2-05's design |
| **Fix** | Fold into **F2-05** for this module: convert the three constants to `hasPermission` with a `TPRM:APPROVE` permission, grant to the roles that actually exist, and delete the fictional names. Until then the pin records the truth |
| **Verified** | `OutsourcingLifecycleTest` asserts the three guard constants verbatim and records `CRO`/`CIO` as recited. A mutation that widens `APPROVE_GUARD` by one role is caught |
| **Lesson** | A guard listing four roles looks twice as strong as one listing two. Reading role names as authority, without joining them to the role table, is how this platform accumulated 537 of these |

## F2-117 · The NCA ECC filing's four-eyes assurance chain is caller-asserted text

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-117 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `nca_ecc` — the fourth instance of F2-112's shape, and the second found by asking the same question of a different module)* |
| **Module** | `nca_ecc` — the annual NCA ECC filing, sold under the `COMPLIANCE` SKU |
| **Severity** | **P1** — a filing submitted to the National Cybersecurity Authority records a peer review and a CISO approval that the platform never verified, and cannot |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN**, with a live tripwire: `NcaEccFilingLifecycleTest` fails the build if the service starts reading a principal, so the fix cannot land without closing this row |
| **Problem** | The filing's assurance chain is `drafted → peer-reviewed → CISO-approved`. `peer_reviewer` and `ciso_approver` are **plain strings taken from the request body**; the service consults no security context and compares the two to nothing. One caller can drive `DRAFT → PEER_REVIEW → CISO_APPROVAL → SUBMITTED` while typing both names — so the filing that reaches the regulator evidences a four-eyes review that may never have happened |
| **Proven live, not read** | The F2-03(b) probe walked exactly that path and submitted a filing whose `peer_reviewer` and `ciso_approver` are **the same person**. 28/28 legs passed, including this one: the platform accepted it without complaint. The probe asked for the self-review; the platform is what allowed it |
| **⚠ Why the comparison was NOT built in this slice** | Because building it would knowingly reproduce **F2-112** — a shipped, tested, documented refusal that could never fire. Measured in dev: **0 of the 4 people-labels** across `drafted_by`/`peer_reviewer`/`ciso_approver` resolve to a platform user (they are display names — *Mohammed Al-Faraj*, *Sara Al-Anezi* — plus an email address in `drafted_by`). A gate comparing those strings would answer "cannot tell" on every filing in the database and refuse nothing, while reporting as a control that exists. That is the exact defect this programme has now closed three times |
| **Fix — V357's shape, in this order** | **(1)** A migration adding `peer_reviewer_user_id` and `ciso_approver_user_id` to `nca_ecc.filings`, with **no backfill** — there is no correct mapping from *Sara Al-Anezi* to a user id, and inventing one would fabricate an assurance record. **(2)** Principal capture on the transitions that assert review and approval, per F2-93/F2-103's rule: the acting principal wins, the body is honoured only when there is no principal. **(3)** Then the refusal — and it must answer **three** ways, not two: same → refuse, different → permit, **unverifiable → permit and report**, because 4 legacy filings carry names that resolve to nobody and a two-valued gate would either refuse them forever or wave them through silently. `ChangeSoD` already encodes exactly this and its coverage surface already publishes `enabledButBlind` per gate |
| **Also worth carrying** | `drafted_by` holds an email address while the other two hold display names, in the same three-column chain. Whatever identity columns land should make that inconsistency unrepresentable rather than tolerated |
| **Evidence** | `NcaEccFilingService.transition` lines 107–116, read 2026-08-06 · dev `nca_ecc.filings`: 4 filings, 4 distinct people-labels, 0 resolving to `iam.users` by username or full name, 3 filings carrying both a reviewer and an approver · live probe leg 5 |
| **Lesson** | Asking *"what does this control compare, and does that data exist?"* has now produced F2-112, F2-113, F2-116 and this. It costs one query per module and it is the cheapest finding generator on this programme |

## F2-118 · 43 enabled automation rules cannot express their own condition, and every one reports SKIPPED

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-118 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `sharia` — a Sharia rule declined the exact case it exists for, and the cause is in the platform kernel, not the module)* |
| **Module** | `platform/automation` — `AutomationRuleEngine.conditionMatches` |
| **Severity** | **P1** — escalations keyed on money thresholds, severity and regulator identity never fire, in `fraud`, `opres`, `reginspect`, `regreporting` and `sharia` |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06** (V360). The operator vocabulary is implemented and the 43 rules ship **disabled**, exactly as this row recommended: *the capability lands, the behaviour does not.* **Live-probed 10/10** — AR-253, the rule that found this defect, was armed temporarily and recorded **SUCCESS** on a HIGH finding (the identical case that produced SKIPPED), **SKIPPED with “Condition did not match”** on a LOW one, and was disarmed with its `fire_count` restored. Suite 3,338/0/0 |
| **What shipped** | `eq` `ne` `gt`/`>` `gte`/`>=` `lt`/`<` `lte`/`<=` `in`, AND-ed when several appear on one field, numerics through the existing `toDouble` so a threshold held as a string still compares, and an unknown operator **fails closed**. `lte`/`<=`/`<` are implemented although no current rule uses them: the set is the closure of a comparison vocabulary, and **half a vocabulary is how the next author writes an unmatchable rule** |
| **The lesson was the fix, not a note under it** | This row's lesson was *“distinguish declined from could not evaluate in the run record, or 293 rows of evidence prove nothing”*. `conditionMatches` now returns a three-valued `ConditionVerdict`, and the engine records *“Condition did not match”* versus *“Condition could not be evaluated: unknown operator 'x' on field 'y'”*. That is the property whose absence hid the defect for months |
| **⚠ A fourth outcome value was considered and declined** | The stronger form is a `NOT_EVALUABLE` outcome rather than a message, and it was **not** taken here: `ck_rrun_outcome` admits only SUCCESS/FAILED/SKIPPED, so it needs a CHECK widening, plus `AutomationRuleController`'s SKIPPED KPI and the frontend's tone map to move with it, or the count silently under-reports. A message is **in** the run record, so the lesson is satisfied; the outcome value is a separate, reviewable change. Recorded rather than done |
| **⚠ Why disabling is the safe direction, not the cautious one** | The 43 were **enabled** and inert: the platform claimed they were on. Leaving them broken is not the conservative option — it is the option where a fraud loss over SAR 1M silently fails to escalate. Disabling them changes **no observable behaviour** (they had never fired once) and makes the register stop lying. V360 is idempotent and self-checking: it THROWs if the predicate matches nothing, if it matches a rule carrying no known operator (which would disable a *working* rule), if any operator rule is still enabled afterwards, or if the disabled count disagrees with the enabled count. Applied twice to dev: 43 then 0 |
| **The tripwire became the contract** | `AutomationConditionVocabularyTest` asserted `isFalse()` on eight real rules' condition JSON and was designed to break when the vocabulary landed. It did, and that failure was the signal to close this row. **Inverted, not deleted** — it still records which rules were inert and on what payload, and it gained boundary cases (`gte` includes, `lt` excludes), string-typed thresholds, a threshold applied to a word, AND-ing, and the declined-vs-inexpressible distinction. 8 checks |
| **⚠ I nearly filed a correction to a correct figure** | My first census bucketed `{}` as *scalar* and reported 22 empty / 149 scalar against this row's 134 / 37. Re-measured properly: **112** `{}` **+ 22 NULL = 134 empty**, 37 scalar, 43 operator — this row was right to the row. **A disagreement with a measured figure is a hypothesis about my own script first** |
| **Still open, and named** | The **arming** — 43 rules to re-enable one at a time after checking each audience resolves. F2-94 measured that 69 of 84 addressed role names do not exist, so an escalation to nobody is not an improvement on no escalation; that check is the precondition, and it is [[F2-05]]'s territory. AR-254's own run summary still reads `NOT A ROLE: SHARIA_OFFICER` |
| **How it was found** | AR-253 — *"HIGH Sharia audit finding → escalate Audit Committee Chair"* — was raised through the API against a genuinely `severity = HIGH` finding. The executor recorded `SKIPPED · "Condition did not match"`. Its condition is `{"severity":{"eq":"HIGH"}}` and `emit("SHARIA_AUDIT_FINDING_RAISED", …)` does publish `severity`, so it should have matched |
| **Root cause** | `AutomationRuleEngine.matches` compares only **scalars**: `isBoolean` / `isNumber` / `isTextual`, falling back to `Objects.equals(expected.asText(), String.valueOf(actual))`. An operator object like `{"eq":"HIGH"}` is an `ObjectNode` — none of the three type tests match, and **`asText()` on an ObjectNode returns the empty string**. So the comparison is `"" == "HIGH"` and the rule can never match, for any payload. There is no operator support in the evaluator at all; the 43 rules were authored assuming there was |
| **Measured 2026-08-05** | 214 rules: **134** empty condition (always match) · **37** scalar (work) · **43 operator-object (cannot match — all enabled, 0 have ever fired)** · 0 unparseable. Operators used: `eq` 33 · `in` 3 · `>` 3 · `gte` 2 · `gt` 2 · `ne` 2 · `lt` 1 |
| **⚠ The silence is the defect's worst property** | Each broken rule records `SKIPPED · "Condition did not match"` — the same outcome a correctly-evaluated negative produces. **293 SKIPPED runs exist.** Nothing distinguishes *"considered the event and declined"* from *"cannot express its own condition"*. A reader auditing the run log sees a functioning engine |
| **⚠ And a threshold rule that skips looks right while being unconditional** | AR-255 (*"Material SNCI > SAR 500k → escalate Board Risk Committee"*) skipped an 18,500 SAR event — the correct answer, for the wrong reason. **A 750,000 SAR event skips identically.** Same for AR-182 (fraud loss > SAR 1M), AR-185 (confirmed loss > SAR 500k), AR-183 (>100 affected customers), AR-202/AR-203 (NCA / CMA inspection opened → notify CISO) |
| **Fix** | Implement the operator vocabulary in `AutomationRuleEngine.matches`: `eq`, `ne`, `gt`, `gte`, `lt`, `lte`, `in`, plus the symbolic `>`/`<` three rules already use. Numeric comparisons must go through the existing `toDouble` so a decimal string compares correctly, and an unknown operator must fail **closed** — a condition the engine cannot understand must not fire, which is the one behaviour the current code gets right |
| **⚠ The owner decision this carries** | Implementing it **arms 43 dormant rules at once**, across five modules, each of which then begins escalating and notifying. That is the S6c/S8e situation — an enforcement change switched on at deploy, acting on work already under way — and F2-94 measured that most escalation audiences do not resolve anyway (69 of 84 addressed role names do not exist). Recommend: implement the evaluator, ship it with the rules **disabled**, then enable per rule after checking each audience resolves. Do not ship the evaluator and the arming in one commit |
| **Related** | Same family as **F2-94** (the fan-out reached nobody) and **F2-100** (the rule fired on the wrong thing), different mechanism. AR-254's own run summary states F2-120 out loud: `ESCALATE → RISK_MANAGER,COMPLIANCE_OFFICER (2 recipients); no active holder of CFO; NOT A ROLE: SHARIA_OFFICER` |
| **Lesson** | A rule engine that fails a condition it cannot parse *the same way* it fails a condition that evaluates false has no observable difference between "working" and "broken". Distinguish **declined** from **could not evaluate** in the run record, or 293 rows of evidence prove nothing |

## F2-119 · Three deadline claims in `sharia`, no clock anywhere in the module

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-119 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `sharia`; the third module in a row whose imperative javadoc names a deadline nothing computes)* |
| **Module** | `sharia` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — SAMA §5.7 and §6.4 / AAOIFI FAS 1 deadlines are documented, displayed and unenforced |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN**, tripwired by `ShariaLifecycleTest.theModuleHasNoDeadlineEnforcement` — the test fails if a scheduler appears, so the finding cannot be silently superseded |
| **Problem** | Three deadline claims, zero enforcement: **(1)** `ShariaAuditFinding` — *"HIGH-severity findings trigger AR-253 Board-Audit-Committee escalation **within 30 days** per SAMA §5.7"* — AR-253 fires on a **creation** event, which is a notification, not a deadline (and per **F2-118** it cannot fire at all). **(2)** `ShariaNonComplianceEvent` — SNCI *"purified … **within 90 days** of detection per SAMA §6.4 + AAOIFI FAS 1"* — and there is **no column** to hold or measure that window. **(3)** AR-260, *"SSB scholar term expiring → T-90/T-30 reminders"*, has **no producer**: the trigger `SHARIA_SCHOLAR_TERM_EXPIRING` is emitted nowhere, because a term-expiry sweep does not exist |
| **Measured** | `@Scheduled` appears nowhere in the module. `remediation_due_at` is written at creation and echoed back in two DTOs, and **nothing ever compares it to the current date**. `sharia.sharia_nc_event` has 28 columns and not one is a due date or deadline |
| **Fix** | A17-D3's shape, and its discipline: a due date that is **NULL when the start moment is missing**, or every row reports compliant forever — the exact trap A17-D3's own row fell into with one EMERGENCY change that had no implementation timestamp. Then a sweep, then a KPI. Also give AR-260 the producer it needs, or disable it: an enabled rule with no producer is indistinguishable from a rule whose condition never matched |
| **Evidence** | `ShariaAuditFinding` javadoc lines 24–26 · `ShariaNonComplianceEvent` javadoc lines 21–26 · `ShariaService` carries one date comparison in total, the F2-121 bound added by this slice · `platform.automation_rules` AR-260 enabled, fire_count 0, trigger emitted 0 times in the module |
| **Lesson** | Three modules in three slices — F2-115's 60-day SAMA notification, A17-D3's ratification SLA, and this — have had a deadline in prose and no clock in code. **Grep every module's imperative javadoc for a number followed by "days" before believing any of its deadlines.** |

## F2-120 · The Sharia Supervisory Board cannot act in the Sharia module

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-120 *(measured writing F2-03's authorisation-branch coverage for `sharia`; F2-116's shape, three times worse)* |
| **Module** | `sharia` — `ShariaController` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the body whose independent authority is the entire regulatory purpose of the module holds no role that exists |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN.** Pinned by `ShariaLifecycleTest` so a silent edit to any of the three guards fails the build |
| **Problem** | Measured against `iam.roles` (58 roles, dev, 2026-08-06), of the eight distinct names the three guard constants recite, **four do not exist**: `SHARIA_OFFICER`, `SHARIA_AUDITOR`, `SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR` and `CRO`. So `READ_GUARD` has 4 real holders of the 7 it names, **`WRITE_GUARD` 2 of 4**, and **`APPROVE_GUARD` 2 of 5** — leaving `PLATFORM_ADMIN` and `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER` as the only parties who can write or approve an SSB resolution, a Sharia audit finding, a non-compliance event or an Islamic product approval |
| **Confirmed by the platform itself** | AR-254's own run summary, recorded by the executor during this slice's probe: `ESCALATE → RISK_MANAGER,COMPLIANCE_OFFICER (2 recipients); no active holder of CFO; NOT A ROLE: SHARIA_OFFICER`. The engine says *NOT A ROLE* in a stored field and nothing reads it |
| **Why it fails silently** | `hasAnyRole` gets no `PLATFORM_ADMIN` bypass and has no referential integrity to the role table, so a name that does not exist simply never matches. No error, no log line, no startup warning. RC-C, and F2-52's ~50% miss rate across 647 guards, landing in the flagship Saudi SKU |
| **⚠ Why filed and not fixed** | Two remedies, not equivalent: **(a)** create `SHARIA_OFFICER`/`SHARIA_AUDITOR`/`SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR` as roles and grant them, which is a governance decision about who holds authority in a customer's organisation; **(b)** convert to `hasPermission` under **F2-05**, the ratified direction, which makes the names irrelevant. Inventing four privileged roles to make a guard true is the F2-14 anti-pattern, and doing it here would pre-empt F2-05's design |
| **Commercial weight** | §6d's contention rule calls F2-03 revenue critical-path *because* `sharia` and `data_officer` had no tests — W3-5 makes the flagship Saudi SKU unsellable until it lands. A prospect asking *"can our Sharia Board approve rulings in the platform?"* is answered *no* today, by data rather than by design |
| **Lesson** | Same as F2-116's, and the repetition is the point: reading role names as authority without joining them to the role table is how this platform accumulated 537 of these |

## F2-121 · The SNCI purification date was unbounded — FIXED

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-121 |
| **Module** | `sharia` — `ShariaService.purifyNcEvent` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the date an AAOIFI FAS 1 disclosure and the SAMA §6.4 window are both computed from accepted any value |
| **Current State** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-08-06** (no migration) |
| **Problem** | `purifiedAt` is `@NotNull` and otherwise unconstrained. Two values are simply incoherent and neither was refused: a **future** date, which asserts a donation that has not happened; and a date **before `detected_at`**, i.e. income purified before the breach that produced it was discovered |
| **Fix** | Both bounded in `ShariaService.purifyNcEvent`, refused as 409 `ProblemDetail` naming the offending date and the detection date |
| **⚠ Deliberately NOT server-derived** | Purification genuinely happens outside the platform and is recorded afterwards from a charity receipt, so a back-dated value is **legitimate** and stamping `LocalDate.now()` would overwrite the real donation date with the data-entry date — the mirror image of RC-E, corrupting the disclosure it feeds. **Bounding a caller's value is the correct treatment when the caller is the only source of it**; a mutation replacing the bound with a server stamp is caught |
| **Verified** | 12 unit checks, **11/11 mutations caught** (including the server-stamp mutation and both bound removals); live **15/16** on the first pass and the one failure was the probe's own instrument, not the platform — see F2-118 |
| **Refuted while investigating** | Four hypotheses about this module were tested and **all four failed**, which is worth recording: the purification evidence **is** mandatory (`@NotBlank` destination + charity receipt); `snciAmountSar` is `@NotNull` at detection so the `Map.of` NPE is unreachable; `subject_area` is NOT NULL so the transition NPE is unreachable; and every entity carries `@Where` so soft deletes are filtered. This module is in better shape than `outsourcing` was |

## F2-122 · One status, two writers, different rules

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-122 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `sharia` — a green machine test would have certified a machine a sibling method bypasses)* |
| **Module** | `sharia` — `transitionNcEvent` vs `ShariaService.purifyNcEvent` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — a documented divergence, not a exploit; but it makes the non-compliance lifecycle two different lifecycles depending on which endpoint you call |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN.** Pinned by `ShariaLifecycleTest.purifyIsASecondWriterThatBypassesTheMap` so neither writer can move without the other being reconsidered |
| **Problem** | `NC_ALLOWED` declares `DETECTED → {INVESTIGATION}` — `DETECTED` may become `INVESTIGATION` and nothing else. `transitionNcEvent` enforces that. `ShariaService.purifyNcEvent` never consults the map: it sets `INCOME_PURIFIED` directly from **either** `INVESTIGATION` **or `DETECTED`**, so the shortcut the map forbids is reachable through a different endpoint |
| **⚠ Why it is not "fixed"** | Whether purifying Sharia non-compliance income may precede investigation is a business decision under SAMA §6.4 / AAOIFI FAS 1, and the `DETECTED` branch in `ShariaService.purifyNcEvent` was **written deliberately** — it is a guard listing two statuses, not an omission. Silently reversing another author's decision because a test was easier to write that way is not a fix. What the floor does instead is make the divergence impossible to miss |
| **Fix** | Decide, then make one writer authoritative: either `ShariaService.purifyNcEvent` routes through `NC_ALLOWED` (and `DETECTED` leaves its allowed-from set), or `NC_ALLOWED` gains `DETECTED → INCOME_PURIFIED` so the map states what the module actually permits. Either way the two must agree — RC-A at the level of a single service |
| **Lesson** | A state-machine test that reads the service's own map proves implementation-against-declaration for **that writer**. It says nothing about a second writer of the same field. Count the writers before trusting a machine test |

## F2-123 · The PDPL 30-day clock started when the DPO typed the request in — FIXED

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-123 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `data_officer`; identified by an inconsistency inside the module rather than by reading the statute)* |
| **Module** | `data_officer` — `DataSubjectRightsService.createDsr` |
| **Severity** | **P1** — a statutory deadline silently extended, in the organisation's favour, on the majority of records |
| **Current State** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-08-06** (no migration — the column already existed and is already populated) |
