| **Problem** | PDPL Art.10 gives a controller 30 days to respond to a data-subject request, measured from **receipt**. `createDsr` stamped `receivedAt = LocalDateTime.now()` and `slaDeadline = LocalDateTime.now().plusDays(30)`, so the clock started when the request was *registered in the platform*, not when it *arrived at the organisation* |
| **Measured 2026-08-06** | 17 requests in dev. **9 of them arrived by a channel where receipt necessarily precedes entry**: `EMAIL` 3, `SDAIA_PORTAL` 2, `CALL` 2, `BRANCH` 1, `SERVICE_CENTER` 1 — against `WEB` 6 and `MOBILE` 2 that are genuinely self-service. All 17 carried `sla_deadline = received_at + exactly 30d`. So a request that came in by post or telephone last week and was logged today received a **fresh 30-day window** — up to 30 days beyond the statute |
| **⚠ How it was identified** | Not by reading PDPL, but by noticing that **the breach path next door does it correctly**: `sdaiaDeadline` is `req.detectedAt().plusHours(72)`, computed from the caller's real-world detection moment. Same module, same author, same regulation family, opposite treatment of the same question. **An inconsistency inside one module is a cheaper detector than any external standard** — one path is usually right |
| **Fix** | `createDsr` takes an optional `receivedAt`; the deadline is `receivedAt.plusDays(30)`. A **future** receipt is refused (a request cannot have arrived before it was sent); a **past** one is not, because late registration is precisely the case being fixed |
| **⚠ Not a break** | `receivedAt` is optional and absence still means "arrived now", so every existing caller — including the frontend, which does not send it — behaves exactly as before. A widening, not a change |
| **⚠ Not server-stamped, deliberately** | F2-121's reasoning one module over: when the caller is the only source of a real-world moment, **bound their value rather than replace it**. Stamping `LocalDateTime.now()` is what produced the defect |
| **Verified** | 11 unit checks, **10/10 mutations caught** — including reverting the clock to `LocalDateTime.now()`, ignoring `receivedAt`, removing the future bound, widening 30 days to 60, widening 72 hours to 168, and both overdue finders losing their exclusions |
| **What this module gets RIGHT, recorded because three predecessors did not** | `data_officer` is the best-built of the four modules floored so far. **37 `hasPermission` guards and no business role named** — only `PLATFORM_ADMIN` appears, so none of F2-116's or F2-120's fictional-role class. **The statutory clocks exist and are consulted**: both deadlines are computed, remaining hours are surfaced per record, and `findOverdue`/`findOverdueSdaia` are counted on the dashboard, each excluding terminal statuses and the breach one additionally requiring `sdaiaNotifiedAt IS NULL`. That is the direct counter-example to **F2-119**, the same platform one module over with three deadline claims and no clock at all |
| **Lesson** | Two paths in one module that treat the same question differently — a caller-supplied moment versus `LocalDateTime.now()` — are a defect detector that costs nothing to run and needs no domain expertise. **Diff the module against itself before diffing it against the regulation.** |

## F2-124 · `spring.jackson.time-zone: UTC` shifts timezone-less `LocalDateTime` inputs by the client's offset

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-124 *(observed live while probing F2-123; recorded rather than concluded, because the fix is a platform-wide config decision)* |
| **Module** | platform-wide — `application.yml:109 — spring.jackson.time-zone: UTC` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — no arithmetic is wrong today, but a wall-clock a user typed is not the wall-clock that gets stored, and it can change the DATE |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN**, needs measurement then a decision. Deliberately not changed by F2-03(d): altering a global Jackson timezone shifts every temporal field across 2,168 mappings |
| **Observed** | Sending `2026-07-28T01:11:16` to `POST /api/v1/data-officer/dsr` stored `2026-07-27 22:11:16` — the field is `LocalDateTime`, which by definition carries **no** timezone, yet it moved by exactly the client/JVM offset (3h; JVM runs UTC, SQL Server and the client run UTC+3) |
| **Why it is not a deadline bug** | Verified before filing: every deadline comparison uses the **JVM** clock on both sides — `sla_deadline` is written from a JVM value and `findOverdue` takes `now` as a parameter, and a grep confirms **no** SQL in the module compares these columns to `GETDATE()`. So the arithmetic is internally consistent and F2-123's fix is sound |
| **Why it still matters** | A DPO in Riyadh recording *"the request arrived 2026-07-28 00:30"* stores `2026-07-27 21:30` — the **date changes**, and a statutory register that reports by day would show the wrong one. The same applies to every `LocalDateTime` the API accepts: breach detection moments, purification dates, meeting times |
| **What to measure before deciding** | How many API-facing `LocalDateTime` fields represent a **user-asserted wall clock** (receipt, detection, a donation date) versus a **machine instant**. The two want opposite treatment: a wall clock must be stored verbatim, an instant must be normalised. `application.yml:109 — spring.jackson.time-zone: UTC` applies one rule to both |
| **Candidate fixes, not yet chosen** | (a) leave the global setting and let `LocalDateTime` bind verbatim, which is Jackson's documented behaviour when the type is timezone-less — the shift suggests something else is converting, so **find that first**; (b) move user-asserted moments to `OffsetDateTime` so the caller's offset is explicit and preserved; (c) keep UTC and make every client send UTC, which is a frontend contract change |
| **⚠ Where it came from** | My own probe asserted a stored value against the local-time string it sent, and failed. The failure was my instrument, not the platform — but chasing it surfaced a real question nobody had asked. **A probe assertion that fails for a timezone reason is worth reading rather than relaxing** |

## F2-125 · The open-banking consent lifecycle is documented, has no machine, and cannot expire

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-125 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `openbanking`)* |
| **Module** | `openbanking` — `ObService` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — latent today, and the register asserts a TPP's access is authorised after the customer's consent has lapsed |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN**, tripwired by `ObLifecycleTest.theConsentLifecycleIsDocumentedButUnenforced`: the test fails the moment a consent machine appears, so the finding cannot be silently superseded |
| **Problem** | `ObService`'s class javadoc states `Consent: AUTHORISED → ACTIVE → EXPIRED | REVOKED`. There is **no** `CONSENT_ALLOWED` map, no validator call on that path, and **nothing anywhere sets** `status = "EXPIRED"` — the sole occurrence of the literal is a read-side terminal check that refuses re-revoking an already finished consent. So an `ACTIVE` consent whose `expires_at` passes stays `ACTIVE` indefinitely |
| **Measured 2026-08-06** | 25 consents: 13 `ACTIVE` (all carrying an expiry), 1 `EXPIRED`, 11 `REVOKED`. ⚠ **Latent, not manifest: 0 of the 13 ACTIVE are currently past their expiry** — stated plainly, because a finding that overstates its own blast radius gets discounted. The single `EXPIRED` row was seeded directly; no code path could have produced it |
| **Refuted while investigating** | The 11 consents with no `expires_at` at all are **all REVOKED**, where an expiry is meaningless — so there is no never-expiring-consent defect, which was the first hypothesis. And the 180-day SAMA OBF default **is** applied when a caller omits an expiry |
| **Fix — a decision first** | Two shapes, not equivalent. **(a) A sweep** moving ACTIVE→EXPIRED past the date: gives the register a real state, but the status lags reality between runs. **(b) A derived read** treating a past-expiry ACTIVE consent as expired wherever it is consulted: cannot lag, and is the position **S7e** took for exactly this reason — a stored word can disagree with the rows. (b) is the better default; either way the machine and every writer must agree, which is F2-122's lesson one module over |
| **Lesson** | A lifecycle in a class javadoc is not a lifecycle. Four states documented, two with transitions, one with no producer at all — the same shape as `sharia`'s AR-260 and CE-13's `PENDING_RATIFICATION` |

## F2-126 · SAMA's 24-hour OB incident window is tracked as a binary, not a deadline

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-126 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `openbanking`)* |
| **Module** | `openbanking` — `ObService.notifySama` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the obligation OBF v2 §11.4 imposes is a *time limit*, and time is the only thing the platform cannot report about it |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN**, tripwired by `ObLifecycleTest.theSamaTwentyFourHourWindowIsNotComputed` |
| **Problem** | `ObIncident`'s javadoc: an incident affecting the OB service *must be reported to SAMA's OBF unit* **within 24 hours** *under OBF v2 §11.4*. `ObService.notifySama` records `sama_notification_ref`, stamps `sama_notified_at` and sets `sama_reportable` — and **never computes or stores anything about the 24 hours**. An incident notified in two hours and one notified in five days are byte-identical in the record |
| **⚠ What makes this small to fix and worth filing** | Every input is already on the row: `detected_at`, `sama_reportable`, `sama_notified_at`. There is no missing column and no missing act — **only the subtraction**. This module does more than `sharia` did (F2-119): the flag is settable at creation and `countByOrganisationIdAndSamaReportableAndStatusNotIn` feeds a `samaReportableOpen` dashboard metric. The gap is precisely the time dimension |
| **Measured 2026-08-06** | 3 incidents. Incident #1 is flagged reportable, was detected **2026-05-26** and has **never** been notified — roughly **72 days** against a 24-hour obligation. The dashboard reports `samaReportableOpen` = 1. It cannot report *72 days late*, because nothing subtracts two timestamps it already holds |
| **Corrected while investigating** | The first hypothesis was that `sama_reportable` is only ever set *by* notifying, making should-report-but-has-not unrepresentable. **False** — it is settable at creation (`i.setSamaReportable(req.getSamaReportable())`), 1 row is in that state, and a finder counts it. Recorded because the module is better instrumented than the guess assumed |
| **Fix** | Derive lateness on read, as `data_officer` already does for its own two statutory clocks — remaining hours per record plus an overdue count, each excluding notified and terminal incidents. No migration: `detected_at` and `sama_notified_at` are enough. `data_officer` is the working pattern to copy, in the same codebase |
| **Lesson** | An obligation with a deadline needs three things — the moment it starts, the moment it is discharged, and the **comparison**. Two of three looks complete on a schema review and reports nothing an examiner would ask for |

## F2-127 · `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER` does not exist, so the module's own owner role cannot write — ✔ CLOSED

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-127 *(the third instance of F2-116's shape, after F2-120)* |
| **Module** | `openbanking` — `ObController` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the named owner of the SAMA Open Banking function holds no role that exists |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-07 by [[F2-145]](e) slice 2.** All 18 guards converted to `hasPermission('OPENBANKING', …)`; the pin was rewritten as `theGuardConstantsAreConvertedToPermissions` and now asserts the closure instead of the defect |
| ✔ **How it closed, and by which of the three mechanisms** | Not by seating and not by adjudicating — by **conversion**, which deletes every name a guard recited. `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER` was seated by **V362** (so the org-chart role became real), had its mis-derived `OPENBANKING:APPROVE` revoked by **V368**, and then the conversion removed the *guard's* dependence on any name at all. `CRO` needed no decision here: its three recitations in this controller simply ceased to exist. This is the second confirmation of ADR-017 D-14's **third** retirement route, after `SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR` |
| 🔁 **The lesson below was right, and the sequencing it asked for is what happened** | It said *“F2-05 should be sequenced on this evidence rather than on the guard count”*. It was: `sharia` and `openbanking` — the two modules with filed ghost findings — went first, and measuring them is what exposed V362's classifier defect in **4 of 6** modules, which a count-driven sweep would have carried straight into `cpty` and `aml` as a silent widening. ⚠ The one thing the lesson got wrong is the figure: **537 sites** was the count at filing; the ratchet measures **493** after this slice, and the number in a prose lesson decays while the constant does not |
| **Problem** | Measured against `iam.roles` (58 roles, dev, 2026-08-06): `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER` — the module's own named owner role — and `CRO` **do not exist**. `READ_GUARD` has 5 real holders of the 6 it names, **`WRITE_GUARD` 2 of 3**, and **`APPROVE_GUARD` 2 of 3**, leaving `PLATFORM_ADMIN` and `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER` as the only parties who can onboard, suspend or revoke a TPP |
| **The pattern, now measured three times** | `outsourcing` recites `CRO`/`CIO` (F2-116), `sharia` recites four fictional names (F2-120), `openbanking` recites two. Every one is the **domain-specialist** role the module was designed around — Chief Risk Officer, Sharia Officer, Open Banking Officer — and none was ever created. **The guards were written from an org chart, not from `iam.roles`** |
| **⚠ Why filed and not fixed** | Same as F2-116 and F2-120: creating privileged roles is a governance decision about a customer's organisation, and converting to `hasPermission` is **F2-05**'s ratified direction, which makes the names irrelevant. Fixing three modules' fictional roles one guard at a time is the wrong shape — it belongs in F2-05, and these three findings are now its evidence base |
| **Lesson** | Three modules, three sets of fictional specialist roles, one cause. **F2-05 should be sequenced on this evidence rather than on the guard count** — the count says 537 sites; these say what the conversion actually buys |

## F2-128 · Lapsed data-protection waivers were in no metric at all — REPORTED (state decision still open)

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-128 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `dataclass`; the manifest twin of F2-125 in `openbanking`)* |
| **Module** | `dataclass` — `DataclassService.dashboard` |
| **Severity** | **P1** — a waiver of a data-protection control, no longer authorised, counted as active and visible nowhere |
| **Current State** | 🟡 **The population is now REPORTED (2026-08-06). The state question is deliberately still open** — see below |
| **Problem** | `EXCEPTION_ALLOWED` declares `ACTIVE → EXPIRED` and **nothing anywhere sets** `status = "EXPIRED"`. So an ACTIVE exception past its `valid_until` stays ACTIVE. That is F2-125's shape — but here it is **manifest**, and the reason it stayed invisible is sharper than an absent sweep |
| **⚠ The window was half-open in the wrong direction** | The only code in the module reading `valid_until` was the *expiring-soon* metric: `!e.getValidUntil().isBefore(today) && !e.getValidUntil().isAfter(today.plusDays(30))`. Its lower bound **excludes everything already lapsed**. So a lapsed waiver appeared in **no metric**: absent from expiring-soon precisely *because* it had expired, and counted inside `exceptionsActive` as though still valid. Somebody did think about expiry, and wrote a half-open window the wrong way round |
| **Measured 2026-08-06, per tenant** | ⚠ **Corrected by the live probe**: the first pass counted across all organisations and would have presented a platform aggregate as one operator's view. Per tenant: **org 1 — 2 ACTIVE, 1 lapsed · org 2 — 1 ACTIVE, 1 lapsed (100%) · org 3 — 1 ACTIVE, 1 lapsed (100%)**. Platform-wide 4 ACTIVE, 3 lapsed, by 46–54 days. **Every tenant has at least one**, which is stronger than the single-tenant reading: it is not one demo dataset's quirk. Two are `MASKING_WAIVER`, one `ENCRYPTION_AT_REST_WAIVER` |
| **What shipped** | A separate `exceptionsLapsed` count, plus a matching `Lapsed but still active` tile toned **danger**. Kept distinct from expiring-soon on purpose: *about to lapse* and *already lapsed* demand different responses, and folding them together hides the urgent case inside the routine one. The tile ships with the count because **a metric nobody renders is this programme's own "a ratchet nobody runs is a comment"** |
| **⚠ What deliberately did NOT ship** | Any state change. Whether a lapsed exception should be **swept** to EXPIRED or **derived** as expired on read is the same open decision as **F2-125**, and it should be taken once for both modules rather than differently in each. S7e's reasoning favours deriving — a stored word can disagree with the rows. Making the population visible needs no such decision, so that is all this slice did |
| **Verified** | 11 unit checks, **13/13 mutations caught** — including deleting the lapsed count, removing the expiring window's lower bound (which would fold the two together), and dropping its terminal-status exclusion so REVOKED waivers would be counted as live. Live: the API returns `exceptionsLapsed` = 1 for org 1, against `exceptionsActive` = 2 and expiring-soon = 0 — the exact operator view that was previously blind |
| **Lesson** | When a metric over a date window reports nothing, check the **bounds** before concluding nobody looked. A half-open window that excludes the overdue population is worse than no metric: it renders a zero that reads as reassurance |

## F2-129 · Reclassifying an asset upward does not re-examine its already-approved transfers

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-129 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `dataclass`, by reading why the Art.7 gate sits where it does)* |
| **Module** | `dataclass` — `transitionAsset` vs `transitionFlow` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — latent, and structurally real: the obligation attaches to a property that can change after it is checked |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN**, tripwired by `DataclassLifecycleTest.reclassificationDoesNotRecheckArticleSeven` — the test fails if the asset path starts consulting flows |
| **Problem** | PDPL Art.7 requires a transfer authorisation reference for a **cross-border** flow of **CONFIDENTIAL/TOP_SECRET** data. The gate runs once, on `PROPOSED → APPROVED`, reading the asset's classification at that moment. But `ASSET_ALLOWED` makes `IN_USE → RECLASSIFY → CLASSIFIED → APPROVED → IN_USE` a first-class path, so an asset can become sensitive **after** its flows were approved — and nothing re-examines a running flow when that happens |
| **⚠ The gate's placement is otherwise correct, and worth recording** | My first test drove `APPROVED → ACTIVE` and failed, which sent me to read the placement. `FLOW_ALLOWED` makes `PROPOSED → APPROVED` the **only** entry to the approved-and-onward region, so every ACTIVE flow has passed the gate, and `SUSPENDED → ACTIVE` reactivates a flow whose reference is already on the row. Checking at approval rather than at activation is the right single point — the gap is not the placement, it is that the checked property is mutable |
| **Measured 2026-08-06** | ⚠ **Latent: 0 rows.** Both ACTIVE cross-border flows in dev are correct — `DF-TXN-AWS-001` carries `PDPL-ART7-2024-014` for its CONFIDENTIAL asset, and `DF-ATC-MKT-REF-001` is INTERNAL and needs none. Stated plainly rather than implied, because a structural gap with no instances is still worth documenting and not worth alarm |
| **Fix** | On an upward reclassification, re-examine the asset's ACTIVE and APPROVED flows and either refuse the reclassification until each cross-border transfer carries a reference, or flag them for review. Refusing is the stronger control but couples two registers; flagging is cheaper and matches how F2-128 now reports rather than blocks. **A decision, not an oversight** |
| **Lesson** | A gate reads a property at a moment. If that property is mutable and the thing it authorises is long-lived, the check needs a second trigger — on the property, not only on the act. Ask of any gate: *can what I just checked change while the thing I authorised is still running?* |

## F2-130 · Only the platform superuser can approve a SAMA recovery plan — and one dead role passes the existence check

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-130 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `rrp`; the fourth instance of F2-116's shape, and the one that refines how the shape must be measured)* |
| **Module** | `rrp` — `RrpController` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — no business role can approve a Recovery &amp; Resolution Plan; approval authority is the platform superuser alone |
| **Current State** | 🔴 **OPEN.** Pinned by `RrpLifecycleTest.theGuardConstantsArePinnedWithTheirUnusableRoles` |
| **Problem** | `APPROVE_GUARD` names three roles. Measured against `iam.roles` **and `iam.user_roles`** on 2026-08-06, exactly **one** is satisfiable: `PLATFORM_ADMIN` exists with 1 holder; **`CRO` does not exist**; **`CFO` exists with 0 holders**. `WRITE_GUARD` is 2 of 4 for the same reason (`BCM_MANAGER` exists, 0 holders) and `READ_GUARD` 4 of 8 |
| **⚠ THE REFINEMENT, and it changes how F2-52 should be read** | F2-116, F2-120 and F2-127 were each measured by asking *does this name exist in `iam.roles`* — and **`CFO` passes that check**. A role that exists with **no holders** is equally unusable and fails differently: a name-existence join reports it as fine. So **F2-52's platform-wide figure (~50% of 74 recited names do not exist) is a LOWER BOUND** on the unusable population. There are two ways a role-name guard authorises nobody, and only one of them has been counted |
| **The pattern, four for four** | `outsourcing` (F2-116) · `sharia` (F2-120) · `openbanking` (F2-127) · `rrp`. Each recites the **domain-specialist** role it was designed around — Chief Risk Officer, Sharia Officer, Open Banking Officer, CRO/CFO — and none of those is usable. **The guards were written from an org chart, not from the role table** |
| **⚠ Why filed and not fixed** | Unchanged from F2-116/120/127: creating a role or granting it is a governance decision about a customer's organisation, and converting to `hasPermission` is **F2-05**'s ratified direction, which makes the names irrelevant. This is the fourth data point for sequencing F2-05, not a fourth patch |
| **What F2-05 must therefore measure** | Both failure modes. A conversion planned on *name existence* alone would leave `CFO`-style guards looking healthy while still authorising nobody. The check is a join to `iam.user_roles`, not to `iam.roles` |
| **Lesson** | Ask of an authorisation list not *do these names exist* but **can anybody satisfy it today**. Existence is a weaker question than holding, and the weaker question is the one four findings had been asking |

## F2-131 · WITHDRAWN before filing — the RRP submit split is deliberate, not a second-writer defect

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-131 — **number allocated and withdrawn on 2026-08-06.** Recorded rather than left as a gap, so nobody hunts for a lost finding |
| **Module** | `rrp` — `RrpService.transitionCycle` vs `RrpService.submitCycle` |
| **Severity** | n/a — **not a defect** |
| **The hypothesis** | `ALLOWED` does **not** contain `APPROVED → SUBMITTED`, yet `RrpService.submitCycle` performs exactly that transition. On the evidence of **F2-122** (`sharia`, where `purifyNcEvent` silently bypassed `NC_ALLOWED`), this looked like the same defect: one status, two writers, contradictory rules — and worse, a map that *forbids* what the sibling writer does |
| **Why it is refuted** | `RrpService.transitionCycle` intercepts `to == "SUBMITTED"` **before** consulting the map and refuses it with *"Use the dedicated /submit endpoint to lodge with SAMA"*. The split is deliberate, the refusal is **actionable — it names the other door** — and the dedicated endpoint exists for a real reason: lodging captures a `regulator_ack_reference` that the generic transition DTO has no field for. Folding submission into the generic path would lose the acknowledgement or force a nullable field on every transition |
| **So it is the counter-example to F2-122** | Both modules split one status across two writers. `sharia` does it silently; `rrp` refuses explicitly and points at the correct endpoint. **The difference between a defect and a design here is a message**, which is worth carrying: two writers of one field are defensible exactly when the one that refuses says where to go instead |
| **Pinned anyway** | `RrpLifecycleTest.theGenericTransitionRoutesSubmissionToItsOwnEndpoint` asserts the refusal message names `/submit` and that `ALLOWED` still omits the edge — so if the map ever gains `APPROVED → SUBMITTED`, the arrangement has decayed into F2-122's version and the build says so |
| **Lesson** | A hypothesis carried over from a previous module is a good prior, not a finding. Four modules into a pattern, the temptation is to file the fifth on resemblance; reading eleven lines of the refusal branch was the whole cost of not doing that |

## F2-132 · A SAMA submission was 128 days overdue in every tenant, and nothing compared the date — REPORTED

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-132 *(found writing F2-03's floor for `rrp`; the fourth deadline-with-no-clock in this row)* |
| **Module** | `rrp` — `RrpService.dashboard` |
| **Severity** | **P1** — a regulatory filing deadline stored, displayed, and compared to nothing, while overdue in all three tenants |
| **Current State** | 🟡 **REPORTED 2026-08-06** (no migration). Deliberately not enforced — see below |
| **Problem** | `submission_due_date` was written at creation, updatable via `updateCycle`, echoed in every cycle response — and **compared to nothing anywhere in the module**. The dashboard counted cycles by status only, so a late filing appeared merely as *active* |
| **Measured 2026-08-06** | **All three tenants** hold an `IN_PREPARATION` FY2026 cycle **128 days past** its 2026-03-31 due date. Orgs 1 and 3 additionally hold FY2025 cycles 493 days past due — correctly *not* overdue, because those are `CLOSED` |
| **What shipped** | `overdueCycleCount` on the dashboard plus a UI tile, placed beside *Active cycles* because an overdue cycle is counted in that tile too and the two must be read together |
| **⚠ The population is the pre-submission statuses, and that mattered** | `ACTIVE_STATUSES` — DRAFT/IN_PREPARATION/BOARD_REVIEW/APPROVED — not *everything not closed*. Once a cycle is `SUBMITTED` or `ACKNOWLEDGED` the filing has happened, so a passed due date is **history rather than an outstanding obligation**; counting those would report a permanent, unfixable overdue figure. That is precisely the trap **A17-D3** fell into by measuring a clock whose start moment was absent. A null due date is likewise not overdue: no deadline was set |
| **⚠ Reported, never enforced** | Nothing refuses a late submission and no status changes. Lateness is a fact about a date; a stored LATE word could disagree with the date it derives from — S7e's reasoning. Consistent with **F2-128** one module over, where the same choice was made for the same reason |
| **The row's fourth instance** | F2-115 (60-day SAMA notification, deferred) · F2-119 (`sharia`, three claims and no clock at all) · F2-126 (`openbanking`, 24 hours tracked as a binary) · this. ⚠ And the counter-example is in the same codebase: `data_officer` computes both of its statutory clocks and counts the overdue population on its dashboard. **The capability was never missing — it was never copied** |
| **Verified** | 8 unit checks, **13/13 mutations caught**, including counting lodged cycles as overdue, treating a null due date as overdue, and widening `ACTIVE_STATUSES` to include `SUBMITTED` |
| **Lesson** | Four modules stored a regulatory deadline and never compared it, while a fifth in the same repository did it properly. When a capability exists once, the finding is not *build it* — it is **why did the pattern not travel**, and that is a review question rather than an engineering one |

## F2-133 · F2-34's rule contradiction is one ambiguous word, and one reading is already in force

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-133 *(found trying to implement F2-08b and reading its blocker instead of working around it)* |
| **Module** | architecture — `MODULE_SEPARABILITY_AND_SKU_MODEL.md` §2 vs §6c, and `Appendix/scripts/coupling.py` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — it does not break anything; it has blocked a P1 spine item on a decision nobody could price |
| **Current State** | 🟡 **The measurement is DONE and the choice is priced. The choice itself remains F2-34's owner decision** |
| **What F2-34 says** | *"`MODULE_SEPARABILITY_AND_SKU_MODEL.md` §2 says kernel imports don't count while §6c's rule says only `common/security/platform/spi` are legal — under the first reading `CommitteePort` is unnecessary… The boundary ratchet cannot be seeded until one reading wins."* Plus two irreconcilable counts: the architecture seat's **6** bidirectional pairs against the plan graph's **13** |
| **⚠ The contradiction is the word "platform", and this tree has two of them** | `com.tss.grc.platform` — **5 files**, a top-level package beside `common`/`config`/`security`. `com.tss.grc.module.platform` — **115 files**, a module holding licensing, auditlog, automation, files, sso, persistence. Every prose statement of the rule says *platform* without saying which. ⚠ **`Appendix/scripts/coupling.py` resolves it BOTH WAYS inside one script**: an import of `com.tss.grc.platform` is tallied `<kernel>` (line 57) while an import of `com.tss.grc.module.platform` is tallied a **peer** (line 54) |
| **Both readings, measured at HEAD** | **A — narrow kernel** (`com.tss.grc.module.platform` is a peer): **975** peer import lines · **140** directed pairs · **13** bidirectional. **B — wide kernel** (`com.tss.grc.module.platform` IS kernel): **311** lines · **90** pairs · **4** bidirectional. ⚠ **Reading A reproduces the plan's "13" exactly**, and **9 of those 13 pairs involve `com.tss.grc.module.platform`** — that is the whole discrepancy. The seat's 6 matches neither, so it used a third method |
| **So the choice is now priced** | Reading B legalises **664** import lines that A counts as coupling, and removes 50 directed pairs and 9 cycles. A ratchet seeded under A starts at 975/140/13; under B at 311/90/4 |
| **⚠ AND READING B IS ALREADY IN FORCE** | `ChangeModuleBoundaryTest` — the only boundary ratchet the codebase has, shipped by S5a — declares `ALLOWED = Set.of("change_mgmt", "platform")` with the javadoc *"platform is the kernel (audit ledger, automation events, licensing, realtime, calendar)"*. **A choice was made, shipped and passing, and F2-34 does not record it.** Seeding F2-08b under reading A would contradict a green test |
| **⚠ And the inversion grew while the item sat blocked** | F2-34 recorded **40** kernel→business import lines at `ff087c7c`. Measured now: **55** (`platform` 38, `integrations` 8, `dashboard` 9). ⚠ Corrected from a first pass that reported 68 by summing `integrations -> platform` as well — business depending on kernel is legal under both readings, and a total mixing directions measures nothing |
| **What shipped** | `Appendix/scripts/boundary_readings.py` — the priced comparison, re-runnable. And `ModuleGraphRatchetTest`, which ratchets **only what is true under both readings**: the **4** business↔business cycles that no reading excuses (`bcm↔tprm, compliance↔policy, privacy↔tprm, scm↔tprm` — three of the four involve `tprm`), and the **55** kernel→business inversions. Both floors may only fall; the reading-dependent totals are deliberately **not** seeded, and the test says so where a reader will look |
| **Why not just pick one** | A ceiling tolerates the population it measures. Seeding 13 or 4 as *tolerated* would legitimise whichever architecture I guessed, across 59 packages — the same defect that forced F2-61(c)'s export ceiling to be replaced by an invariant, at 59× the scale |
| **Recommendation to the Architecture seat** | Ratify **reading B** and say so in `MODULE_SEPARABILITY_AND_SKU_MODEL.md` §2 and §6c: it is what `ChangeModuleBoundaryTest` already enforces, it is what `Appendix/scripts/coupling.py` already assumes for `com.tss.grc.platform`, and it leaves a 311/90/4 graph that is seedable. Then rename one of the two namespaces, because the ambiguity will otherwise recur every time the rule is quoted |
| **Lesson** | A blocked item's blocker is worth reading before routing around it. F2-34 looked like an architecture dispute needing a seat; it was a word with two referents, measurable in an afternoon, with the answer already shipped in a test nobody had cited |

## F2-134 · The drift gate existed and nothing ran it — because of a hardcoded Windows path

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-134 *(found implementing F2-19b; the same shape as F2-36, one mechanism over)* |
| **Module** | `.github/workflows/ci.yml` · `Appendix/scripts/` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — every published figure in the measurement corpus could go stale silently, and did |
| **Current State** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-08-06.** The gate runs on every push and pull request |
| **The gate already existed** | `baseline_census.py` has carried `--no-db --check` — *exit 1 when a gated figure drifted* — since **F2-69** built it, for exactly this purpose. It was never wired to a runner. This is **F2-36's shape**: the guard is written, the enforcement is absent, and *a ratchet nobody runs is a comment*. F2-36's own instance was `vitest` missing from CI, which let 8 shipped-but-unreachable screens survive |
| **⚠ What actually blocked it was a PATH, not a database** | The obvious hypothesis was that the scripts need SQL Server and CI has none. **False.** `baseline_census.py` imports `pyodbc` *inside* a function behind a try/except and degrades to a source-only census. The real blocker: **three of the four scripts hardcoded** `ROOT = r"D:\AYMAN\GRC"`, so none could execute on a Linux runner. `baseline_census.py` already derived its root from `__file__`; the pattern had simply never spread. ⚠ **Including to a script I wrote an hour earlier in this same session** — `boundary_readings.py` copied the local idiom and inherited the defect |
| **Proven on its first real run** | Wired up and run in anger, `--no-db --check` **immediately failed**: `MEASURED_BASELINE.md` published **15** zero-test packages against a tree holding **8**, because F2-03 had floored seven of them that day. The gate found live, self-inflicted staleness within a minute of being connected — which is the whole argument for connecting it |
| **What shipped** | A `measurement` CI job on every push and PR: `baseline_census.py` `--no-db --check` (the drift assertion), plus `coupling.py`, `boundary_readings.py` and `doc_register.py`. All four scripts now derive `ROOT` from `__file__` with a `GRC_ROOT` override, and a bogus root **fails loudly** rather than measuring the wrong tree — verified. `coupling.py` and `boundary_readings.py` gained **non-vacuity floors** that exit 1 when the scan collapses, proven against a two-module synthetic tree; F2-69 is the precedent, where a broken entity predicate published 417 for a tree of 413 |
| **⚠ Deliberately NOT a byte-diff of the generated artefacts** | `coupling.json` moves with every code change and `DOCUMENT_REGISTER.csv` embeds git dates, so `git diff --exit-code` would fail on essentially every commit and be switched off within a week — §1c's rule again. The drift that matters is *a published figure going stale*, and `--no-db --check` asserts exactly that. The artefacts are uploaded for diagnosis instead |
| **Relationship to the Java gate** | `MeasuredBaselineFreshnessTest` already gates six source-derived figures inside `mvn verify`, so this job is the belt to that braces: it covers the figures the test does not, and — the part a test cannot do — it catches **the scripts themselves breaking** |
| **Also measured in passing** | `doc_register.py` reports **352** tracked `.md` files against **1,413** on disk: **1,061** are duplicates inside `.claude/worktrees/`. Not acted on — removing another session's worktrees is not mine to do — but it explains why on-disk document counts have never matched tracked ones |
| **Lesson** | When a guard is not enforced, ask *what stopped it* before rebuilding it. The answer here was six characters of Windows path in three files, and the fix took minutes; the assumed answer (a database CI cannot have) would have justified leaving the row open indefinitely. ⚠ And check whether **your own** recent work inherited the defect — mine had, within the hour |

## F2-135 — "Which Saudi pack governs" is a false binary: neither generation contains the other

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-135 *(found implementing W0-6; the row asked for a decision whose premise the measurement refutes — **F2-34's shape**)* |
| **Module** | `Saudi-GRC-Platform-Integration/` vs `docs/Saudi-GRC-Platform-Integration/` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a regulatory deliverable pack with two live generations and no stated authority; picking the wrong one silently deletes three sections |
| **Current State** | ⚠ **OPEN — owner decision (Product/Architecture seat).** The *harm* is closed: both packs are now stamped as point-in-time with the split of authority named on every file, so no reader is misled today. What governs is still undecided |
| **What the row asked for** | *"decide which Saudi pack generation governs and stamp the other `SUPERSEDED BY`"* — which presumes one supersedes the other |
| **Measured — the two packs** | **PACK A** `Saudi-GRC-Platform-Integration/`: 20 files, 4,267 lines *(4,347 once W0-6 stamped them)*, 2026-05-11–05-14, filenames `product-blueprint.md`. **PACK B** `docs/Saudi-GRC-Platform-Integration/`: 33 files, 4,208 lines *(4,340 once stamped)*, **all** 2026-05-18, filenames `01_Product_Blueprint.md`. Same 16-section taxonomy, **one** shared relative path. B is 7 days newer, has 13 more files, and is deeper on every shared section |
| **⚠ Why "B governs" is wrong** | **B does not contain A.** Section-level containment, measured: A holds **three sections B dropped entirely** — `16-AI-Features` (320 lines), `17-Frontend-Requirements` (306) and `18-Excel-Workbook` (384): **1,010 lines with no counterpart in B**. Worse, B **reuses the number `16`** for different content (`16-Assumptions`). Declaring B governing would delete the AI-features, frontend-requirements and Excel-workbook deliverables from the governing record, and leave two different "section 16"s in circulation |
| **⚠ Why "A governs" is also wrong** | A is the older, thinner pack on all 16 shared sections, and A is the one whose provenance was **unverifiable** until this row fixed it (it anchored to deleted branch `feat/pdpl-extensions`, now cited as commit `c6f116f`) |
| **So the honest verdict** | Neither. The available evidence supports *exactly one* claim — **both packs are stale point-in-time artefacts, and each is uniquely load-bearing.** "Newer and bigger" is not authority for a regulatory pack; a re-generation that drops sections is a **partial** re-generation, and nothing in either pack says so |
| **What shipped (no decision required)** | All 53 files in both packs carry a dated HISTORIC banner naming the current authority, **plus a per-pack note stating that the other generation exists, that neither supersedes the other, which sections are unique to it, and that the choice is open.** The failure mode W0-6 exists to prevent — a reader citing a stale pack as current — is closed for both packs without pre-empting the owner |
| **The decision, priced** | **(a)** Migrate A's sections 16–18 into B, renumber B's `16-Assumptions`, then stamp A superseded — ~1,010 lines to move, and the only option that leaves one authority. **(b)** Ratify a split: B governs sections 1–15, A governs 16–18 — zero work, but two documents to keep in step forever, which is the RC-A shape this register already tracks. **(c)** Declare both historic and regenerate from the platform as built — largest effort, only option that yields something current, since **both packs are ~3 months stale regardless** |
| **Recommendation** | **(a)**, and it is not close. (b) institutionalises the two-authorities defect; (c) is right eventually but does not need to block this row. Whatever is chosen, **the `16` collision must be resolved either way** — it is the one part of this that is a defect rather than a decision |
| **Lesson** | When a plan row says *"decide which of two things governs"*, **measure containment before deciding**. The word "supersedes" quietly asserts A ⊆ B, and here that assertion was false in both directions — so either answer would have destroyed content, and the fastest route to the wrong answer was to do exactly what the row said. Same shape as F2-34, one layer up: the decision was unmakeable as posed, and saying so is the deliverable |

## F2-136 — A second instruction file, 84% identical and wrong on every figure that mattered

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-136 *(found implementing W0-6; **RC-A** — two authorities for one truth — in the governance layer)* |
| **Module** | `AGENTS.md` — the repo-root instruction file |
| **Severity** | **P2** — an agent that read this file instead of `CLAUDE.md` would have reused a taken migration version against the deployed server |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06.** `AGENTS.md` is now a ~40-line pointer to `CLAUDE.md`. The duplication is **gone rather than refreshed** |
| **What W0-6 asked for** | *"Fix or delete `AGENTS.md` (wrong remote)"* — and the remote **had** been fixed, on 2026-07-25 in `99a719da`. The row stayed open, and that turned out to be right for a reason the row did not state |
| **⚠ Fixing the remote closed the symptom and left the mechanism** | The file was `CLAUDE.md` copied verbatim with a blind `Claude`→`Codex` substitution. Correcting one invented remote fixed **one wrong fact out of four** and left a duplicated instruction file with **nothing keeping it in sync**. Six weeks later, measured: |
| **The drift, measured 2026-08-06** | **ratchet ≤ 62** vs enforced **21** (three times the real ceiling — invites waving through endpoints the build rejects) · **`next free V296`** vs head **V358** (V296 taken since 2026-07-13 — **a Flyway checksum failure on the deployed server**) · **94 permissions / 24 modules** vs **114 / 31** (scopes 35 packages out of the RBAC catalogue, the test bar and every plan) · remote `Codex-GRC-2` where the real remote is `Claude-GRC-2` (fixed 2026-07-25) |
| **⚠ Why nobody noticed** | The copy still shared **83.6%** of its substantive lines verbatim with `CLAUDE.md` (107 of 128). It read as *"basically the same file"* — while the four figures that could break a deployment had all diverged. **The prose stayed identical and the numbers rotted**, which is the worst possible combination for a reviewer skimming two files side by side |
| **Where the numbers came from** | Every one had been **true in `CLAUDE.md` once**, was found false by this programme, and was fixed **there only**. F2-69 corrected the module count; the ratchet line carries its own ⚠ about having said 62 for months; the migration head is re-read from the directory precisely because prose goes stale. **A copy does not receive corrections** — that is the whole finding |
| **What shipped** | `AGENTS.md` reduced to a pointer that states the rule, tabulates what the copy had drifted to and why each figure was dangerous, and sends the reader to `CLAUDE.md`. **A pointer cannot drift.** `GovernanceDocumentIntegrityTest` enforces it: substantive-line overlap with `CLAUDE.md` must stay ≤ **25%** — empirical and mutation-proof in both directions, since the copy at `99a719da` measures **83.6%** and the pointer measures **0.0%** |
| **⚠ The stated acceptance criterion was unsatisfiable** | W0-6's acceptance is `grep -rl "Codex-GRC-2" .` → empty — but after the fix the **only** files naming that remote are the four findings documents *reporting the defect*. Satisfying the grep literally means **deleting the evidence**. The test asserts the rule that actually protects a reader instead: *the fake remote is never named without the real one in the same file* |
| **Lesson** | **Deleting a duplicate beats syncing it.** When a row says "fix or delete", and the thing is a copy of a file under active correction, *fix* is the trap — it leaves the copy alive to drift again. Also: **an acceptance criterion written as a `grep` over a repository that documents its own defects will be unsatisfiable**, because the remediation and the report contain the same string. State the invariant, not the absence |

## F2-137 — A second whistleblower intake exists, and it never leaves the browser

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-137 *(found implementing F2-83, 2026-08-06)* |
| **Module** | `pages/portal/SpeakUpPortalPage.tsx` vs `lib/intake.ts` |
| **Severity** | **P1** on a Nazaha / SAMA CG Art.28 / CMA CG Art.87 mandated channel — a report filed through it reaches nobody and is destroyed by clearing site data |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06** (no migration). `/portal/speakup` is retired: the route now redirects to the real page, `/ethics/whistleblower/submit`, **carrying the query string across** — since F2-83 made `?org=` load-bearing, a plain `<Navigate>` would have silently converted a link a bank had already published into a 404. `SpeakUpPortalPage.tsx` (282 lines) is deleted, so the platform has **one** whistleblower channel and it posts to the server. Chose retiring over the finding's option (a) *rewire*: rewiring would have left two routes doing the identical thing, and the duplication was the defect |
| **What stopped it being seen** | Two green ratchets were looking straight at it. W2-1b's `demoFallbackContract` matches `/mock/i` **in the import path**, and `lib/intake.ts` is named after the domain concept it fakes rather than after being fake. F2-07b's `routeReachability` asks whether a route is *reachable* — and this one was perfectly reachable. **Neither asked whether the page reaches a server**, which is the question that mattered |
| **What shipped to keep it shut** | `publicPortalBackedByServer.test.ts` (5 checks): no page on a `/portal/*` or `/public/*` route may import a client-only store, with named exemptions that must each name the finding blocking them. It **measures its own premise** — one check proves `lib/intake` really makes zero network calls, so if it ever grows a `fetch` the exemptions stop being justified by silence. Mutation-proven: removing the `/portal/track` exemption fails the offender check |
| **⚠ And the guard shipped with the defect it was written to catch** | The first version anchored on the whole `import` statement, and `[^;\n]*?` cannot cross a newline — so `CaseTrackerPage`'s multi-line `import { useIntake, INTAKE_CATALOG, … } from '../../lib/intake'` was invisible to it. It surfaced as the exemption check calling a live exemption spent, but the **offender** check shared the hole: a page importing `useIntake` across two lines would have passed clean. Now anchored on the `from '…'` clause, which is always one line, with a test asserting the matcher sees both shapes |
| **What is there** | The platform routes **two** whistleblower intake surfaces. `/ethics/whistleblower/submit` — `WhistleblowerSubmitPublicPage.tsx` — posts to the real backend (`whistleblowerApi.publicSubmit`) and is what F2-83 fixed. `/portal/speakup` — `SpeakUpPortalPage` — calls `useIntake().submit` from `lib/intake.ts`, which makes **zero** network calls: it appends to React state and persists to `localStorage`. Its own doc comment says so outright — *"all flow through here so the triager inbox can see them **without a backend round-trip**"* |
| **Consequence** | A report submitted at `/portal/speakup` never reaches the server, so no ethics officer on any other machine can see it, it is absent from `ethics.whistleblower_case`, it is outside the audit ledger, it never starts an SLA clock, and it is gone when the browser clears storage. The reporter is shown a reference code, which makes it indistinguishable from a real submission — **the failure is silent on both sides** |
| **Why it survived** | It is not a missing feature; it is a *demo* fixture wired into a production route. `lib/intake.ts` is honest about being seed + overlay, and for the triager-inbox demo that is fine. What is not fine is a public route pointing at it. Related to **F2-15** (26 remaining mock import sites) and **W2-1b**, whose ratchet bans `/mock/i` imports outside test files — `lib/intake.ts` is named neither `mock` nor `__fixtures__`, so the ratchet does not see it |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a duplicate of F2-83. F2-83 was *"the report goes to the wrong tenant"*; this is *"the report goes nowhere at all"*. The two are independent, and F2-83's own Business Impact section — Bank B's report landing in Bank A's tenant — describes a path that **cannot occur through this surface**, because nothing is written to any tenant |
| **Recommendation** | Decide which surface is the product. Either (a) point `/portal/speakup` at `whistleblowerApi.publicSubmit` with a `?org=` code, reusing everything F2-83 built, and keep `lib/intake.ts` for the demo inbox only; or (b) retire the route. **(a)** is small — the real client already exists and the page's form fields map onto `CaseSubmissionRequest` — and it is the only option that leaves the platform with one whistleblower channel |
| **Effort** | **S** for (a). The backend is done; this is a page swapping one call for another |
| **References** | `pages/portal/SpeakUpPortalPage.tsx` · `pages/ethics/WhistleblowerSubmitPublicPage.tsx` · `lib/intake.ts` · [[F2-83]] · [[F2-15]] |
| **Lesson** | **Two surfaces for one regulatory channel is one surface too many, and the mock is always the one that looks finished.** Found only by asking who calls the endpoint being fixed — the defect is invisible from either the backend (which has a correct, tested submit path) or the page (which renders a convincing confirmation with a reference code). Worth pairing with the F2-83 finding that its own defect was covered by *passing* tests: **neither a green suite nor a working screen is evidence that the two are connected** |

## F2-138 — A data subject cannot exercise a PDPL right: every DSR endpoint requires a staff permission

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-138 *(found closing F2-137, 2026-08-06 — it is the reason the mock existed)* |
| **Module** | `DataOfficerController#createDsr` · `PrivacyController#createDsr` · `lib/intake.ts` · `DsrPortalPage.tsx` |
| **Severity** | **P1** on PDPL Articles 4 and 22, which grant the *data subject* the right to request access, correction, deletion and portability. The platform records DSRs; it has no way for the person holding the right to raise one |
| **Current State** | ⚠ **OPEN.** Not built here: this needs a public endpoint with tenancy, rate limiting and the Article 10 clock, plus a decision about identity verification — not a front-end rewire |
| **Measured** | Both create paths are permission-gated: `POST /api/v1/data-officer/dsr` requires `PRIVACY:WRITE` (or `PLATFORM_ADMIN`), and `POST /api/v1/privacy/dsrs` requires `PRIVACY:WRITE`. **No `permitAll` DSR submission endpoint exists anywhere.** An external data subject has no account, so under the shipped authorisation model they cannot submit a request at all |
| **⚠ The asymmetry that gives it away** | `GET /api/v1/intake/track/**` **is** `permitAll` — a public tracker — while `POST /api/v1/intake` requires `INTAKE:WRITE`. So the platform ships a way to *follow* an anonymous submission and no way to *make* one. A public read whose matching public write does not exist is a strong signal that the channel was designed and then only half built |
| **How the gap was hidden** | `DsrPortalPage.tsx` at `/portal/dsr` renders a complete DSR self-service form and returns a reference code. It writes to `localStorage` via `lib/intake.ts`, which makes **zero** network calls. So the capability *appears* delivered from the outside, and the missing endpoint is invisible from either end — the same shape as [[F2-137]], one channel over. `/portal/track` has the same backing |
| **Consequence if a real request arrives** | The request reaches nobody, no row exists in the privacy module, and **the Article 10 clock never starts** — which is the clock [[F2-123]] was raised to fix. F2-123 made the deadline count from actual receipt rather than data entry; that fix is correct and still cannot help a request that was never recorded. Two findings on the same statutory deadline, one about *when* the clock starts and one about *whether* it starts at all |
| **Recommendation** | Build `POST /api/v1/privacy/dsrs/public` on the F2-83 pattern, which now exists and is proven: opaque `?org=` short code, refuse unknown/inactive with an identical 404, resolve through `PublicPortalTenantResolver`. Then point `DsrPortalPage.tsx` at it and delete its exemption in `publicPortalBackedByServer.test.ts` — **deleting that exemption is the acceptance test.** Reuse the whistleblower portal's rate limiter; the endpoint is a spam target |
| **Open questions for the owner** | (1) **Identity verification** — PDPL requires the controller to verify the requester before disclosing personal data. A public form cannot; the realistic answer is submit-then-verify, with disclosure gated on verification rather than on submission. (2) Whether `lib/intake.ts` becomes the one public intake surface for all kinds (DSR, whistleblower, VRP) or each keeps its own endpoint — the whistleblower one already exists and works, so a single generic endpoint would mean retiring a working one |
| **Effort** | **M.** The endpoint is small; verification policy and rate limiting are the real work |
| **References** | `DataOfficerController#createDsr` · `PrivacyController#createDsr` · `IntakeController` · `publicPortalBackedByServer.test.ts` · [[F2-137]] · [[F2-123]] · [[F2-83]] |
| **Lesson** | **A public read with no matching public write is a half-built channel, and it reads as a finished one.** Found by asking, for a page that submits, *what endpoint does this actually reach* — and then, when the answer was "none", checking whether one could exist. Also: **a convincing form is not evidence of a capability.** The DSR portal looks more finished than the whistleblower portal did, and it is the one with nothing behind it |

## F2-139 — The BCP binder feature had never produced a binder, and its register was seeded to look as though it had

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-139 *(found fixing **F2-78**, 2026-08-06 — two defects wearing one 500)* |
| **Module** | `BcpBinderService` · `bcm.bcp_binder_export` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a regulator-facing artefact (the offline BCP binder a crisis team carries) that could not be produced, in a register that showed four of them |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06** (no migration). Generation works: live, a real binder rendered **47,549 bytes / 12 pages** of `%PDF-` and downloaded with the counter incrementing |
| **⚠ Defect 1 — generation always threw, on every call** | `generate` saved the row **twice**: a pre-save "so we have an external_id + DB-side id before render", then again after the render. `BcpBinderExport.row_version` is a SQL Server ROWVERSION mapped `@Version @Column(insertable = false, updatable = false)`, so the **database** assigns it and Hibernate cannot predict the value the first flush produced — the second UPDATE carried the pre-insert version in its WHERE clause, matched nothing, and threw `StaleObjectStateException`. **This is the ROWVERSION double-flush landmine `CLAUDE.md` documents**, and it had never been caught here because nobody had ever successfully pressed the button |
| **The pre-save's three reasons all dissolved on inspection** | (1) *"so we have an external_id"* — `externalId` defaults to `UUID.randomUUID()` in the entity, so it exists before any save, and it is what the file path is built from. (2) *"a DB-side id before render"* — the render does not use the id. (3) *"This is what the UI polls on (GENERATING / READY)"* — **the render is synchronous**: the controller returns 202 with the terminal status already set, so no client can ever observe GENERATING. The comment described an asynchronous design this code is not. Each path now saves exactly once |
| **⚠ Defect 2 — the register was fabricated, which is why defect 1 was invisible** | Four seeded rows, and every tell is there: `byte_size` of **2,915,000 / 2,310,000 / 2,780,000**, page counts 84/66/80, durations 4200/3600/4100 ms, **download counts of 6, 4 and 11 for files that never existed**, `generated_by_name` = *"Director of Business Continuity"* (a job title, not a username), all four rows created inside the same **4 milliseconds**, and `file_path` in the shape `binders/<year>/<code>_<slug>_<version>.pdf` — a scheme `generate` does not produce (it writes `binders/<org>/<uuid>.pdf`). `uploads/` has **never** contained a `binders` directory. **The register showed a working feature; the feature had never run.** The landmine `CLAUDE.md` states as *"don't seed an empty table before checking it's wired"* |
| **What F2-78 reported, and how it was wrong** | *"Returns 500 for every caller against three real rows, `language=BILINGUAL` is the place to start."* The rows are not real, `BILINGUAL` is irrelevant (a BILINGUAL binder renders fine — it is what the live probe generated), and the third row is a correct **409** (`ARCHIVED`), not a 500. **The stated hypothesis pointed at the export/RTL history because that is where this repo's scar tissue is** — the actual cause was in the log, and reproducing before theorising found it in one grep |
| **Also fixed: the message told the caller to do the one thing that cannot work** | A READY binder with no artefact answered *"PDF export failed / Document generation failed. Please try again or contact your administrator."* Nothing was generating, and retrying can never help. Now a **409** naming the binder, the real cause, that retrying will not help, and the remedy — and it deliberately does **not** silently rebuild the file, because a binder is the immutable record of what was distributed offline. A genuine unreadable-but-present file still raises `ExportException` and a 500, which is what that exception is for |
| **The commercial sting stands and sharpens** | V320 Part 3 granted `CISO` and `CFO` a **module-wide** `BCM:EXPORT` specifically to preserve access to this endpoint — and that grant is what inverted three other BCM guards (**F2-61**). *A permission was widened, and a defect class opened, to keep working something that had never worked once.* Nothing measured whether the endpoint served bytes, because the question asked was **who may call it** |
| **⚠ And its tests had pinned the double-save as correct** | Three assertions read `verify(binders, times(2)).save(...)`. The double flush *was* the defect, so asserting it locked it in; they now assert exactly one save, with a comment saying why. **The fourth instance this programme has found of a passing test specifying the defect** (after F2-83's two org-1 tests and F2-80's name-leak assertion) |
| **Not done, deliberately** | The four fabricated rows are **left in dev**. Deleting seed data is a destructive DB operation and needs the owner's word; and they are now the only live example of the refusal path, which is worth keeping until the seed script that created them is fixed. The right fix is in the seeder, not a DELETE |
| **Lesson** | **Reproduce before theorising, and read the log before the plan.** The row named a plausible cause from this repo's own history (Arabic/RTL export) and the truth was an optimistic-lock exception sitting in the server log. Also: **a register full of plausible rows is a claim that a feature works, and it is the cheapest claim in the codebase to fabricate** — round byte counts and a job title where a username belongs were visible without running anything |

## F2-140 — Three live tables and an unknown number of live columns are created by no migration, and F2-70 read as closed

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-140 *(measured while advancing [[F2-77]] past V113, 2026-08-06)* |
| **Category** | Deployment integrity — the schema the migration set produces is not the schema the platform runs |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a fresh installation built from the migration folder would be missing tables the application reads and columns it queries. Latent while every environment descends from a restored snapshot; structural the first time anyone provisions from scratch |
| **Provenance** | Not sought. F2-77's V113 step failed on `evidence.evidence_items.review_due_date`, and the file's own comment claimed *"the column was already there per V47"*. It was not — so the question became *what else does the platform have that no migration makes?* |
| **⚠ F2-70 read as closed and was an undercount** | F2-70 was *"the 67 tables have no DDL"*, closed by the V341/V342 spine bootstrap and proven by two ratchets now reading zero. Measured again from the other direction: **3 live tables are created by no migration at all** — `compliance.compliance_assessments` (**10 rows**), `compliance.compliance_findings` (**18 rows**), `compliance.evidence_items` (**83 rows**). None is among the spine's 67. The ratchets are not wrong; they assert that every **mapped** table has runnable DDL, and these three have no JPA entity, so nothing was ever asked about them |
| **⚠ The aggregate matched perfectly while the membership did not** | The migration set CREATEs **470** distinct tables. The live database holds **470** base tables. **The totals are identical and 3 differ in each direction** — which is exactly the shape a count-based check cannot see. Anyone comparing the two numbers would have concluded the folder was complete |
| ✔ **Now gated, 2026-08-06 ([[F2-09]])** | Three assertions in `SchemaContractRatchetTest` pin both directions **by name**, hermetically — the generated register against the migration folder, no database, so they run inside `mvn -o clean package`. The residue can now only shrink, and a fourth bootstrap-only table fails the build on the commit that adds it rather than on the first customer provisioning. 🔑 **And a third assertion makes the toleration honest**: ADR-017 D-2 chose *no DDL* for these three because nothing reads them, so the gate refuses to let any of them **acquire a reader** — the moment one gains a JPA entity or a Java reference, "no migration creates it" stops being dead ballast and becomes [[F2-146]]'s broken fresh install. ⚠ The plausible way to break the gate is treating `rows_dev = 0` as absent: **45 of the 470 live tables are empty** (including `bcm.crisis_comms_cascade`, [[F2-147]]), and that edit moves all 45 into the orphan list. Mutation-tested **6/6**, including that one |
| ⚠ **And then the real two-database diff refuted that gate's other half (2026-08-06)** | `schema_convergence.py --stop 320` reported **0 extra tables**, while the hermetic gate tolerated three — `risk.kri_definitions`, `kri_readings`, `kri_breach_events` — as *“created by a migration and absent from dev”*, with a javadoc asserting *“the drop lives in a bootstrap script”*. **It does not: V247 and V337 drop them as migrations.** The scan matched `CREATE TABLE` and never `DROP TABLE`, so it saw V1's create and none of the retirement. Now DROP-aware, ordered by **(version, offset-in-file)**, and the tolerated set is **empty** — a hard zero. 🔑 **A version-only rule invents two defects**: `bcm.crisis_events` (V102) and `platform.controls` (V93) are `DROP; CREATE` within a single file, so comparing versions alone reads them as dropped, and both are live, mapped and populated. **A scan can be wrong in the alarming direction too, and that costs a reviewer's trust just as fast.** Mutation-tested 3/3 |
| **The other three are correct history, and that was checked** | `risk.kri_definitions`, `risk.kri_readings` and `risk.kri_breach_events` are created by V1 and exist in no live database — because **V247 and V337 drop them** (the ERM E-E KRI consolidation). Created then dropped is what an ordered history is *supposed* to look like, so this side is not a defect and is recorded so the 470-vs-470 symmetry is not mistaken for one |
| **The column class, and why its size is not yet knowable** | `evidence.evidence_items.review_due_date` is created by nothing: V8 creates `evidence.evidence_items` with 31 columns and no such column, V197 creates a same-named column on a **different** table, and the live column is `DATE NULL` with **77 populated rows**. A crude name-scan finds **22** live columns whose name appears nowhere in any of the 348 files (11 on `compliance.findings`, 8 on `evidence.evidence_items`, 3 on `compliance.evidence_items`). **That is a lower bound, and `evidence.evidence_items.review_due_date` proves it**: its name *does* appear, so name-matching missed it — a column can be referenced without ever being created on its table. The exact figure needs the chain to run to completion and then a schema diff, which is **[[F2-09]]**, which is blocked on **[[F2-77]]** |
| **⚠ The cause is one sentence, and it is in the code** | V113's header reads: *"A surprising amount of the schema was already in place (… EvidenceItem chain-of-custody + `review_due_date` + WORM/hash …)"*. It **was** already in place — **in the database**. The author checked the database and not the migration history, and the folder has been maintained that way throughout. That is F2-77's thesis stated by one of its own files |
| **Recommendation** | Two separable decisions. **(a) The three tables are an owner call, not a fix**: they carry 111 rows between them, have no JPA entity, and sit beside live siblings (`compliance.assessments` exists as well as `compliance.compliance_assessments`). Either they are legacy to be dropped — in which case a migration should drop them and the row closes — or they are load-bearing, in which case they need DDL *and* an entity. Guessing either way writes the wrong migration. **(b) The columns are a fix**, and the pattern is settled: create each where the feature that needs it first appears, guarded, as this slice did for `evidence.evidence_items.review_due_date` in V113. Both are below Flyway baseline 292, so prod never runs them |
| **Effort** | **S** per column once F2-09 can enumerate them; **owner decision** for the three tables |
| **Verification** | Reproducible in one query each: `INFORMATION_SCHEMA.TABLES` against the CREATE TABLE set parsed from the folder; and for columns, the name-scan above. The authoritative version is F2-09's schema diff against a chain-built database |
| **Status** | ⚠ **OPEN.** The `evidence.evidence_items.review_due_date` instance is fixed (V113, this slice). The three tables and the remaining columns are not |
| **References** | [[F2-77]] · [[F2-70]] · [[F2-09]] · `Appendix/scripts/chain_proof.py` · V8 · V113 · V341/V342 |
| **Lesson** | **A closed row can be an undercount, and the instrument that closed it will not say so** — F2-70's ratchets ask whether every *mapped* table has DDL, and a table with no entity is invisible to that question. Also, twice over in one measurement: **an aggregate that matches is not membership that matches** (470 = 470, three wrong each way), and **a name-scan is a lower bound, never a count** — `evidence.evidence_items.review_due_date` is referenced in two files and created in none |

## F2-78 — The BCP binder download returned 500 for every caller, including admin

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-78 *(found live-probing exports, 2026-07-31; spec written 2026-08-06 when the row was worked — it had lived only in a plan cell, which is [[F2-79]])* |
| **Category** | Broken feature + error contract. Two defects wearing one status code |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the offline BCP binder is the artefact a crisis team carries when systems are down, and it could not be produced at all |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06** (no migration). Live: a real binder renders **47,549 bytes / 12 pages** of `%PDF-` and downloads with the counter incrementing |
| **What the row claimed, and what was true** | *"500 for every caller against three real rows; `language=BILINGUAL` is the place to start."* The hypothesis pointed at this repo's Arabic/RTL export history, which is where its scar tissue is. Reproducing first and reading the server log found something else: **the rows are not real, `language=BILINGUAL` is irrelevant** (a BILINGUAL binder is what the fix's probe generated), and the third row is a correct **409** (`ARCHIVED`), not a 500 |
| **⚠ Defect 1 — generation had never once succeeded** | `BcpBinderService.generate` saved the row **twice** around the render: a pre-save "so we have an external_id + DB-side id", then again after. `BcpBinderExport.row_version` is a SQL Server ROWVERSION mapped `@Version @Column(insertable = false, updatable = false)`, so the database assigns it and Hibernate cannot predict what the first flush produced — the second UPDATE carried the pre-insert version in its WHERE clause, matched nothing, and threw `StaleObjectStateException`. **The ROWVERSION double-flush landmine, on every call.** The pre-save's three stated reasons all dissolve: `externalId` defaults to `UUID.randomUUID()` in the entity (and is what the file path is built from), the render never uses the DB id, and the render is **synchronous** so no client can observe GENERATING — the comment described an asynchronous design the code is not |
| **⚠ Defect 2 — the register was fabricated, which is why defect 1 was invisible** | Four seeded rows, every tell present: `byte_size` of 2,915,000 / 2,310,000 / 2,780,000, page counts 84/66/80, durations 4200/3600/4100 ms, **download counts of 6, 4 and 11 for files that never existed**, `generated_by_name` = *"Director of Business Continuity"* (a job title, not a username), all four created inside the same **4 milliseconds**, and a `file_path` scheme `BcpBinderService.generate` does not produce. `uploads/` has never held a `binders` directory. Recorded separately as [[F2-139]] |
| **Also fixed: the message told the caller to do the one thing that cannot work** | A READY binder with no artefact answered *"PDF export failed / Document generation failed. Please try again or contact your administrator."* Nothing was generating and retrying cannot help. Now a **409** naming the binder, the cause, that retrying will not help, and the remedy — and it deliberately does **not** rebuild the file, because a binder is the immutable record of what was distributed offline. A file that exists and cannot be read still raises `ExportException` and a 500, which is what that exception is for |
| **⚠ Three of its own tests had pinned the double-save as correct** | `verify(binders, times(2)).save(...)`. The double flush *was* the defect, so asserting it locked it in. They now assert exactly one save. **The fourth instance this programme has found of a passing test specifying the defect**, after [[F2-83]]'s two organisation-1 tests and [[F2-80]]'s name-leak assertion |
| **The commercial sting** | V320 Part 3 granted `CISO` and `CFO` a **module-wide** `BCM:EXPORT` specifically to preserve access to this endpoint — and that grant is what inverted three other BCM guards ([[F2-61]]). *A permission was widened, and a defect class opened, to keep working something that had never worked once.* Nothing measured whether the endpoint served bytes, because the question asked was **who may call it** |
| **Verification** | Live 16/16; suite 3,334/0/0. The four fabricated rows are deliberately left in dev — deleting seed data needs the owner's word, and the fix belongs in the seeder |
| **References** | `BcpBinderService#download` · `BcpBinderController#download` · [[F2-139]] · [[F2-61]] · [[F2-80]] · [[F2-83]] |
| **Lesson** | **Reproduce before theorising, and read the log before the plan.** The row named a plausible cause from this repo's own history and the truth was an optimistic-lock exception sitting in the server log. Also: **a register full of plausible rows is a claim that a feature works, and it is the cheapest claim in the codebase to fabricate** — round byte counts and a job title where a username belongs were visible without running anything |

## F2-79 — The register lagged the plan by 40 specs, and the line counting the lag grepped one hash too many

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-79 *(derived, not observed — the count was recomputed and disagreed with the document asserting it)* |
| **Category** | Measurement integrity, on this programme's own instruments |
| **Severity** | **P3** documentation debt — but the *cause* is the recurring class this register exists to record |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06.** Re-measured and gated: `FindingsRegisterCoverageTest` (4 checks) fails the build if the owed count grows, if either scan collapses, **or if the heading pattern stops matching at all** |
| **The defect** | The doc-map cell asserted *"`F2-54`…`F2-63` have ZERO entries in the register (grep: 0 hits each)"*. The grep was for `### F2-`; the register writes `## F2-… · Title` — **one hash too many.** So it reported zero for **every** id in the file, including the 54 that were present, and the conclusion drawn was wrong in the direction that looks like diligence |
| **⚠ Why this is a class, not a typo** | **A query against the wrong shape returns a clean-looking ZERO rather than an error.** [[F2-72]]'s comparator dissolved its own definition and read green. [[F2-01]]'s prose count-gate produced 69 false positives. [[F2-140]]'s aggregate matched at 470-vs-470 while three tables differed in each direction. **A measurement that cannot fail is indistinguishable from one that passes** |
| **Re-measured 2026-08-06** | The plan references **137** distinct `F2-*` ids; the register carries **107** specs; **33 owed** — **31** after this slice wrote the two specs it owed (F2-78 and this row). (The row said 40 of 94/57 when written — the gap narrowed as specs were added, and it is re-derived by the gate rather than restated). Also **3 specced but never referenced** by the plan (F2-47, F2-113, F2-124) — the mirror debt, capped rather than demanded to zero. ⚠ Confirmed the defect is still live: `### F2-` returns **0 hits** against the current register |
| **What shipped** | `FindingsRegisterCoverageTest`. Deliberately a **ratchet, not zero**: 31 specs are owed, and demanding zero would block unrelated work on months of accumulated paperwork — the corrected rule is narrower, *a spec is owed before a finding is WORKED*. The load-bearing check is `theHeadingPatternActuallyMatchesTheRegister()`, which exists **purely so a pattern that stops matching fails loudly instead of reporting a tidy zero owed** — the exact failure mode of the line it replaces |
| **⚠ And this slice was contributing to the debt while paying it** | Specs were written for every finding *discovered* in this session (F2-135…F2-140) while [[F2-77]] and [[F2-78]] were closed or advanced with **plan rows only**. Two of the owed 33 were mine. [[F2-78]]'s spec is written in this slice; F2-77's is owed until that row closes |
| **Why it is worth a gate at all** | **The register outlives the plan and is what an external auditor is handed.** A finding whose only record is a plan cell disappears the moment the plan is superseded — and this programme has already superseded three planning documents |
| **References** | `FindingsRegisterCoverageTest` · [[F2-72]] · [[F2-01]] · [[F2-140]] |
| ⚠ **What the mutation run proved** | Breaking both scans at once (`## F2-` → `### F2-`, `F2-` → `FX-`) gives **4 run, 2 failures** — and the two that fail are the *instrument* checks. The owed ratchet read **0 owed**; the orphan ratchet read **0 orphans**. With nothing matching on either side both set differences are empty, so **both ratchets were perfectly green while both scans were dead** — which is not a hypothetical about this gate, it is what the line it replaces actually did. **A ratchet cannot detect its own blindness**, because the direction a broken scan fails in is always the reassuring one |
| **Lesson** | **Assert the instrument, not only the number.** A count of zero and a broken pattern look identical from the outside, so the fix is a test that the pattern still matches something — not a more careful reading of the count. The general form: **any check whose failure mode is silence needs a second check that it can still speak** |

## F2-38 — A fabricated user directory reached every page, and the notification API behind it served two fields that had no columns

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-38 *(found by F2-26 while building the committee seat picker; worked 2026-08-06)* |
| **Category** | Fabricated data reaching a live surface + a DTO that could not tell the truth |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the server half is a bilingual defect on an Arabic-primary product; the frontend half is fabricated identities on every page |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06, both halves** (no migration). The bell reads the server; the fabricated-directory import count went **8 → 6** and `UserPicker.tsx` is deleted. Live 17/17 + browser-verified. ⚠ What remains is **not** this row: [[F2-142]] owns the fabricated multi-channel outbox, and the six remaining directory importers are that one cluster |
| **⚠ Three of the row's own statements were wrong** | (i) *"Consumers to fix: `ActivityFeedDrawer`, `NotificationOutboxPage`"* — an **undercount of four times**: **8** files import `lib/userDirectory`, including `Topbar.tsx`, which puts invented identities on **every page in the product**. (ii) *"`UserPicker.tsx` reads it"* — true, but the picker is **dead**: the only `<UserPicker` occurrence in the repository is inside its own doc comment. The fix is deletion, not rewiring; only `Avatar`, exported from the same file, has real consumers (5). (iii) The directory's `onCall` flag is commented *"for crisis routing"*, which reads as fabricated phone numbers in a crisis path — **it is consumed only inside the dead picker.** Nothing routes on it. *I nearly wrote that sentence as a finding; measuring refuted it* |
| **🔑 What measuring actually found** | Three consuming libs (`lib/notificationOutbox`, `lib/activityLog`, `lib/recordRegistry`) make **zero** network calls, and `platform.notifications` holds **445 real rows the UI never shows** — `admin` alone has **57 unread of 75**, served by a working `isAuthenticated()` endpoint. **CE-10 shipped the change engine's ability to reach people two commits earlier, and the bell reads a different store, so nobody can see it.** The row was filed about a picker; the defect was a subsystem |
| **The server defect — null for four of ten fields** | `NotificationService.toResponse` passed `null` for `severity` and `linkUrl`, which **are not columns on the table at all**: declared on the record and hard-coded null for the endpoint's entire life. For `bodyAr`, which **is** a column — `message_ar` has existed since `V1__core_schemas.sql` and holds real Arabic on **42 of 445 rows** — but **the entity mapped `title_ar` and not this**, so the Arabic body was unreachable from Java entirely. Notifications were *half* bilingual, against house rule 7.
