And `entityType`/`entityId` were populated on **all 445** rows and exposed by nothing, which is *why* `linkUrl` existed as a null placeholder: the server never knew the SPA's routes |
| **⚠ Why severity was deleted rather than populated** | `notification_type` carries **two vocabularies in one column**: 4 of its 16 values are severity-shaped (`INFO/WARNING/DANGER/NOTIFICATION`, 48 rows) and 12 are event types (`EVIDENCE_REQUEST_ESCALATION` alone is 162). Deriving a severity would have assigned one to **397 rows that never had it**. **A fabricated field is worse than an absent one, because it reads as data** — which is the same judgement the rest of this row is about |
| **What shipped** | The record now carries what the table holds and nothing it does not; `entityType`/`entityId` replace `linkUrl` so the *client* builds the route, keeping frontend routing in the frontend. **Live 4/4**: `severity` and `linkUrl` gone from the response, `entityType` present on 75/75, and the Arabic body served for the first time — *"ثغرات حرجة مفتوحة لأكثر من 30 يومًا."* |
| **The gate** | `NotificationResponseMappingTest` (4 checks). The load-bearing one walks the record's components **by reflection** and fails on any that returns null from a fully-populated row, so **adding a field and forgetting to map it fails the build** — ten hand-written assertions would have fixed today's ten fields without preventing the eleventh. Mutation-proven: reinstating `null` for `messageAr` fails 2 of 4 and the reflective check **names the field**. It uses no mocking framework, deliberately: `NotificationService.toResponse` touches none of the service's dependencies, so mocking a Spring Data repository to prove a field-copy would have tested the stub |
| **⚠ An existing ratchet caught the entity change and its prescription was wrong** | `BilingualInvariantRatchetTest` went 157 → 158 and told me to add `@NotBlank` instead of raising it. Checked before obeying: `markRead()` **saves the entity**, so that constraint makes **403 of 445 notifications impossible to mark as read**. Recorded in `DATA_BLOCKED` at 403/445, the worst ratio in that list. The 39 call sites cannot supply Arabic either — the three notify methods take `titleAr` and **no Arabic body**, so the signature is where the gap lives. Produced [[F2-141]] |
| **Deliberately not done** | Arabic copy for 39 call sites across 14 files. That needs real Saudi GRC Arabic, and machine-translating 39 governance notifications would be the same defect class this row is about |
| **Verification** | Live 4/4 · suite 3,346/0/0 · mutation 2 of 4 |
| **References** | `NotificationService.toResponse` · `NotificationResponseMappingTest` · [[F2-141]] · [[F2-142]] · [[F2-26]] · [[F2-137]] |
| **Lesson** | **Measure the row before fixing the row.** Three of this one's statements were wrong and the real defect was one layer down, on the server, in a mapper nobody had read. Also: **two fields that can never be non-null survived the endpoint's whole life because nothing compares a DTO to its table** — and the question that found them is this programme's standing one, *what does this claim, and does the data exist?* |

## F2-141 — The bilingual ratchet reads entity declarations, so an unmapped Arabic column is invisible to it

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-141 *(measured 2026-08-06 while resolving the ratchet [[F2-38]] tripped)* |
| **Category** | Measurement integrity — an instrument whose scope is narrower than its subject |
| **Severity** | **P3** — no live exposure; the cost is that a house rule reads as 91% enforced when the denominator is wrong |
| **Current State** | 🔶 **TABLE HALF DECIDED 2026-08-06 (ADR-017 **D-15**); column half closed as “do not map”; the `DROP` is owner-authorised and open.** Bilingual baseline stays **158**, with the reason recorded *in the test* rather than only in a commit message |
| **The defect** | `BilingualInvariantRatchetTest` scans `@Entity` fields for a mandatory `*_en` beside an optional `*_ar` (house rule 7) and ratchets the count downward. It therefore **cannot see a column no entity maps**. Measured from the schema instead: **631** live `*_ar` columns, **11 mapped by no entity at all**, and **5** of those sit beside a `NOT NULL` `*_en` sibling — `bcm_lc.drills.scenario_ar` (0/5 blank), `reporting.report_definitions.report_name_ar` (0/20), `risk_lc.kri_breaches.kri_name_ar` (0/12), plus `privacy_lc.ropa_activities.purposes_ar` and `risk_lc.kri_escalation_log.step_ar` on empty tables. **So 157 was a lower bound and the true figure was at least 163** |
| **🔑 Why the number going UP is not a regression** | `platform.notifications.message_ar` has existed since `V1__core_schemas.sql` beside a `NOT NULL` `message_en`, with Arabic on 42 of 445 rows. [[F2-38]] added the entity field. **The asymmetry is not new — it became visible.** Mapping a column made the number worse and the codebase better, and that is the only shape of ratchet increase worth accepting |
| **The five are unreachable, not unfixable — and that is the opposite prescription** | All five hold **zero** blank rows, so `@NotBlank` would land free *if the field existed*. That distinguishes them from the 35 entries in `DATA_BLOCKED`, which need a tenant-side data fix. **A quieter problem: those are known and blocked; these were not counted at all.** Two of the five are in `privacy_lc` / `risk_lc`, the lifecycle packages `CLAUDE.md` already flags as orphaned, so *whether the column should exist* is part of the question rather than a detail |
| **⚠ Second instance of one class in two days** | [[F2-140]] found the same shape in the schema ratchets: a table with **no JPA entity** was invisible to a control asserting that every *mapped* table has runnable DDL, and F2-70 read as closed. **A control that asks about mapped things cannot see unmapped ones, and its green reading is not evidence of absence** |
| **Recommendation** | ~~Map each of the five~~ — **superseded by the row's own sharpening and then by measurement: do not map them.** A bilingual column on a table no code reads is a field that exists and serves nothing |
| 🔑 **DECIDED 2026-08-06 — ADR-017 D-15, and the population is 36, not 5** | The five were an artifact of *this* instrument: a bilingual scan can only see a dead table that happens to carry an `*_ar` column beside a `NOT NULL` `*_en`. The committed register already classifies **36** tables as `ORPHAN_CONFIRMED`/`SEED_ONLY`/`LEGACY` — no JPA mapping and no Java reference anywhere — holding **381 rows** in dev and the same 381 in production, which inherited them by dump restore. **This row's own lesson, one level up.** Verdict: none of the 36 should exist |
| **✔ What shipped — the schema-side companion scan this row asked for** | Three assertions in `SchemaContractRatchetTest`, DB-free, reading the committed register: no migration above the Flyway baseline may **CREATE** a table nothing maps (88 created above V292, 88 mapped — **hard zero**); none may **INSERT or ALTER** a table the application cannot read (**hard zero**); and the 36 are a **decreasing ratchet**, which also catches the one path the hard gates cannot see — a table added by the Python bootstrap, where there is no migration to fail. **Mutation-proven 6 of 6** |
| **⚠ The verification scan's own bug is the finding** | Re-deriving “no Java reference” independently (1,843 main + 352 test + 778 FE files, comment-stripped, 7 anti-vacuity positives) first reported **5 of the 36 as REFERENCED** — every one a `@Table` in a **different schema with the same table name**: `bcm.bia_records` vs `bcm_lc.bia_records`, `evidence.evidence_items` vs `compliance.evidence_items`, `socops.threat_intel` vs `cyber_lc.threat_intel`, `cyber.cyber_incidents` vs `cyber_lc.cyber_incidents`, `privacy.cross_border_transfers` vs `privacy_lc.cross_border_transfers`. **A schema-blind name check cannot tell a live table from its superseded twin — which is why the twins survived**, and it makes any `DROP` here schema-qualified by necessity. Schema-aware: **36 of 36 clean**. (`table_xref.py` itself does resolve the schema, including the `hibernate.default_schema` fallback — the hole was mine) |
| **⛔ What is NOT done, and why** | The `DROP` (house rule 6: destructive DB operations need the owner's explicit OK) and the writer-removal that must precede it. **24 of the 36 have a writer** — 16 by one of six Python seed scripts, 15 by a migration (V28, V120–V123), 7 by both — so dropping a table whose seed insert still exists breaks the bootstrap. **The writer goes first**, which is [[F2-144]]'s ordering on the data plane |
| **Effort** | **S** per column, plus the companion scan |
| **Verification (table half)** | Suite **3,364/0/0** · mutation **6 of 6** · live **7/7**. The successor claim was made an observation rather than an inference from column lists: `GET /api/ndi/reports` returns **12** rows whose ids are a subset of `ndi.report_definition`'s, not the dead table's 20; the JSON carries `nameEn` and no `reportNameEn`; a row inserted straight into `reporting.report_definitions` is **invisible** to the endpoint and an id existing only there **404s**; the converse control fires (12 → 13). ⚠ **Two probe legs were wrong first, both in the reassuring direction:** `org` came back `None` from a login response that carries no `organisationId`, so leg 1 read as *the endpoint does not serve ndi*; and a `/api/v1/search` leg **passed while proving nothing** because no global search endpoint exists, so the 404 body trivially contained no tag — replaced, not excused |
| **Verification** | Reproducible from the schema: every `*_ar` column whose name appears in no `@Column(name = "…")` in the entity sources, intersected with those whose `*_en` sibling is `NOT NULL`. ⚠ A name-scan is a **lower bound, never a count** — the same caveat [[F2-140]] carries |
| **References** | [[F2-38]] · [[F2-140]] · [[F2-09]] · [[F2-148]] · `BilingualInvariantRatchetTest` · `SchemaContractRatchetTest` · `TABLE_DISPOSITION_REGISTER.csv` · ADR-017 D-15 |
| **Lesson** | **Ask what an instrument's population is, not just what it asserts.** This one asserts a rule over *entity declarations* and was read as asserting it over *the platform*. The gap between those two sentences was six findings wide |

## F2-148 — A first-generation data model was superseded module by module and never removed; ten dead tables share a name with their live successor

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-148 *(measured 2026-08-06 while deciding [[F2-141]]; ADR-017 D-15)* |
| **Category** | Schema archaeology — two generations of the same data model live in one database |
| **Severity** | **P3** — no live exposure (nothing reads them), but it is the standing hazard behind every schema question this programme asks, and it made one of my own scans wrong |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **OPEN as a cleanup; DECIDED as a direction.** D-15 settles that the 36 should not exist; this row is the *why*, and it is what makes the drop a per-schema job rather than a list |
| **The finding** | Ten of the 36 no-code tables have a **live, mapped successor whose table name is a near-match in a different schema**: `reporting.report_definitions` → `ndi.report_definition` · `audit.audit_logs` → `platform.audit_log` · `bcm_lc.bia_records` → `bcm.bia_records` · `bcm_lc.drills` + `bcm_lc.recovery_strategies` → the live `bcm` module · `compliance.compliance_assessments` → `ndi.compliance_assessment` · `compliance.evidence_items` → `evidence.evidence_items` + `ndi.evidence_item` · `cyber_lc.cyber_incidents` → `cyber.cyber_incidents` · `cyber_lc.threat_intel` → `socops.threat_intel` · `privacy.data_flows` → `dataclass.data_flow` · `privacy_lc.cross_border_transfers` → `privacy.cross_border_transfers` · `risk_lc.kri_breaches` → `risk.kri_breach` |
| **🔑 It is a generational predecessor, not a duplicate** | [[F2-141]]'s lead called `reporting.report_definitions` a *superseded duplicate*. Measured, the two share only **seven generic columns** (`id`, `external_id`, `organisation_id`, `report_type`, `created_at`, `created_by`, `is_deleted`); the live `ndi.report_definition` adds `report_code`, `schedule_cron`, `recipients`, `language`, `status` and four `last_run_*` fields. **The successor is strictly more capable**, which is the signature of a rebuild rather than a copy — and it means no data migration is owed, only a drop |
| **The `*_lc` schemas are the same story, and `CLAUDE.md` already half-records it** | `bcm_lc`, `cyber_lc`, `privacy_lc`, `risk_lc` are lifecycle packages created ahead of modules that were then built properly elsewhere. The landmine list says it for one of them — *“Don't 'seed' an empty table before checking it's wired (`privacy_lc` is orphaned)”* — and the same sentence is true of the other three. **The note was right and its scope was a quarter of the truth** |
| **⚠ Why this is more than tidiness: it made a scan of mine wrong** | Verifying “no Java reference” with a matcher that read `@Table(name = "…")` **without the schema** reported 5 of the 36 as REFERENCED — all five matching the *live twin's* entity. A schema-blind name check cannot distinguish them, **and neither can a developer reading the annotation without the schema attribute.** That is the mechanism by which two generations coexisted unnoticed, and it is why a `DROP` here must be schema-qualified: `DROP TABLE evidence_items` against the wrong default schema deletes the live evidence subsystem's table |
| **Recommendation** | Fold into D-15's drop, ordered **per schema, not per table**: retire `*_lc` as four units after confirming each successor covers it, and treat the four pre-modular strays (`audit.audit_logs`, `platform.business_units`, `platform.control_frameworks`, `privacy.*`) individually because their rows may predate the successor. Add the near-name pairing to `table_xref.py`'s output so the successor is named in the register rather than rediscovered |
| **Effort** | **S** for the report; the drop is D-15's and owner-authorised |
| **Verification** | Reproducible: for each no-code table, normalise the name (strip a trailing `s`/`es`) and look for another `sys.tables` row with the same normalised name in a different schema; then read the two column lists side by side. 10 of 36 hit |
| **References** | [[F2-141]] · [[F2-140]] · [[F2-146]] · ADR-017 D-15 · `TABLE_DISPOSITION_REGISTER.csv` |
| **Lesson** | **When two things share a name, the name is the least reliable thing about them.** This platform has one live and one dead table called `evidence_items`, `bia_records`, `threat_intel`, `cyber_incidents` and `cross_border_transfers` — and every instrument that identifies a table by name alone is one default-schema assumption away from acting on the wrong one. Qualify, then act |

## F2-142 — The UI presents a multi-channel notification delivery subsystem with no server, and the browser marks its own messages SENT

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-142 *(found 2026-08-06 measuring [[F2-38]])* |
| **Category** | Fabricated capability on a live route — and a regulatory claim, not a cosmetic one |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a screen reports delivery that never occurred, through channels the platform has no integration with |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06 — option (a), retire.** And the scope was **3 screens + 2 stores**, not the one page this spec describes |
| **The defect** | `/notifications/outbox` is a **live route** rendering `lib/notificationOutbox` — **183 lines of `localStorage` with zero network calls** — which models **eight channels** (`EMAIL`, `SMS`, `WHATSAPP`, `SLACK`, `TEAMS`, `IN_APP`, including **`NAFATH`** and **`TADAWUL_PORTAL`**), a five-state delivery lifecycle (`QUEUED/SENDING/SENT/FAILED/CANCELLED`), a *"channel-routing engine"* mapping priority + recipient role to a channel set, and retry / cancel buttons. `markSent` and `markFailed` are functions **the client calls on itself** |
| **🔑 Why this is more than a mock** | Naming **Nafath** (the Saudi national identity service) and the **Tadawul portal** makes this a claim about regulated delivery channels. A GRC platform whose outbox says `SENT` for a Nafath notification that no system ever sent is making an assertion an examiner would act on. This is the [[F2-42]] / [[F2-137]] / [[F2-139]] class — **a surface asserting that something happened** — and the fourth instance found in this programme |
| **⚠ The real API cannot back it, and that was checked before proposing a repoint** | `NotificationResponse` has **no recipient, no channel and no delivery status**: it is a per-user in-app **inbox**, and it is real (445 rows, 75 for `admin`). The outbox describes a **different subsystem that does not exist**. *An inbox substituted for an outbox would have been a fix that changed the screen without changing the truth* |
| **Recommendation — two defensible options, and one that is not** | **(a) Retire the page**, following the [[F2-137]] precedent: that fake portal was deleted and its route redirected, because a route that works and lies is worse than a 404. **(b) Build delivery for real** and scope the channels deliberately — Nafath and Tadawul are integration projects with their own approvals, not a channel enum. **Not defensible:** leaving a screen that reports `SENT` for messages no system ever sent. ⚠ Also not defensible: inventing an SMS gateway to make the page honest — that would be the larger error |
| **Cheap interim** | The same route can serve the **real inbox**, which has data. That is strictly more honest than today and does not pre-empt the owner's call on delivery |
| **Effort** | **S** to retire · **L** to build · the decision is the owner's |
| **Verification** | `grep -c 'apiFetch|fetch('` over `lib/notificationOutbox` returns **zero**; the route is in `App.tsx:860`; `platform.notifications` holds 445 rows the page never reads |
| **References** | [[F2-38]] · [[F2-137]] · [[F2-42]] · [[F2-139]] · `lib/notificationOutbox` |
| **What shipped** | **5 files / ~1,091 lines deleted** — `lib/notificationOutbox` (183), `lib/notificationDispatch` (341), `NotificationOutboxPage` (171), `NotificationDispatchPage` (234), `NotificationChannelsPage` (162). All three routes **redirect to the real inbox** through `RedirectPreservingQuery` (never raw `<Navigate>`, which drops [[F2-83]]'s `?org=`); the whole nav group is gone; **72 orphaned translation keys** pruned from `en`+`ar` with parity re-asserted at 5,825 each. `RiskLifecyclePage`'s CRO notify was **deleted rather than repointed** — three fabrications stacked, and F8 writes those rows server-side |
| **⚠ The scope was three times the filed row, and the worst instance was not the one filed** | `lib/notificationDispatch.ts` (341 lines) is an *“operator console for the notification dispatch runtime”* naming **real commercial providers** — `AWS SES (KSA region)`, `WhatsApp Business Cloud API`, `CITC-licensed Saudi SMS gateway` — with `dailyQuota: 50_000` and a `lastVerifiedAt` documented as *“when this channel was last verified by smoke-test”* that is `_hoursAgo(6)` **evaluated at module load**. The grid therefore always read “verified 6 hours ago”, about nothing. A named provider plus a freshness timestamp is a stronger claim than an outbox status |
| **🔑 The nav group duplicated a capability that already worked** | The real, server-backed inbox has had its own nav entry throughout. So retiring three screens cost **no capability at all** and the redirect target was already genuine — which is why option (a) was clearly right rather than merely cheaper |
| **Verification** | **Browser-verified**, not only unit-tested: all three routes land on `/notifications`; `GET /api/v1/notifications?page=0&size=50` and `/unread-count` both **200**; the page renders **75 total / 57 unread** real F8 escalations; **zero** fabricated links among 134 on the page; and `/notifications/outbox?org=2` → `/notifications?org=2`, so [[F2-83]]'s tenant parameter survives the redirect *in the browser* rather than only in an assertion. Suite **3,360/0/0** backend, **315/315 in 35/35 files** frontend (the file count matters — a partially-executed vitest run reports all-pass) |
| **➕ Two guards, mutation-proven 3/3** | (i) the five files must stay deleted; (ii) **no file may call `markSent`/`markFailed` against `localStorage`** — a **shape** check, because names are replaceable and this spec's own Lesson says the tell is that `markSent` exists. A rebuilt store under a new name is caught. `routeReachability` correctly demanded a reason for the now-unlinked stubs: they are recorded off-nav rather than re-linked, since re-linking is how the defect returns |
| **⚠ Deliberately not touched** | `lib/stakeholders.ts` (966 lines, five real consumers) and BCM crisis comms, which is **server-backed** — only its two KPI links pointed here and they are removed rather than repointed, because a cascade's recipient count does not live in a global inbox and *a link that goes somewhere wrong is worse than no link*. Measuring the crisis path produced **[[F2-147]]** |
| **🔑 The product's own readiness page already said so** | `prCkNotifDesc` read *“Today the runtime simulates delivery”* while three screens next door reported `SENT`. **An honest checklist item does not make a dishonest screen acceptable** — it means the defect was known and shipped. That item now describes reality and points at the server |
| **Lesson** | **A client-side store with a delivery lifecycle is not a mock, it is a false claim with a schema.** The tell is not the fake data — it is that `markSent` exists at all: nothing that actually sends a message needs the sender to mark it sent. ➕ And the corollary this closure added: **a fabricated capability is rarely one file.** The filed row named one page; the estate was three screens, two stores, one nav group and 72 strings, and the instance nobody had filed was the worst one |

## F2-57 — Two SKUs have boundaries that are not package boundaries, and the register could not say so

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-57 *(found ratifying the SKU catalogue, 2026-07-30; closed 2026-08-06)* |
| **Category** | Commercial register integrity — an instrument that cannot express a truth |
| **Severity** | **P3** — no runtime exposure (entitlement enforces correctly), but the document a reader consults to answer *"what does this tenant pay for"* was wrong about 73 files |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06** (no migration). `carve_outs` on the two affected registry entries, asserted against `SkuRegistry.CARVE_OUTS` in both directions and checked on disk |
| **⚠ Half the row was already true** | The *enforcement* half has been correct since **ADR-018 R6**: `SkuRegistry.CARVE_OUTS` maps `admin.committee → BOARD` and `iam.governance → ACCESS_GOV`, and is consulted **before** `PACKAGE_TO_SKU` precisely because the parent package is Base in both cases. ⚠ Which is why this row could not be closed by reading its own text — the fix it implies was already shipped, and the defect it names is one layer up |
| **The actual defect** | `theHomingAgreesWithSkuRegistryInBothDirections()` compares **top-level packages only**. `SkuRegistry.CARVE_OUTS` is consulted by **no test**. So a carve-out could be added or deleted in code and nothing would notice, while `module-registry.yaml` went on declaring `admin: homing: base` over **51 files** of paid `BOARD` content, and `ACCESS_GOV` — 22 files in `iam/governance` — appeared nowhere as a boundary at all |
| **The measurement that makes it commercial, not cosmetic** | `BOARD` = `board_pack` (**6 files**) **+** `admin/committee` (**51 files, inside a kernel package**). `ACCESS_GOV` = `iam/governance` (**22 files**) and **no top-level package**. **73 of the 79 files behind these two paid SKUs live under a package the register called Base** |
| **🔑 Why this is a class, not a one-off** | **A register that cannot express a truth reads as asserting its absence.** Third instance in two days: [[F2-140]] (live tables no entity maps, so a DDL ratchet could not see them), [[F2-141]] (Arabic columns no entity maps, so the bilingual ratchet's 157 was a lower bound), and this. In each case the instrument was *correct about its own population* and was read as a statement about the platform |
| **What shipped** | `carve_outs` on `admin` and `iam`, plus `everySkuCarveOutIsDeclaredInBothPlacesAndExistsOnDisk()`. It **asserts** against the code and never re-declares it: a registry that restates the homing becomes the second authority it exists to prevent, which is **RC-A**, the root cause this programme's queue is ordered by. ➕ Includes a **floor** — if `SkuRegistry.CARVE_OUTS` ever reads empty, the carve-outs did not become package-shaped, the constant was gutted |
| **➕ And it asks whether the thing exists** | A carve-out naming a sub-package that is not a directory is *a paid boundary declared over no code*, so both are checked against disk. That is the question four findings on this programme have turned on: **what does this compare, and does that data exist?** |
| **Verification** | Suite **3,347/0/0**; contract test 6 → 7 checks. **Mutation-proven 3/3.** ⚠ The third needed isolating: renaming the sub-package in the YAML alone trips the *agreement* assertion and **masks** the directory check, so both sides had to be mutated consistently. *Two assertions in one test let the first hide the second* — second time this session |
| **References** | `SkuRegistry.CARVE_OUTS` · `everySkuCarveOutIsDeclaredInBothPlacesAndExistsOnDisk()` · `module-registry.yaml` · ADR-018 R6 · [[F2-01]] · [[F2-140]] · [[F2-141]] |
| **Lesson** | **Ask what a register can express before trusting what it says.** This one was internally consistent, CI-enforced and green, and wrong about 73 files — because the question *"which package is this SKU"* has no answer for two of the fifteen, and a schema with one row per package cannot represent that. **Enforcement being right is not the same as the record being right, and only the record is what a customer or an auditor reads** |

## F2-144 — No employee could submit their own conflict-of-interest declaration, and the obvious fix would have opened impersonation

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-144 *(found 2026-08-06 by [[F2-52]]'s new role-existence gate; closed same day, both parts)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation — a guard reciting names that are not roles, over a write that trusted the request body |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a Nazaha-aligned COI attestation campaign ran with every declarant locked out, and the natural remedy would have allowed attesting in another person's name |
| **Current State** | ✔ **CLOSED 2026-08-06** — (a) declarant bound server-side, then (b) guard opened. No migration: the decision is that **no permission is created** |
| **The defect** | `CoiController.SUBMIT_GUARD` was `hasAnyRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN','ETHICS_OFFICER','BOARD_SECRETARY','COMPLIANCE_OFFICER','USER')` and **`ETHICS_OFFICER` and `USER` exist in no row of `iam.roles`**. Both legs were permanently false, so submission reduced to three administrators — while the class javadoc stated the opposite intent: *"any authenticated employee can submit their own attestation"* |
| **🔑 That capability had never once worked** | Not a regression. The names were wrong from the first commit, and nothing anywhere reports a guard leg that resolves to nothing: no compile error, no startup warning, no log line. The observable symptom is an empty campaign, which reads as *employees not complying* rather than *employees unable to comply* |
| **⚠ And the fix the gate invited was worse than the defect** | `submitDeclaration` took `userId`, `fullNameEn` and `fullNameAr` **from the request body** and stored them verbatim. Correcting the ghost names — or relaxing to `isAuthenticated()` as the javadoc implies — would have let **any authenticated user attest in a colleague's name**, the one outcome a conflict-of-interest register exists to prevent. **The dead legs were the only thing preventing an unbound write** |
| **Part (a) — bind the declarant** | `CoiService.declarantFor` now chooses: a holder of a COI **management authority** may still name any `userId` (filing on an employee's behalf is the only behaviour that ever worked here), everyone else declares for themselves and the body's `userId` is **ignored rather than rejected** — a rejection would disclose whether another id exists. **No principal at all is refused**: an attestation attributable to nobody is worse than a refused one |
| **Part (b) — open the guard, under a rule** | `SUBMIT_GUARD` is now `isAuthenticated()`, admitted to **ADR-010 §Sanctioned exceptions** (new) and recorded as **ADR-017 D-13**: *an endpoint discharging an obligation every employee is under is guarded by `isAuthenticated()`, not by a permission* — because **a grant list can only ever wrongly exclude someone**, and an employee who cannot file is a compliance failure that reads as the employee's fault. Three admission conditions, of which **server-bound subject is load-bearing**. Two members: knowledge-base reads ([[F2-53]] / D-7) and this |
| **⚠ The ghost names were NOT simply dropped** | `ETHICS_OFFICER` remains in `MANAGE_GUARD`. Removing it would silently ratify **compliance-officer ownership of ethics oversight** — [[F2-145]]'s reasoning on a second module — and no such decision has been made. So a one-line change removing two dead names moves [[F2-52]]'s ghost baseline by **one** (24 → 23; `USER` had exactly one occurrence in all of `src/main/java`), which is the clearest demonstration available that **the ghost count is a name count, not a defect count** |
| **➕ The durable half is a different test** | `ControllerAuthorizationRatchetTest` asserted `count <= 21` while its javadoc **enumerated** those 21 in three reasoned buckets — prose is not an assertion. A ceiling tolerates the population it measures: any member could have been swapped for a different unguarded endpoint and it would have read green. That is house rule 3's `BARE_EXPORT_CEILING` defect, **in the ratchet that house rule is about**. Granting this exception was the first request to *add* a floor member since the enumeration was written, so the floor is now a **pinned set asserted in both directions** — it fails when a member gets guarded and is not removed, and **22 is derived** from the reviewed set rather than declared beside it |
| **Verification** | **Live 9/9** through the real stack: `viewer` (role `VIEWER`, zero ethics grants) POSTs **201**; naming `userId=4` in the body still stores **6**, its own id; all four authority acts still **403** for the same caller, so the exception did not leak sideways; a manager naming `userId=4` stores **4**. Probe rows removed with `CHARINDEX`, residue re-checked at **0** in both tables. **Mutation-proven 4/4**, each in its own test method so no assertion masks another. Suite **3,360/0/0** |
| **⚠ It broke 5 existing tests, and that was evidence** | `CoiServiceTest`'s auto-flag tests submitted declarations with **no authenticated principal at all** and passed, because the service stored whatever the body said. The tests were documenting the defect |
| **References** | `CoiService.declarantFor` · `CoiDeclarantBindingTest` · `CoiSubmitGuardTest` · `ControllerAuthorizationRatchetTest.SELF_SCOPED` · ADR-010 §Sanctioned exceptions · ADR-017 D-13 · [[F2-52]] · [[F2-53]] · [[F2-145]] |
| **Lesson** | **Before applying a gate's prescribed remedy, check what the remedy does.** The role-existence gate said *these names are not roles*, which is true, and the implied fix — correct or drop them — would have opened an impersonation path. **The order was not optional: bind the subject, then open the guard.** Same standing lesson as the `@NotBlank` episode, and the reason "sweep the ghost names" is a per-site review rather than a rename pass |

## F2-147 — A crisis cascade reports N/N delivered over SMS, WhatsApp and Teams, and no gateway exists for any of them

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-147 *(found 2026-08-06 while retiring [[F2-142]]'s fake screens)* |
| **Category** | Fabricated delivery — but the **inverse** shape of F2-142 |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the cascade row is BCM evidence that people were notified, and during a real crisis an operator would believe it |
| **Current State** | ✔ **SLICE 1 SHIPPED 2026-08-06** (`285f1823`, **V363**) — a simulated dispatch is now visibly and durably simulated: declared on the SPI, persisted on the cascade row, named in the roll-up and the F8 payload, labelled in the wizard before *and* after the trigger, in both locales. ⬜ **Slice 2 (owner) still open**: commission the gateways — STC/Mobily for SMS, WhatsApp Business, SMTP. That is [[F2-142]]'s deferred half and the production-readiness item `prCkNotif` |
| 🔑 **And the finding is LATENT, not realised** | Measured before writing V363: `bcm.crisis_comms_cascade` holds **0 rows in dev and 0 in prod**. Nobody has yet been shown `12/12` for a message that was never sent — the subsystem has never been dispatched in either environment. It would have fired the first time somebody used it, which for a crisis-communications tool is **during a crisis**. That is also what made `NOT NULL DEFAULT (0)` safe: there is no historical row to mislabel as delivered. Had either table held rows, the correct column was NULLABLE with NULL meaning *“written before the flag existed”*, because defaulting an unknown to the reassuring value is the defect this finding is about |
| ⚠ **Two of this row's own claims were overstated, and the correction matters** | It said `DispatchResult` had *“no field for it”* and that there was *“no surface anywhere that could tell them otherwise”*. Both are false as written: `CascadeResponse.responsePayload` **did** carry the stub string, `CrisisCommsDispatchService` populated it, and the wizard **did** print it — in the result table's Details column. Three things were really wrong, and they are what shipped: **(1)** the fact lived only in free-text **English**, so it could not be filtered, totalled, or read by an Arabic-first operator — this platform's primary locale; **(2)** every aggregate an operator actually reads (the toast, the Success KPI, the status badge) was computed without consulting it; **(3)** it was **never persisted**, so the cascade row — the BCM evidence, read months later by someone who never saw the screen — could not express it at all. A response field cannot reach that reader, which is why slice 1 carried a migration the plan row said it would not need |
| **What shipped, mechanism by mechanism** | `ChannelSender.deliveryMode()` → `LIVE` or `SIMULATED`, **abstract with no default** so the next Sprint-12-style stub cannot inherit an honest answer it has not earned; `SendResult` gains `simulated` + a `simulated(…)` factory; the three stubs and EMAIL's no-mail-bean branch use it. `EmailChannelSender` **derives** its mode from the bean, so configuring `spring.mail.host` flips it to LIVE with no code change. **V363** persists `simulated` on the cascade. **`GET /api/v1/bcm/comms/v2/channels`** reports per-channel readiness under `BCM:READ` — the surface that lets an operator find out *before* a crisis, and `isHealthy()`'s **first caller ever**. The wizard gains readiness chips + a pre-send warning on the channel picker, a warning-variant toast, a `مُسجَّل (محاكاة)` KPI in place of `نجح`, and a `COMPLETED · محاكاة` badge with the green tick removed |
| 🔑 **`listCascades` had zero callers, so a cascade could only ever be seen once** | The server has served `GET /cascades?crisisEventId=N` since Slice 9-E and **nothing read it** — it sat in `apiClientReachability`'s `KNOWN_UNREACHED`. The only view of a cascade was the wizard's transient result step, which *“Send another”* discards (`setResult(null)`). So the persisted BCM evidence had no reader at all. Now wired onto step 1: what has already gone out for this event is what an operator needs before deciding to send again |
| **A dangling drill-through found in passing** | The result panel's `Channels` KPI still carried `to="/notifications/channels"` — an [[F2-142]] redirect stub that lands on the notification inbox, where a cascade's channel count does not live. The comment two lines below it already explained why its **two neighbours** lost their links (F2-38's *“a link that goes somewhere wrong is worse than no link”*); the comment covered three KPIs and the fix had covered two. Removed. ⚠ And doing so **falsified a claim in F2-142's own note** in `routeReachability.test.ts`, which said `/notifications/channels` was unlisted *“only because nothing ever linked it either”*. That KPI was the one inbound link; removing it made the route unreachable and the guard fired on the next full run. **A negative claim in a comment is not a measurement — the guard beside it was** |
| ⚠ **Two of my own instruments were wrong first, both failing in the reassuring direction** | **(a)** The LIVE-detection scan matched any `deliveryMode(){…DeliveryMode.LIVE}` and flagged `EmailChannelSender`, whose body is a ternary on the mail bean — the honest shape. The comment beside the regex *said* a ternary was fine; the regex did not implement the comment. An offence is a body that names LIVE and never names SIMULATED, which is what “unconditionally” means in source. **(b)** The frontend tests queried English only and **all four render legs failed** on `Unable to find … /Next/i`: `I18nProvider` defaults to **Arabic**, so the wizard under test was rendering Arabic throughout, and the single query that did pass was a hard-coded placeholder identical in both languages. An English-only test of this screen asserts nothing about what a Saudi crisis operator sees. Every assertion now runs in **both** locales |
| ⚠ **And one claim I nearly made and measured instead** | The sender scan strips comments, and I was about to record that as load-bearing. Measured: replacing the stripper with the identity function leaves **all six tests green**. It stays for the direction it protects — the tells only decide *which* files get checked, so a future sender whose javadoc says “we would dispatch via Twilio here” while genuinely sending live would be pulled in and flagged for a plain `LIVE`: a false accusation, not a miss. The javadoc now says exactly that |
| **⚠ This subsystem is real, which is what makes it different** | `CrisisCommsDispatchWizard` calls `api.post /api/v1/bcm/comms/v2/dispatch`; `CrisisCommsDispatchController` exists; a genuine cascade row is written against the crisis event. Nothing here is a localStorage fake. **It was checked precisely because [[F2-142]]'s two KPI links pointed at the fabricated outbox, and the wizard could easily have been dismissed along with them** |
| **The defect** | The senders return success unconditionally. `SmsChannelSender.send` logs `[sms-stub] would dispatch` and returns `SendResult.ok(n, "stub-sms: … integrate STC/Mobily HTTP gateway in Sprint 12")`; `TeamsChannelSender` and `WhatsAppChannelSender` are identical; `isHealthy()` is a literal `return true` commented *"stub always healthy"*. `EmailChannelSender` is the one with a real `JavaMailSender` path — but with no mail bean it takes the same log-and-succeed branch |
| **🔑 The server is honest and the DTO drops it** | Every stub puts its status in `SendResult`'s detail string. `DispatchResult` carries only `totalRecipients / totalSuccess / totalFailure / elapsedMillis` — **no field for it**. So the operator sees `12/12` and a *"تم الإرسال"* toast, and **there is no surface anywhere that could tell them otherwise.** The truth exists, is written down, and is discarded one layer before the only person who needs it |
| **⚠ The trade-off was made deliberately, and is documented** | `EmailChannelSender`'s comment states it: *"dev-mode log-and-succeed so the cascade row is still recorded against the crisis event (audit trail integrity wins over realism in environments where SMTP is not configured)"*. That reasoning is defensible for the **row**; it does not license reporting **success**. The fix is not to stop writing the row — it is to mark it simulated |
| **Recommendation** | **Slice 1 (S, no migration):** carry the per-channel detail — or a `simulated: true` — into `DispatchResult` and `CascadeResponse`, surface it in the wizard's result step and the cascade timeline, and pin it with a test that a stub sender's result **cannot render as an unqualified success**. Real gateways then land against a UI that already distinguishes them. **Slice 2 (owner):** commission the gateways — STC/Mobily SMS, WhatsApp Business, SMTP — which is [[F2-142]]'s deferred half and the `prCkNotif` readiness item |
| **Verification (slice 1)** | Backend **3,379/0/0** · frontend **325/325 in 36/36 files** (the file count matters: a partially-executed vitest run reports all-pass) · `tsc` clean · **9 of 9 mutations caught**, including the two that survived the first pass — the badge *tone* and an unconditional `toast.success`, both closed with real DOM assertions rather than a source scan. ⚠ The tone mutation “survived” for a third reason worth recording: the anchor `tone={c.simulated ? …}` appears **twice** (result table and history table) and a single-occurrence replace hit whichever came first in the file, so the mutation never touched the code the test covered. Both are now asserted. **V363's guard mutation-tested against the database**: column dropped, a row inserted, migration re-run → refused with its own message; row deleted, re-run → applied cleanly. **Live 13/13** through the real stack: readiness reports four SIMULATED + `VOICE_BLAST` UNREGISTERED, `ranalyst` **403**, dispatch flags all three channels, the **persisted** rows carry `simulated=1`, the timeline endpoint serves it back. **Browser-verified in Arabic through the Vite proxy end to end** — chips, pre-send warning, banner, KPI, badges and the history table. Residue **0**, cleaned with `CHARINDEX` |
| **Verification (original, as filed)** | Read from source, not inferred: `SendResult.ok(n, "stub-sms: …")` in `SmsChannelSender:31`; `isHealthy()` → `return true` with the comment; `DispatchResult` in `lib/crisisComms.ts:111` has four numeric fields and no detail; the wizard's toast is `تم الإرسال — ${out.totalSuccess}/${out.totalRecipients}` |
| **References** | `CrisisCommsDispatchService` · `SmsChannelSender` / `WhatsAppChannelSender` / `TeamsChannelSender` / `EmailChannelSender` · `CrisisCommsDispatchWizard` · `V363__crisis_cascade_simulated_flag.sql` · `CrisisCommsHonestDeliveryTest` · `crisisCascadeHonesty.test.tsx` · `routeReachability.test.ts` · `apiClientReachability.test.ts` · commit `285f1823` · [[F2-142]] · [[F2-139]] · [[F2-42]] · [[F2-68]] |
| **Lesson** | **The inverse of [[F2-142]] is just as dangerous and much harder to see.** There, a screen lied and there was no server. Here the server tells the truth in a string, and the DTO has nowhere to put it — so a truthful backend and a truthful comment produce a false screen anyway. ⚠ **A stub is only honest if its honesty reaches the user**; a log line the operator will never read is not a disclosure. Ask of every stub: *who sees that it is a stub?* |

## F2-143 — A migration-built database is 36 permissions and 4 roles short, and one is the tenant administrator

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-143 *(measured 2026-08-06, the moment [[F2-77]]'s chain first reached V320)* |
| **Category** | Deployment integrity — the authorisation catalogue a fresh installation gets is not the one the code enforces |
| **Severity** | **P1** — a customer provisioned from the folder cannot be given the primary tenant-administrator role, and 36 permissions guard endpoints only `PLATFORM_ADMIN` can reach, through a code bypass, fail-closed and silent |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **OPEN.** Filed, not fixed: the fix is a generated migration and belongs in its own slice |
| **The measurement** | Chain-built vs live, same instance: **79 of 115 permissions** (**36 missing, 31%**) across **14 modules** — `ADMIN`, `BCM`, `COMPLIANCE`, `CYBER`, `PRIVACY`, `RISK`, `TPRM`, `SCM`, `BOARD`, `KB`, `FRAUD`, `INTEGRATIONS`, `AUTOMATION`, `REPORTING` — and **54 of 58 roles**, missing `ORG_ADMIN`, `VIEWER`, `RISK_ANALYST`, `FRAUD_INVESTIGATOR` |
| **🔑 Why it is P1 and not paperwork** | `ORG_ADMIN` is the primary tenant administrator. **[[F2-81]]'s tenant onboarding, closed earlier the same day, provisions a first administrator into a role that does not exist in a fresh build.** The endpoint is correct; the database it would run against is not. Every one of the 36 permissions guards live endpoints, and a missing catalogue row means the guard can never be satisfied by a grant — only by `ModulePermissionEvaluator`'s hardcoded `PLATFORM_ADMIN` path |
| **V321's THROW was right** | The chain stops at `V321__reporting_write_grant.sql` with `50321: REPORTING:WRITE is still held by no role`. Its grant JOINs `iam.role_permissions` against `iam.permissions`, and a chain build holds only `REPORTING:DELETE` — `READ`, `WRITE` and `EXPORT` are absent — so the JOIN matches nothing. **A guard written months ago caught exactly what it was written to catch, on a database nobody had ever built.** It is not an obstacle to route around |
| **⚠ Why nothing detected this** | The catalogue is seeded by the Python bootstrap and by migrations a baselined production never re-ran. So the two authorities anyone would compare — the code's `@PreAuthorize` set and the live catalogue — **agreed**, and the drifting copy was the folder nobody executed. Identical shape to the V40 committee vocabulary closed hours earlier: **when two authorities agree, suspect a third** |
| **Recommendation** | A catalogue-completion migration **generated from the code's own `@PreAuthorize` set**, plus rows for the 4 missing roles. ⚠ Not hand-typed, and **not copied from the live database** — that is a third authority of unknown provenance, and this register's standing rule is that a fabricated row reads as data. `PermissionCatalogueUsageTest` already computes the code's set; generating from it makes the migration checkable against the same source that enforces it |
| **Effort** | **M** — and it unblocks V321—V335's guards in one step, which is most of the chain's remaining distance |
| **Verification** | Reproducible: run `Appendix/scripts/chain_proof.py --keep`, then diff `iam.permissions` and `iam.roles` between `TSS_GRC_ChainProof` and `TSS_GRC` |
| **References** | [[F2-77]] · [[F2-09]] · [[F2-81]] · `PermissionCatalogueUsageTest` · V321 |
| **Lesson** | **This is what F2-77 was for.** The chain was never the deliverable — it is the instrument, and the first thing it produced on reaching V320 is a P1 nobody could have measured while the folder went unexecuted. **A migration set that has never been run is not a deployment mechanism, it is a description of one** |

## F2-145 — The Sharia module has no working Sharia role, and compliance holds it instead

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-145 *(found 2026-08-06 working F2-52's ghost sweep with reflection instead of grep)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation — a governance separation that exists in the guard text and nowhere else |
| **Severity** | **P2** — no data is exposed to an outsider, but Sharia oversight is not independent of compliance, and for a Saudi Islamic-finance module that independence *is* the control |
| **Current State** | ✔ **ACCESS HALF CLOSED 2026-08-06 (V362, ADR-017 D-14)** · ✔ **`sharia` FULLY CLOSED 2026-08-07 — and with it [[F2-120]]** · ✔ **`openbanking` FULLY CLOSED 2026-08-07 (V368) — and with it [[F2-127]]** · 🔶 **independence half: (b) shipped (V367 + V368), (a) measured and blocked, (c)/(d) blocked** · ⬜ **(e) 39 of 102 sites converted; `cpty`/`aml` now known to need catalogue decisions before conversion** |
| **✔ What closed the Sharia case, 2026-08-07 — F2-145(e) slice 1** | All **21** `ShariaController` guards now read `hasPermission('SHARIA', READ│WRITE│APPROVE)`. 🔑 **The conversion is what made the two preceding migrations decide anything.** V362 catalogued the permissions and V367 revoked the auditor's APPROVE, and *both were inert* while the guards compared role names — `hasAnyRole` never reaches `ModulePermissionEvaluator`. Three artefacts had to land in order and only the third is observable: catalogue → revoke → convert. **Live 15/15, five personas × three tiers**: a seated `SHARIA_AUDITOR` writes **200** and approves **403** (V367 deciding a request for the first time), `INTERNAL_AUDITOR` reads 200 / writes 403, `viewer` 403 throughout, `compliance` 200/200/201, and `admin` **201 on approve while no longer being named** — the evaluator's in-code bypass, which was the one regression this conversion could plausibly have caused and the reason it was probed first |
| **🔑 Population-preserving, and that was measured before the edit rather than argued after it** | Per action, the guard's surviving names and the catalogued grants were already the **same set**, because V362 derived the catalogue *from these guards*: READ named 7 (1 ghost `CRO`), WRITE 4 (0 ghosts), APPROVE 5 (2 ghosts `CRO` + `SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR`) — **LOST none, GAINED none** on all three. ADR-010's amendment demands the *intended* population, not the incidental one; here they coincide, which is the only reason this shipped as a single slice. `PLATFORM_ADMIN` disappears from the expressions without widening or narrowing anything |
| **🔑 A third way to retire a ghost role, cheaper than either of D-14's** | `SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR` left the ghost population **without being seated and without being adjudicated**: converting the guard deletes every name it recited. Its 4 sites were all `SHARIA` APPROVE in one controller, and it now appears nowhere in `src/main`. The ghost baseline falls **9 → 8** and pure role sites **532 → 511** by one edit. This is why the other 81 sites are queued behind conversion rather than each getting a seating decision — and it means the ghost count falls for a *fourth* distinguishable reason, which the ratchet's javadoc now records |
| **⚠ A test that pinned this defect had to be rewritten, and it survived only because its author wrote the exit condition into the failure message** | `ShariaLifecycleTest.theGuardConstantsArePinnedWithTheirDeadRoles` asserted the four fictional names were **present**, carrying the note *"when they are created or the guards convert to hasPermission under F2-05, close F2-120 and update this"*. Without that sentence it would have read as a requirement that the ghosts stay — [[F2-115]]'s shape, a floor written over a defective control pinning the defect as correct. ➕ **And the mechanical reason it failed legibly**: its extractor asserted `m.find()` with *"a pattern that matches nothing passes forever"*, so a stale regex announced itself by name instead of throwing inside a helper |
| **⚠ Two published baselines were wrong within minutes of the edit, and a test caught both** | `MeasuredBaselineFreshnessTest` failed on *packages with zero `hasPermission`* (17 → **16**) and *distinct permission modules used by guards* (31 → **32**). Neither is a defect in the conversion; both are the measurement contract working. Updated in `MEASURED_BASELINE.md` §0 and §4 **and** in `baseline_census.py`'s `PUBLISHED` dict, because a doc and a generator that disagree is [[F2-19b]]'s failure mode |
| ✔ **What closed the Open Banking case, 2026-08-07 — F2-145(e) slice 2** | All **18** `ObController` guards now read `hasPermission('OPENBANKING', READ│WRITE│APPROVE)`, and **this one needed a migration first**. Live **15/15, five personas × three tiers**: a purpose-seated `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER` writes **200** and approves **403**, `INTERNAL_AUDITOR` reads 200 / writes 403, `viewer` 403 throughout, `compliance` 200/200/201, `admin` **201 on approve while named on no guard and holding no grant** — the in-code bypass, probed first as the one plausible regression. Pure role sites **511 → 493**; distinct role names recited **50 → 49**, because `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER` was recited in this controller and nowhere else, so its authority now flows only through the grant — the end state, not a loss |
| 🔑 **THE FINDING OF THIS SLICE: V362's catalogue is *derived* from the guards but not *faithful* to them** | Converting `openbanking` would have **widened** the APPROVE tier: the catalogue granted `OPENBANKING:APPROVE` to `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER`, a role **no APPROVE guard there has ever named**, which would have handed the module's operator `createTpp`, `transitionTpp` (KYB approve / suspend / revoke) and `notifySama` — so the officer who grants customer consents to a TPP would also admit that TPP. The provenance is mechanical and it generalises: V362's own header says the action came from the endpoint's **HTTP verb**, *“or APPROVE when the method is approve/reject/transition/decide/sign-shaped”*. That classifier reads the **method**; a guard's **tier** is a design decision it never read. `ObController` puts two transition-shaped methods (`transitionIncident`, `revokeConsent`) on WRITE, so V362 promoted that whole tier to APPROVE. **⚙ Measured across all six V362 modules — 4 of 6 would change population on conversion:** `SHARIA_AUDITOR`’s APPROVE (caught, V367) · `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER`’s APPROVE (**V368**, this slice) · `CFO`’s `CPTY:WRITE` · `KYC_OFFICER` + `ONBOARDING_AGENT`’s `AML:WRITE`. Only sharia’s instance had been noticed, and it had been read as a one-off SoD slip rather than as the classifier’s shape. **“Derived from the guards” is the word that made it feel already-checked** |
| **V368, and what it deliberately does not fix** | Revokes `OPENBANKING:APPROVE` from `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER` and **corrects two permission descriptions that were the classifier's belief written down** — WRITE claimed *“Register TPPs”* (that is APPROVE) and APPROVE claimed *“Transition Open Banking incidents and consent decisions”* (those are WRITE). A permission description that contradicts its guard is a comment that lies about code, and this one had already misled a grant. **Zero impact, measured**: the role is seated but held by **0 active users** in dev, and prod has not applied V362 yet. Afterwards `OPENBANKING:APPROVE` is reachable by `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER` alone — one approver, not zero, so this is separation and not the lockout shape [[F2-92]] and S6b-ii rejected. ⚠ **Not fixed here, and a revoke could not fix it**: nothing enforces maker ≠ checker on the APPROVE tier — `ObService.transitionTpp` stamps an actor and never compares it to the creator, so `compliance` can register a TPP and approve its own KYB. That needs a server-side 409 like the platform's other four-eyes gates |
| 🔁 **The same test shape failed the same way, one module over — and again the exit condition saved it** | `ObLifecycleTest.theGuardConstantsArePinnedWithTheirDeadRoles` pinned `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER` and `CRO` as **present**, carrying *“when these are created, or the guards convert to hasPermission under F2-05, close the finding and update this”*. Second occurrence in two slices, same rescue: the failure read as *the fix landed, update me* rather than *the conversion broke something*. ➕ And again the mechanical half — its extractor asserted `m.find()` with *“a pattern that matches nothing passes forever”*, so a stale regex announced itself by name. **Both halves of that discipline are now proven across two independent modules**, which is the argument for putting them in every defect pin rather than in the ones I happen to remember |
| **Mutation-tested, 5/5 killed** | The new `ObControllerGuardTest` was attacked before being trusted: demote `notifySama` to WRITE → killed by the exhaustive tier map · point READ at an uncatalogued `EXPORT` → killed by the catalogue assertion · revert WRITE to a role list → killed by the role-name assertion · strip `dashboard`'s guard → killed by the map's size floor · **delete V368's `DELETE` while keeping its header → killed**, which is the one that matters, because a migration that only *describes* a revocation leaves the APPROVE tier wide and nothing else in the hermetic suite can reach a database to notice. ⚠ Two harness lessons, both from mutations that matched **0** places and said so instead of reporting a false survival: `mvn` is not an executable on Windows (`CreateProcess` needs `mvn.cmd`), and **`ObController.java` is CRLF** — 227 CRLF, 0 bare LF — so every multi-line anchor written with `\n` matched nothing. Same family as the MEMORY.md orphan: a script assuming where a line ends, about a file it never checked |
| **The rest of the queue is no longer “63 more of the same”** | Measuring all six modules turned the remaining work from a count into four different problems. `consumer` **9** — cleanest: one flat name-set on all 9 endpoints regardless of verb, and the catalogue grants exactly it, so it converts with zero widening at any tiering (and tiering by verb is a strict improvement, since writes stop being reachable by read-only holders). `cpty` **14** — **`CPTY:APPROVE` is not catalogued at all**, so its APPROVE tier (`PLATFORM_ADMIN`, `CRO` ghost, `CFO`) has nothing to convert to; and `CFO` holds a mis-derived `CPTY:WRITE` the guards never gave it. `aml` **14** — **6 distinct guard name-sets** over 14 sites (`refreshList` narrower than `createList`; `performScreening` admits onboarding agents), so one `AML:WRITE` cannot express them and converting as-is would let a KYC officer create sanctions lists and review hits. `ethics` **26** — part-converted; its `TRIAGE` tier has no `TRIAGE` permission catalogued. **Three of the four need a catalogue decision, not a conversion**, and none of that was visible from the site count |
| **What shipped, and the mechanism matters** | **V362** seats the **9** domain roles that were job families nothing recorded, catalogues **17** permissions across the six modules and writes **57** grants derived from the guards' own HTTP verbs. 🔑 **No code change was needed**: `hasAnyRole` compares role NAMES, so the guards that had always recited `SHARIA_OFFICER` began matching the moment the row existed — **live-proven 403 → 200** on `/api/v1/sharia/scholars` with `ShariaController` untouched, plus **201** on its write guard, and still **403** on a licensed-but-ungranted module so the seating is scoped |
| **🔑 Two of the eleven ghosts already had their answer in the database** | `MODEL_OWNER` is **not** seated: `ai_governance.ml_model.model_owner_user_id` is populated on **25 of 25** rows and resolves to real users, so a role would be **looser than the column** — the owner of model A could act on model B. `SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR` is **not** seated: `sharia.sharia_scholar` already holds the board — 5 rows, `ssb_role` ∈ {CHAIRMAN, MEMBER×3, SECRETARY}, terms, credentials, COI declarations — and a chair is a term-limited **seat** held by someone who may be external. Both stay declared gaps rather than fabricated roles. *Answering this row's (a)-or-(b) uniformly would have created a role for a fact the schema already records* |
| **⚠ Corrections to this spec, from re-measuring rather than re-reading** | **`ai` does not belong in the seven** — it is the only one with a catalogued permission module and its surviving population (RISK_MANAGER 23, CISO 20, DPO 19, AUDITOR 14) is a defensible AI-governance population; by this spec's own criterion (*“usually COMPLIANCE_OFFICER”*, here 5 of 26 sites) it is excluded. **Six modules.** `ETHICS_OFFICER` is **22** sites, not 23 — D-13 removed one. And `consumer` is the severe case, not `sharia`: **PLATFORM_ADMIN and COMPLIANCE_OFFICER and nobody else on all 9 guards** |
| **⚠ The grant derivation caught itself being wrong** | Classifying the action by METHOD NAME made `WhistleblowerCaseController#updates` — a `@GetMapping` whose name is the plural noun *updates* — a write, and would have granted `ETHICS:WRITE` to `INTERNAL_AUDITOR` on the strength of a regex. Reclassified by **HTTP verb**, then `review` was restored to the APPROVE shapes because `CoiController#reviewDeclaration` is a POST deciding somebody else's declaration and its omission left `ethics` with no APPROVE at all. The two matrices were **diffed**: exactly 3 cells moved, all three that endpoint's grantees |
| **⚠ Why this is not closed, stated with the number that proves it** | Inside `SHARIA`, **`COMPLIANCE_OFFICER`, `SHARIA_OFFICER` and `SHARIA_AUDITOR` all hold READ+WRITE+APPROVE** — three roles, one privilege set, which is **not** a separation of duties; the same in `AML` and `CONSUMER`. So the *access* claim is answered and the *independence* claim — the one this spec calls the control — is not. Narrowing compliance out of Sharia approval removes access somebody may be using today, which ADR-010's Consequences gate behind a per-tenant impact query. **Left visible rather than smuggled into a migration**, and the identical-grant check is computed by the generator, not asserted by me |
| **➕ It also amended another ADR** | ADR-010's *“conversion is behaviour-preserving”* rule is **unsafe for exactly this set**: granting `CONSUMER:*` to the roles that could ever match would encode compliance's monopoly in the grant table — turning a dead guard leg, which is at least evidence of unmet intent, into a decision an auditor reads. The rule now requires resolving the ghost **first** |
| **The measurement** | Every guard on `ShariaController` reads `PLATFORM_ADMIN │ COMPLIANCE_OFFICER │ … │ SHARIA_OFFICER │ SHARIA_AUDITOR`, plus `SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR` on the scholar path. **All three exist in no row of `iam.roles`** — and neither does `CRO`, which the read guards also recite. So the effective population for the whole module is `PLATFORM_ADMIN`, `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER`, `RISK_MANAGER`, `INTERNAL_AUDITOR` |
| **🔑 Why that is a governance defect and not a typo** | **A Sharia scholar, auditor or board chair cannot reach the Sharia module at all, and a compliance officer can do everything in it.** A Sharia board exists to be independent of the compliance function; here the guard *says* that and the database does not deliver it. The endpoints work, the register fills, and the separation is absent |
| **⚠ Why the ghost legs must NOT be dropped** | The reflex from the rest of this sweep is wrong here. For `CISO_GOVERNANCE` — one dead leg among eleven real roles — dropping it loses nothing and was done. For Sharia the ghosts are **the only surviving record of intended access**: delete them and the guard reads as a deliberate decision to hand Sharia oversight to compliance, which nobody made. *Removing evidence of unmet intent is not the same as meeting it* |
| **How it was found** | Reading `target/role-guard-sites.txt`, the reflection dump `RoleGuardConversionRatchetTest` writes. ⚠ A `grep` over `hasAnyRole(...)` for the same names returned **one** guard, because these span source lines — the trap that test's own javadoc records (F2-13a: **3 by grep, 73 by reflection**) and which I reached for first anyway |
| **Recommendation** | **(a)** catalogue and seat the three Sharia bodies as roles, making the guards work as written; or **(b)** convert the module to `SHARIA:READ/WRITE/APPROVE` per F2-05's standing direction and grant to whichever real roles a tenant's Sharia governance uses. **(b)** is the platform's stated direction and survives a tenant whose scholars are external — and [[F2-26]] already models governance **seats** separately from roles, which may be where a board chair belongs |
| **Effort** | **M** — and it is a decision first |
| **References** | [[F2-52]] · [[F2-05]] · [[F2-26]] · [[F2-120]] · [[F2-115]] · `ShariaController` · `ShariaControllerGuardTest` · `ShariaLifecycleTest.theGuardConstantsAreConvertedToPermissions` · `V362` · `V367` · `MeasuredBaselineFreshnessTest` · `target/role-guard-sites.txt` |
| **⚠ Sharia is not special — it is the clearest case of a pattern** | Grouping all ghost names by module (24 at the time of measurement, **23 after F2-144(b) retired `USER`** — the seven modules below are unaffected, because `USER` was nobody's domain role) shows the same shape in **seven modules**, every one of them a Saudi or financial-sector module whose domain role exists only in guard text: `sharia` — `SHARIA_OFFICER` 21 sites, `SHARIA_AUDITOR` 17, `SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR` 4 · `ethics` — `ETHICS_OFFICER` 23 · `openbanking` — `OPEN_BANKING_OFFICER` 15 · `ai` — `MODEL_OWNER` 14 · `aml` — `AML_OFFICER` 14, `KYC_OFFICER` 6, `ONBOARDING_AGENT` 5 · `cpty` — `CREDIT_OFFICER` 12 · `consumer` — `CONSUMER_PROTECTION_OFFICER` 9. **In each, the module's only domain-specific role is a ghost, so the module belongs to whoever else the guard lists — usually `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER`.** F2-144 already recorded the ethics case from the other direction: an ethics officer cannot manage the COI register they own |
| **What the pattern is not** | ⚠ Three modules look similar and are **fine**: `privacy` (`PRIVACY_OFFICER` is a ghost but `PRIVACY_MANAGER` **and** `DPO` are real), `bcm` (`FACILITATOR`, `INCIDENT_COMMANDER`, `CRISIS_COMMANDER` are ghosts but `BCM_MANAGER`, `CRISIS_MANAGER`, `EMERGENCY_RESPONDER` are real), and `cyber` (F2-52 cleared `SOC_LEAD` because `CISO` sits in the same guards). There the ghost is a synonym for a role that exists, and dropping it is the right move — which is what makes the seven above different and worth a decision rather than a sweep |
| **Separately — a different mechanism, and ⚠ I first overstated it** | The `dashboard` module contributes **seven** names that are not roles: `CYBER_READ`, `INCIDENT_READ`, `COMPLIANCE_READ`, `RISK_READ`, `SCM_READ`, `BCM_READ`, `PRIVACY_READ`, in two `hasAnyAuthority(...)` guards on `DashboardController`. **Correction (same day):** I recorded these as sites with "no real non-admin role beside them", candidates for being reachable by nobody. **That is wrong.** `ModulePermissionEvaluator` documents granted authorities as `MODULE_ACTION` and matches that exact string at line 42, so `RISK_READ` *is* a real granted authority and these guards work for every user holding the permission — the normal population. What they lose is the `ROLE_PLATFORM_ADMIN` bypass the evaluator applies first, so the failure is **too tight for admins only**, exactly the direction F2-53 predicted. They are worth rewriting as `hasPermission(...)` for consistency and to regain the bypass; they are **not** broken authorisation, and my ghost-name gate counting them as absent roles is a labelling artefact, not seven defects |
| **Lesson** | **A guard that names a role nobody created is a design document, not a control.** It reads as separation of duties, passes review, and delegates the module to whoever else is on the list. The ghost-name gate found it in a week; the guard had been asserting that separation for months. 🔑 And the generalisation is the real finding: **the platform's Saudi and financial-sector modules were built with their domain roles written into guards and never into `iam.roles`** — so exactly the modules a regulator would ask about are the ones where the named specialist has no access |

## F2-146 — A fresh install cannot read compliance findings: 13 mapped columns no migration creates

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-146 *(found 2026-08-06 by F2-09's schema-convergence diff, first run)* |
| **Category** | Deployment correctness — the dev schema and a migration-built schema are not the same database |
| **Severity** | **P1** — not a cosmetic gap: on a fresh install, ordinary reads of the compliance findings register fail at the SQL layer |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **OPEN** |
| **The measurement** | `schema_convergence.py --stop 330` diffs a Flyway-built database against the Python-bootstrapped dev schema and partitions every difference by which migration would create it. Of 134 raw differences: **77 tables + 27 columns** come from migrations above the chain's stopping point (unrun, **not defects**), **0** are guard-skipped, and the real residue is **3 tables + 26 columns that no migration anywhere creates** |
| **🔑 Why it is P1 and not bookkeeping** | Thirteen of those 26 columns are on `compliance.findings`, and `AuditFinding` **maps them with `@Column`** — `capa_stage` at [AuditFinding.java:50](tss-grc-backend/src/main/java/com/tss/grc/module/compliance/entity/AuditFinding.java:50), `owner_test_result` at [:56](tss-grc-backend/src/main/java/com/tss/grc/module/compliance/entity/AuditFinding.java:56), plus `audit_signoff_at/by_id/notes`, `clause_ref`, `independent_test_at/by_id/notes/result`, `owner_test_at/evidence_id/notes`. With `ddl-auto=none` Hibernate creates nothing, so **on a migration-built install every SELECT through this entity names columns that do not exist** and fails with an invalid-column error. The whole audit-signoff / owner-test / independent-test workflow is dev-only |
| **The rest of the residue** | 3 tables — `compliance.compliance_assessments`, `compliance.compliance_findings`, `compliance.evidence_items` — which are **exactly** [[F2-140]]'s three, confirming that finding was precise rather than a sample. Remaining columns: `cyber.cyber_sub_controls.description_ar` (a bilingual column, so also [[F2-141]]'s shape) and four on `evidence.evidence_items` (`collected_at`, `collection_method`, `collector_id`, `collector_name`) |
| **One separate case** | `bcm.crisis_events.cycle_id` is named by **V99**, at or below the stop, and is absent from the built database — so either an idempotency guard skipped the `ALTER`, or V99's `CREATE TABLE` and the bootstrap disagree about the column set. Only one such case exists in 330 migrations, which is a good result for the guards and a reason to look at this one closely |
| **⚠ What this finding is not** | It is **not** "the migration set is 134 objects behind". I said that before partitioning and it was wrong — see the correction in `163fd21c`. The migrations are nearly complete; they simply cannot *run* past V331. **The gap that survives partitioning is 3 tables and 27 columns, and only that residue is a defect** |
| **Recommendation** | Write the missing DDL as a migration: 13 columns on `compliance.findings`, `description_ar` on `cyber.cyber_sub_controls`, the four `evidence.evidence_items` columns, and V99's `cycle_id`; the 3 tables wait on [[F2-140]]'s drop-or-keep decision. ➕ Then make this permanent — F2-09's purpose was always a **CI gate**, and the residue count is exactly the ratchet: 3 tables / 27 columns today, and it may only fall |
| **Effort** | **M** — the DDL is mechanical; the value is the gate that stops it recurring |
| **References** | `Appendix/scripts/schema_convergence.py` · `AuditFinding.java` · [[F2-140]] · [[F2-141]] · [[F2-143]] · [[F2-09]] |
| **Lesson** | **Every finding in this programme was verified against a database no customer will ever have.** Dev is Python-bootstrapped, a customer's is Flyway-built, and until this diff existed nothing compared them — so "verified in dev" carried an unexamined assumption for the whole engagement. F2-143 found the same thing in the grant tables; this found it in the schema. The instrument mattered more than any single defect it found |

## Sequencing — what to do, in what order

Dependency-ordered, not severity-ordered. Severity says what matters; this says what is *possible*.

| Wave | Items | Why this order |
|---|---|---|
| **First (this week, ahead of everything)** | **F2-21 vote provenance** · F2-22 audit script + `@JsonIgnore` mitigation | F2-21 invalidates the platform's central control claim and takes ~2 days. Nothing else in this register earns more per hour. F2-22 rides alongside because it is the same question — *who is allowed to set this field?* |
| **Now (days)** | F2-10 restart · F2-02 three files · F2-07 reconciliation matrix · F2-08a export-toolkit promotion · F2-08b boundary ratchet · F2-19 CI-generated baseline · F2-20 delete | Highest value per hour. Each is small, independent, and needs no decision. |
| **Wave 1 (weeks)** | F2-01 module registry → then F2-03 test floors, F2-05 permission conversion, F2-06 enforcement test | The registry is the spine: floors, permissions, schemas and navigation all attach to it. Nothing durable is buildable before it. |
| **Wave 2** | F2-04 entitlement **rollout** (**gated on the per-module SKU decision**) · F2-09 schema convergence · F2-13 grant tightness · F2-14 control-health SPI | Wave 2 is where the modular business model becomes real — and where the platform's assurance story becomes saleable. F2-04 is a rollout of an existing subsystem, so it is days rather than weeks. |
| **Wave 3** | F2-07 workspace IA · F2-11 i18n convergence · F2-12 `ndi` · F2-16 monoliths · F2-15 remaining mocks · F2-17 performance · F2-18 integration tests | Larger, benefits from Waves 1–2 being in place (IA needs entitlement; `ndi` needs test floors). |

**The one decision that must be made before Wave 2:** is the commercial model **per-module SKUs** or a
single suite? F2-04, F2-05 and F2-06 are P0/P1 under the first reading and P2 hygiene under the second.
This is Baseline §9 question 2, and it is a business decision — it is not ours to assume.
